## Material Changes Summary (Phase 3 Verification)

Per M2 v1.2.1 §7 verified-file convention, the Material Changes Summary at front of document documents Phase 3 verification-driven changes using the Standard 11 four-field MCS entry format. MC-NN identifiers in this section refer to *Phase 3 resolved verification items* and are distinct from the substructure watch-list identifiers (MC53–MC61 + MHPAEA Both/And + Telehealth Both/And) which continue to operate within the embedded SD content + back-of-document synthesis as analytical-territory references per Q-Resumption-4 disambiguation.

Pattern 12 v1.2.1 binding-scope rule: each entry's **"What it affects"** field enumerates the body locations where inline `*Verification update*` markers have been applied. Each entry must correspond to inline edits at all enumerated locations.

## Regime Declaration

Internet-access regime applies throughout D21 Phase 1, Phase 2, and Phase 3 verification. All seven SDs were drafted under internet-access regime per Standard 1; source-grounded specifics carry inline citations to the D21 source workspace. Synthesis sections at Phase 2 reformulate findings established in the SD files without introducing new substantive content (Standard 12.C). Phase 3 verification confirms web-search-tractable inline corrections (applied with `*Verification update*` italicized markers per M2 v1.2.1 §7 Capability 2); institutional-retrieval items deferred to Unverified-Items Sidecar; pre-window-date-locked T-flag dispositions + post-window observation points documented in Temporal-Changes Sidecar per Standard 18.A vector format.

## Domain-Level Data Provenance Statement

D21 operates under mixed Philadelphia-specific and national-benchmark-applied-by-inference evidence per Standard 1. Philadelphia-specific evidence concentrates at: Pennsylvania Medicaid program design (HealthChoices PH-MCO Southeast Zone; CHC-MCO architecture; PA Insurance Department oversight; PA DOH licensure; PA OMHSAS / DBHIDS / CBH architecture; Pennie state-based-exchange); Philadelphia County Medicare Advantage enrollment data and plan landscape (CMS Plan Finder; aggregator references with F-flag for CMS primary verification); Philadelphia City controlled-entity architecture (DBHIDS / CBH; PDPH clinics; School District of Philadelphia AHERA enforcement context cross-referenced to D6 MC-03); PA-3 anchor institutional architecture (Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health, CHOP; SEPA Regional CHNA participation); PA medical-malpractice tort reform 2024 architecture (HUP $182.7M verdict; documented anchor-system litigation activity). National-benchmark-applied-by-inference evidence concentrates at: national FQHC operational-margin benchmarks applied to PA-3 FQHC fiscal vulnerability framing; CBO Medicaid impact estimates applied to PA-3 expansion-state exposure; national MA market-penetration data applied with PA-state and Philadelphia-County calibration; national 340B program scale ($66.3B in 2024) applied to anchor pharmacy-revenue analytical territory. PA-3-specific disaggregated data (Medicare beneficiary count; FQHC patient-volume sub-area distribution; CBH service-utilization by sub-area; sub-area-disaggregated MA penetration) is documented as data gap and carries F-flag designation for Phase 3 verification or institutional-retrieval documentation.

Standard 13.A/13.B/13.C v2.2 aggregate constituent discipline is observed throughout. Named individuals appear only under the Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out for source-grounded identification of government officials in official capacity (Federal Register entries; agency press releases; legislative records), advocacy-organization principals in publicly-attributed capacity, and litigation case-name parties; pathway tracing, statistical profiles, and aggregate constituent profiles use stated categories without individuation per Standard 13.A.

---

## Synthesis Section 1: Domain Architecture Overview

### Scope statement

D21 documents the federal/state/local architecture governing healthcare delivery to PA-3 constituents — the structural framework through which the over-65 population, the Medicaid-eligible population, the ACA marketplace and commercial-coverage population, the safety-net-dependent population, the behavioral-health-and-SUD service population, and the specialty-clinical population receive (or fail to receive) clinical care. The domain analyzes delivery architecture rather than coverage-eligibility architecture: D12 Social Welfare owns Medicare/Medicaid/coverage-eligibility, enrollment, redetermination, and means-testing per Boundary 1; D21 owns the provider-side institutional architecture, payment-and-participation conditions, and clinical-pathway delivery for those same coverage populations.

The domain's analytical territory is unusually broad among project domains: it integrates federal entitlement architecture (Medicare Title XVIII; Medicaid Title XIX), federal marketplace architecture (ACA), federal regulatory floor architecture (CMS Conditions of Participation; ACGME; 501(r); EMTALA; HIPAA; No Surprises Act; MHPAEA; Hospital Price Transparency Rule), PA state-overlay architecture (Insurance Department MA + commercial oversight; DOH hospital and FQHC licensure; DHS OMAP managed-care contracting; OMHSAS / DBHIDS / county-based BH carve-out; DDAP SUD treatment licensure), Philadelphia city-level architecture (CBH single-MCO contracting; PDPH clinical operations; SDP school-based clinical-partnership infrastructure), and major federal-policy-cycle disruption architectures operative in 2025-2026 (IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation; OBBBA Medicaid provisions; IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit expiration; 340B Rebate Model Pilot litigation and HRSA rulemaking; Title X grant disruption sequence; MHPAEA 2024 Final Rule partial non-enforcement; Medicare telehealth post-PHE flexibility extensions). The breadth is structural: healthcare delivery in the United States is irreducibly multi-payer and multi-regulator, and the D21 architectural analysis reflects that irreducibility.

### Substructure summary

The D21 substructure (lead-approved 2026-05-09) adopts an **instrument-based organizing principle with three structural carve-outs**. Sub-Domains 1–3 decompose by federal entitlement / regulatory instrument (Medicare in SD1; Medicaid in SD2; ACA marketplace plus commercial in SD3). Sub-Domains 4–6 are structural carve-outs from the instrument-based decomposition: SD4 (hospital institutional architecture) carves out the institutional regulatory architecture that operates across all three payer instruments and concentrates at PA-3's anchor hospital institutions; SD5 (FQHC and safety-net delivery) carves out the safety-net-institutional architecture that operates at the structurally-distinctive intersection of federal § 330 program architecture, 340B drug-pricing architecture, and cumulative federal-policy-cycle fiscal vulnerability; SD6 (behavioral health and SUD delivery) carves out the structurally-distinctive Pennsylvania county-based behavioral-health-MCO carve-out architecture (the only such carve-out among the 50 states with this scope) and the SUD delivery architecture. SD7 (specialty clinical and cross-cutting delivery) closes Phase 1 architecturally at the convergence of cross-domain principal-anchor deferrals (three from D13; secondary cross-references to D6, D2, D9, D10, D11, D24) and concurrent federal-policy-cycle mechanisms (IIJA reauthorization; 340B post-vacatur trajectory; Title X disruption sequence; Medicare telehealth post-PHE; OBBBA flow-through; MHPAEA non-enforcement).

The eight-item Multi-Classification (MC) watch list at substructure (MC53–MC60) expanded to nine items during Phase 1 execution with MC61 surfaced at SD3 (Pennie post-EPTC-expiration Both/And candidate) and Telehealth Both/And surfaced at SD7 <a id="g21-sd7-04"></a>G21-SD7-04 (forward MC62 candidate at lead direction). Two hold-open-magnitude (HOM) candidates were flagged at substructure: SD4 anchor community-benefit commitment-vs-outcome shape (5th confirmed-pending instance at substructure; operationalized in Phase 1 as the 7th confirmed-pending instance project-wide following D6-Q2 Phase 3 PRIMARY confirmation) and SD5 safety-net cumulative fiscal vulnerability emergent-from-interaction shape (2nd confirmed-pending instance project-wide; within-shape sub-pattern variation cumulative-impact-on-institutions, distinct from D11 SD7 cumulative-impact-on-individuals).

SD-as-synthesis evaluation at substructure: NO. The default M2 §6 separate-synthesis-content embedded in OUTPUT applies; no single SD functions as cumulative synthesis. Domain synthesis sections integrate across SDs per the kickoff-specified Section 1–5 architecture.

### Cross-cutting threads (operationalized at each SD as Thread A / Thread B / Thread C)

Each Phase 1 SD declared three analytical threads (Thread A, Thread B, Thread C) tailored to that SD's substantive territory. The threads are thematically aligned at the domain level while operationalized SD-specifically:

- **Thread A (federal-floor + state-overlay architecture) — operative at all 7 SDs.** The federal entitlement and regulatory floor (Medicare Title XVIII at SD1; Medicaid Title XIX at SD2; ACA at SD3; CMS CoP + ACGME + 501(r) + EMTALA + HIPAA + Price Transparency Rule at SD4; HRSA § 330 + 340B + FTCA at SD5; MHPAEA + SAMHSA + DEA + 988 at SD6; specialty service-line federal architecture at SD7) operates with Pennsylvania state-administrative-overlay (Insurance Department at SD1/SD3/SD6; DHS OMAP at SD2; DOH licensure at SD4/SD5; OMHSAS at SD6) and at SD6 a structurally-distinctive Philadelphia city-level carve-out (DBHIDS / CBH single-MCO architecture). The federal-state interface is asymmetric: federal authority dominates at program-design structural features; state and local authority operate at administrative and consumer-protection layers.

- **Thread B (federal-policy-cycle disruption mechanism — varies by SD).** Each SD documents a substantively-distinct federal-policy-cycle disruption mechanism operative in 2025-2026: IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program delivery-side operationalization at SD1; OBBBA Sections 71107 / 71115 / 71117 / 71119 Medicaid delivery-side flow-through at SD2; IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit December 31, 2025 expiration at SD3; six-dimensional anchor accountability framework completion via INHERITANCE from D6 Synthesis Section 2 at SD4; 340B Rebate Model Pilot court vacatur and HRSA RFI trajectory at SD5; MHPAEA 2024 Final Rule Trump-administration partial non-enforcement at SD6; convergence of IIJA reauthorization inflection + 340B post-RFI + Title X disruption + Medicare telehealth post-2027 + OBBBA flow-through + MHPAEA non-enforcement at SD7. The Thread B mechanisms operate concurrently and compound at SD5 (six concurrent mechanisms) and SD7 (six convergent mechanisms at the cross-cutting closure layer).

- **Thread C (Both/And designations and cross-SD / cross-domain integration).** Each SD operationalizes the Both/And designation discipline at one or more MC items: MC54 at SD1 (Medicare Advantage federal-administrative-vulnerability + substantive coordination); MC60 at SD2 (substantive managed-care architecture + structural OBBBA fiscal disruption); MC61 candidate at SD3 (substantive ACA architecture + structural EPTC-expiration disruption); MC53 at SD4 (501(r) community-benefit substantive contribution + revenue-cycle structural impact); MC55 at SD5 (substantive § 330 + 340B + sliding-fee architecture + cumulative structural fiscal vulnerability); MC56 at SD6 (CBH single-MCO substantive innovation + structural concentration risk; MHPAEA Both/And at G21-SD6-02); MC57/58/59 candidates plus Telehealth Both/And at SD7. Thread C also carries the cross-SD bidirectional integration (D21 SDs cross-reference each other through Standard 9 specificity) and the cross-domain principal-anchor architecture (D6 / D7 / D8 / D9 / D10 / D11 / D12 / D13 / D24 cross-references operative).

### The seven sub-domains in document order

**SD1 — Medicare Delivery Architecture (8,474w).** Medicare Title XVIII federal entitlement architecture covering the four Medicare parts (A hospital insurance; B medical insurance; C Medicare Advantage MCO architecture; D prescription drug coverage); federal regulatory framework conditioning provider participation, plan operations, pharmacy delivery; Pennsylvania Insurance Department MA regulatory overlay; PA Department of Aging Medicare counseling architecture; Philadelphia Corporation for Aging APPRISE/PA MEDI counselor architecture; IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program delivery-side operationalization (10 Part D drugs effective January 1, 2026; ~$1.5B annual beneficiary OOP savings estimate); MC54 PRIMARY Both/And operationalization on Medicare Advantage federal-administrative-vulnerability + substantive coordination including D-SNP behavioral integration architecture and one-way structural lock-in absent Pennsylvania state guaranteed-issue Medigap return rights outside the initial 6-month Open Enrollment Period.

**SD2 — Medicaid Delivery Architecture (8,737w).** Medicaid Title XIX federal entitlement architecture; Pennsylvania HealthChoices Physical Health MCO 4-plan Southeast Zone (Health Partners Plans / Keystone First / UPMC for You / Independence-administered AmeriHealth Caritas) plus 5th plan re-procurement architecture; Community HealthChoices (CHC-MCO) 5-plan re-procurement architecture serving dual-eligibles and LTSS-eligible adults; PA Medicaid PPS rate-setting trajectory; specialty network adequacy architecture; BH-PH care coordination architecture at the carve-out interface (cross-reference SD6); MC60 PRIMARY Both/And operationalization on OBBBA Sections 71107 + 71115 + 71117 + 71119 provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown delivery-side flow-through (effective October 2026 through January 2027 and beyond; CBO estimates 9.1M Medicaid recipients impacted by FY2034); Boundary 1 with D12 SD2 maintained at delivery-side interface.

**SD3 — ACA Marketplace and Commercial Insurance Delivery (7,486w).** ACA marketplace federal architecture; Pennsylvania State-Based Exchange Pennie under Act 42 of 2019 architecture; MC61 candidate Both/And operationalization on IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit December 31, 2025 expiration delivery-side coverage loss (Pennie 102% premium increase 2026; total enrollment decline from ~500,000 in 2025 to 452,525 as of May 1, 2026; cumulative cancellations exceeding 145,000; House-passed 3-year EPTC extension pending Senate); OBBBA H.R. 1 ACA marketplace verification + immigrant-eligibility modifications; PA Act 54 of 2024 State Health Insurance Exchange Affordability Program appropriation gap; MHPAEA commercial implementation network-adequacy and enforcement gap (parallels SD6 G21-SD6-02); No Surprises Act IDR architecture operational complexity; ERISA-preempted self-funded plan architecture.

**SD4 — Hospital Institutional Architecture (8,840w).** CMS Conditions of Participation (42 C.F.R. Parts 482-485); IRC § 501(r) community benefit / FAP / CHNA / Schedule H architecture; EMTALA emergency-stabilization-and-screening obligations; HIPAA institutional architecture; No Surprises Act hospital-side; Hospital Price Transparency Rule (only 30% of PA hospitals compliant per independent audit); ACGME training architecture (specialty-distribution + primary-care-pipeline + geographic-concentration Both/And); PA medical malpractice 2024 tort reform 18-month-window architecture delivery-side implications (HUP $182.7M verdict; Temple $44.9M; Jefferson $2.5M; Hahnemann $32M); MC53 PRIMARY Both/And operationalization on anchor community-benefit substantive contribution + structural revenue-cycle impact on PA-3 household financial security via medical-debt architecture; **commitment-vs-outcome HOM 7th-instance candidate at G21-SD4-01**; **six-dimensional anchor accountability framework completion via INHERITANCE** from D6 Synthesis Section 2 (D6 environmental + D7 real estate + D8 procurement + D9 fiscal + D10 employment + D21 SD4 healthcare delivery) without re-extension at framework level; D6 MC-03 SDP AHERA criminal enforcement cross-reference at <a id="g21-sd4-06"></a>G21-SD4-06.

**SD5 — FQHC and Safety-Net Delivery (6,832w).** HRSA Health Center Program § 330 architecture; 340B Drug Pricing Program (42 U.S.C. § 256b); sliding-fee scale architecture; FTCA medical malpractice coverage architecture; Community Health Center Fund (CHCF) reauthorization at $4.6B FY 2026 with December 31, 2026 cliff (chronic short-term extension pattern since 2019); 340B Rebate Model Pilot Program court vacatur and HRSA RFI trajectory; OBBBA Section 71109 noncitizen Medicaid restrictions effective October 1, 2026; Hahnemann-precedent institutional-fragility architectural context; PA-3 FQHC roster and Philadelphia Department of Public Health clinical operations; MC55 PRIMARY HOM emergent-from-interaction 2nd-instance candidate operationalization on PA-3 FQHC + safety-net cumulative fiscal vulnerability under six concurrent federal-policy-cycle mechanisms (OBBBA Medicaid + 6-month redetermination + 340B Rebate Pilot + CHCF reauthorization + provider-tax stepdown + Hahnemann-precedent); within-shape sub-pattern variation cumulative-impact-on-institutions.

**SD6 — Behavioral Health and SUD Delivery (10,219w).** MHPAEA federal parity architecture; 2024 Final Rule with Trump-administration partial non-enforcement policy operational impact (May 9-15, 2025 non-enforcement; March 3, 2026 Tri-Agencies Fourth Report; ERIC v. DOL/HHS/Treasury case in abeyance); SAMHSA 42 C.F.R. Part 8 OTP architecture; MAT Act DATA-Waiver elimination; DEA-HHS permanent telemedicine flexibility (substantive federal architectural simplification); 988 Suicide and Crisis Lifeline implementation; Pennsylvania Behavioral HealthChoices county-based BH-MCO carve-out architecture (5 BH-MCOs across 67 counties; **Community Behavioral Health (CBH) exclusively in Philadelphia County**); CBH operational since February 1997; ~420,000 Philadelphia Medicaid recipients with behavioral health coverage; 100,700+ active service users 2023 with $860M+ expenditures; MC56 Both/And operationalization (PRIMARY-vs-non-PRIMARY designation pending lead direction at batch checkpoint review); MHPAEA Both/And at G21-SD6-02; CCBHC participation architecture; PA OMHSAS + DBHIDS architectural-overlay.

**SD7 — Specialty Clinical and Cross-Cutting Delivery (11,744w).** Specialty service-line architecture (oncology delivery at Penn Abramson, Temple, Jefferson Sidney Kimmel, CHOP pediatric oncology; cardiology, orthopedics, ESRD/dialysis); cross-cutting workforce architecture (nursing; pharmacist; advanced practice; behavioral-health; ACGME-funded GME pipeline); medical respite + street medicine; pharmacy regulatory architecture; school-based clinical delivery; environmental-health clinical interface; **three cross-domain principal-anchor deferrals** (D13 G13-SD7-01 IIJA reauthorization September 30, 2026 inflection point; D13 cumulative-burden geography Eastwick/Cobbs Creek concentration; D13 PA-state-fiscal-architectural asymmetry); **MC57/58/59 candidate Both/And designations** (IIJA reauthorization + 340B Rebate Pilot post-court-vacatur + Title X 2025-2026 administrative-disruption sequence with Pennsylvania partial-affected exposure); **Telehealth Both/And** at G21-SD7-04 (Medicare telehealth post-December 31, 2027 reversion risk + select permanent provisions); convergence-layer documentation of six concurrent federal-policy-cycle mechanisms; veterans-civilian boundary architecture (cross-reference D24 SD1/SD4/SD5).

## Synthesis Section 2: Aggregate Finding

### The aggregate analytical finding

D21's aggregate finding is that **PA-3 healthcare delivery operates through a federal-entitlement-floor + Pennsylvania-state-overlay + dynamic-federal-policy-cycle architecture in which substantive delivery contributions and structural disruption mechanisms are simultaneously operative across multiple payer instruments, multiple institutional architectures, and multiple regulatory layers — producing distributional outcomes whose direction is documentable but whose magnitude is held open at the points of cumulative-mechanism interaction**. The architecture is not coherent in the sense of being designed-as-a-system; it is the structural consequence of layered federal program design choices interacting with Pennsylvania's specific implementation choices (HealthChoices PH-MCO + CHC-MCO dual managed-care; county-based BH-MCO carve-out; state-based-exchange Pennie; PA Insurance Department oversight) interacting with Philadelphia-specific institutional features (CBH single-MCO contracting; PA-3 anchor institutional concentration at Penn Medicine / Temple Health / Jefferson Health / CHOP; safety-net infrastructure at Hahnemann-precedent-shaped FQHC + PDPH clinical landscape) interacting with the 2025-2026 federal-policy-cycle architecture (IRA delivery-side architecture; OBBBA Medicaid provisions; EPTC expiration; 340B Rebate Model Pilot trajectory; Title X disruption sequence; Medicare telehealth post-PHE flexibilities; MHPAEA partial non-enforcement; six-dimensional anchor accountability framework operative).

The aggregate finding is stated as a structural consequence of documented design features per Standard 4. The federal entitlement and regulatory floor (Medicare; Medicaid; ACA; CMS CoP; ACGME; 501(r); EMTALA; HIPAA; MHPAEA; SAMHSA; HRSA § 330; 340B; FTCA; specialty service-line federal architecture) provides substantive delivery capacity to PA-3 constituents at scale: approximately 2,981,142 Pennsylvania residents enrolled in Medicare for the 2026 plan year; ~420,000 Philadelphia Medicaid recipients with behavioral health coverage through CBH; ~9 million Part D enrollees nationally benefiting from the IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program first-year negotiated prices; the SEPA Regional CHNA architecture coordinating community benefit planning across PA-3 anchor health systems; the federal MAT Act elimination of DATA-Waiver and DEA-HHS permanent telemedicine flexibility simplifying the SUD treatment architecture. The Pennsylvania state-overlay operates with substantive contribution at the consumer protection layer (PA Medigap excess-charge prohibition; PA Insurance Department MA marketing oversight), the managed-care contracting layer (PA HealthChoices PH-MCO 4-plan Southeast Zone + CHC-MCO 5-plan architecture), the licensure layer (PA DOH hospital and FQHC oversight), and the carve-out architecture (BH single-MCO assignment producing both substantive innovation and structural concentration risk at <a id="g21-sd6-01"></a>G21-SD6-01).

Simultaneously, the federal-policy-cycle architecture introduces structural disruption mechanisms operative across the same delivery instruments. OBBBA Sections 71107 + 71115 + 71117 + 71119 (provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown; MCO-tax tightening; community-engagement work requirements; 6-month redeterminations and reduced retroactive coverage) flow through to PA-3 Medicaid delivery beginning October 2026 through January 2027 with multi-year compounding through FY 2032. The IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit December 31, 2025 expiration produced documented Pennie 102% premium increases for 2026 plan year and 145,000+ cumulative cancellations through May 1, 2026, with House-passed 3-year extension pending Senate action and presidential signature. The 340B Rebate Model Pilot court vacatur (*AHA v. Kennedy*, D. Me. February 10, 2026) and HRSA post-RFI rulemaking trajectory affect PA-3 anchor hospital pharmacy revenue architecture at substantial scale (national 340B spending $66.3B in 2024). The Title X 2025-2026 administrative-disruption sequence (March 31, 2025 withholding of $65.8M affecting ~842,000 patients; December 2025 restoration; March 13, 2026 HHS guidance removing "Quality Family Planning" framework) affects Pennsylvania partial-Title-X-clinic exposure. The Medicare telehealth flexibility extension through December 31, 2027 (CAA 2026) with select permanent provisions creates a known reversion-risk inflection point at the post-2027 horizon. The MHPAEA 2024 Final Rule Trump-administration partial non-enforcement policy (operative May 9-15, 2025; March 3, 2026 Tri-Agencies Fourth Report; ERIC v. DOL/HHS/Treasury in abeyance) shifts federal parity enforcement architecture mid-cycle.

The substantive-and-structural simultaneity is operationalized through the **Both/And designations at five PRIMARY MC entries and four candidate MC entries plus Telehealth Both/And** — nine plus one operative architecture-level Both/Ands at the cross-SD scale. The Both/And discipline (Standard 14) holds that the substantive delivery contribution and the structural disruption mechanism are both simultaneously operative at the analytical territory; the disciplines that the synthesis cannot collapse to either-or (Standard 4) and cannot close held-open magnitude through synthesis assertion (Standard 14). The synthesis preserves the Both/And designations across all nine plus one architectures; PRIMARY vs candidate vs Both/And designations are documented per the MCS table at Synthesis Section 4 below.

### Six-dimensional anchor accountability framework completion via INHERITANCE at SD4

D6 Synthesis Section 2 (verified 2026-05-11) established the five-dimensional anchor accountability framework architecture for PA-3 anchor institutions (Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health, CHOP): the dimensions of environmental compliance (D6 SD1–SD5 PRINCIPAL ANCHOR at D6 Synthesis Section 2), real estate (D7 SD1 anchor real estate), procurement (D8 SD3 anchor procurement), fiscal architecture (D9 SD4 PILOET PRINCIPAL ANCHOR), and employment (D10 SD4 employment). D21 SD4 (Hospital Institutional Architecture) **inherits the five-dimensional framework as ESTABLISHED CONTEXT and adds the sixth dimension — healthcare delivery — without re-extending the framework at framework level**. The completion is documented as INHERITANCE: D21 SD4 G21-SD4-01 operates under the established five-dimensional framework architecture and adds the healthcare-delivery dimension as the sixth (and most heavily-regulated) accountability dimension; subsequent domains (D15, D16, D17, D22, D25, D26) will inherit the completed six-dimensional framework without re-extending.

The healthcare-delivery dimension is the most heavily-regulated of the six accountability dimensions: the federal regulatory floor at D21 SD4 (CMS CoP; ACGME; 501(r); EMTALA; HIPAA; No Surprises Act; ACA § 9007 community-benefit reporting; Hospital Price Transparency Rule) is uniquely dense relative to the other five accountability dimensions. The PA-3 anchor institutions operate simultaneously across all six dimensions: Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health, and CHOP appear at D6 as substantial environmental-compliance-regulated entities, at D7 as substantial real-estate actors with documented displacement and land-acquisition footprints, at D8 as substantial procurement participants, at D9 as PILOET tax-exempt institutions with documented payment-in-lieu-of-taxes contribution architecture, at D10 as substantial employers with workforce architecture relevant to PA-3 labor and employment representation, and at D21 SD4 as healthcare-delivery institutions subject to the densest federal regulatory architecture among the six dimensions.

The MC53 PRIMARY Both/And at G21-SD4-01 (anchor community-benefit commitment-vs-outcome) operationalizes the healthcare-delivery dimension specifically: the substantive community-benefit architecture (FAP; CHNA; Schedule H reporting; charity care provision; SEPA Regional CHNA collaboration) is documented as operative AND the structural impact on PA-3 household financial security via revenue-cycle architecture (medical-debt collection; medical-bankruptcy contribution; documented PA hospital Price Transparency Rule non-compliance at ~70% of PA hospitals per independent audit; CFPB medical-debt rulemaking trajectory post-*Cornerstone Credit Union League v. CFPB* July 2025 vacatur) is documented as operative. The two are simultaneously operative; the synthesis preserves the Both/And.

### Two hold-open-magnitude (HOM) candidates operative at D21

D21 surfaces two HOM candidates that the synthesis preserves at-magnitude per Standard 14 hold-open-magnitude discipline (no closure-through-synthesis-assertion). Each is documented as candidate pending lead-direction formalization at batch checkpoint review and Phase 3 verification empirical confirmation.

**HOM 1 — Commitment-vs-outcome 7th-instance candidate at G21-SD4-01 (D21 SD4 anchor hospital community benefit).** Project-wide commitment-vs-outcome HOM shape inventory at the close of D21 Phase 1 contains 7 confirmed-pending instances: G7-SD1-03 (D7 Housing/Land); D8-Q2 (D8 Commerce & Industry); D10-Q1 (D10 Labor/Employment); D24-Q1 (D24 Veterans Affairs); G11-SD1-04 (D11 Education Penn Alexander candidate); D6-Q2 (D6 Environment & Natural Resources confirmed PRIMARY per D6 MC-03 Phase 3 resolution); and the new G21-SD4-01 7th-instance candidate at D21 SD4. The shape recurs at the structurally-distinctive intersection of formal institutional commitment (community-benefit programs; financial-assistance policies; CHNA-informed implementation; partnership architectures) and constituent-level outcome trajectories (medical-debt collection; revenue-cycle pressure; financial-security disruption). The 7th-instance candidate's substantive content at SD4 documents the commitment side (501(r) FAP architecture; SEPA Regional CHNA participation; specific Penn $100M contribution to SDP environmental management; the federal community-benefit reporting requirement framework) and the outcome side (PA hospital Price Transparency Rule compliance at ~30% per independent audit; medical-debt prevalence in PA; PA medical malpractice 2024 tort reform litigation context; revenue-cycle architecture impact). The HOM preservation discipline holds that the relative magnitude of commitment-vs-outcome mechanisms cannot be ranked from the synthesis level without analytical closure that the evidence does not support; specific mechanism-by-mechanism magnitudes carry F-flag designation for Phase 3 institutional-retrieval verification (anchor FAP income thresholds and AGB methodologies F21-SD4-01; aggregate community benefit Form 990 Schedule H F21-SD4-02; medical-debt-collection-lawsuit volume F21-SD4-03; Price Transparency Rule compliance status F21-SD4-04). The HOM 7th-instance designation rests with lead direction at batch checkpoint review and Phase 3 verification empirical assessment.

**HOM 2 — Emergent-from-interaction 2nd-instance candidate at G21-SD5-01 (D21 SD5 FQHC + safety-net cumulative fiscal vulnerability) with within-shape sub-pattern variation.** Project-wide emergent-from-interaction HOM shape inventory at the close of D21 Phase 1 contains 2 confirmed-pending instances: D11 SD7 school-to-prison pipeline (1st-instance; within-shape sub-pattern variation: cumulative-impact-on-individuals — children ending up in prison through the interaction of independently-legitimate education, juvenile justice, welfare, and housing mechanisms) and the new G21-SD5-01 2nd-instance candidate at D21 SD5 (within-shape sub-pattern variation: cumulative-impact-on-institutions — FQHCs and safety-net hospitals fiscally vulnerable through the interaction of independently-legitimate OBBBA Medicaid provisions, 6-month Medicaid redetermination procedural-loss, 340B Rebate Pilot disposition, CHCF reauthorization uncertainty, provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown, and Hahnemann-precedent institutional-fragility mechanisms). The emergent-from-interaction shape is distinct from the commitment-vs-outcome shape: emergent-from-interaction surfaces cumulative consequences that no single mechanism produces independently but that the interaction of multiple independently-legitimate mechanisms produces collectively; commitment-vs-outcome surfaces the gap between formal institutional commitment and constituent-experienced outcome at a single institutional point.

The within-shape sub-pattern variation (cumulative-impact-on-individuals vs cumulative-impact-on-institutions) is the methodological innovation surfaced at D21 Phase 1 (OL-3): the emergent-from-interaction shape carries sub-pattern variation depending on whether the cumulative impact concentrates on individuals (children at D11 SD7) or institutions (safety-net providers at D21 SD5). The sub-pattern variation is documented as methodology absorption queue candidate (item 7 at batch checkpoint methodology absorption queue) for potential formalization as a methodology pattern type at lock pass. The 2nd-instance candidate and within-shape sub-pattern variation designations rest with lead direction at batch checkpoint review and Phase 3 verification empirical assessment.

### Cross-domain principal-anchor architecture preservation

D21 Synthesis Section 2 preserves cross-domain principal-anchor architecture with Standard 9 specificity. Substantive content owned by upstream verified domains (D2, D6, D7, D8, D9, D10, D11, D12, D13, D24) is cross-referenced with stable-ID specificity but **NOT re-extended at D21 layer**. The cross-domain principal-anchor deferrals operative at D21 Phase 1 — most heavily at SD7 (three D13 deferrals: G13-SD7-01 IIJA reauthorization; cumulative-burden geography Eastwick/Cobbs Creek; PA-state-fiscal-architectural asymmetry; plus D6 MC-03 G6-SD4-02 SDP AHERA; D6 MC-04/32 federal/state EJ-divergence; D6 Synthesis Section 2 five-dimensional framework INHERITANCE; D2 SD7 cancer architecture; D9 SD4 PILOET PRINCIPAL ANCHOR; D11 SD7 SDP-Penn partnership; D24 SD1/SD4/SD5 veterans-civilian boundary) — operationalize the project-wide cross-domain principal-anchor discipline OL-10: downstream domains rely on upstream verified-file content as ESTABLISHED CONTEXT; analytical extension occurs only at the cross-domain integration interface; substantive cross-domain analysis lives in the dedicated cross-domain notes architecture rather than in-domain. D21 SD4 INHERITANCE of the six-dimensional anchor accountability framework is the architectural exemplar: the framework completion is structural (the sixth dimension closes the framework) without re-extension of the five upstream principal-anchor analyses.

### Synthesis-stage interpretive moves explicitly acknowledged

Per Standard 12.C synthesis-specific source discipline, the synthesis articulates the cumulative finding using established SD findings as inputs; interpretive moves beyond reformulation are explicitly acknowledged. Three synthesis-stage interpretive moves at Synthesis Section 2: (1) the characterization of the architecture as "not coherent in the sense of being designed-as-a-system" is a synthesis-stage interpretive move applying Standard 4 advocacy-language discipline to avoid intent-attribution while documenting structural-consequence; the underlying SD findings document the federal/state/local layering and the federal-policy-cycle interaction without making the "not designed-as-a-system" characterization at SD level. (2) The characterization of the healthcare-delivery dimension as "the most heavily-regulated of the six accountability dimensions" is a synthesis-stage comparative claim that the SD-level Thread A federal-regulatory-floor documentation at SD4 supports but does not make explicit; the synthesis claim rests on the documented SD4 federal-regulatory floor (CMS CoP + ACGME + 501(r) + EMTALA + HIPAA + NSA + Price Transparency Rule + ACA § 9007) being denser than the D6/D7/D8/D9/D10 regulatory floors at the same anchor institutional set. (3) The characterization of the substantive-and-structural simultaneity as operationalized through "nine plus one operative architecture-level Both/Ands" is a synthesis-stage tally of the Both/And designations documented in Phase 1; the underlying designations are SD-level and the Both/And character is preserved through the synthesis without collapse.

*End Synthesis Section 2.*

## Synthesis Section 3: Held-Open-at-Magnitude Inventory

Per Standard 14 hold-open-magnitude discipline (distinct from Both/And designation; held-open-magnitude preserves relative-magnitude questions where closure would require analytical assertion beyond what evidence supports). The held-open territory at D21 organizes into five categories: (1) two HOM candidates at architectural-load-bearing magnitude; (2) F-flag clusters constituting structural-pattern observations; (3) MC designation territory held open pending lead direction; (4) chronic-pattern observations operative across multiple SDs; (5) methodology absorption queue candidates surfacing through D21 Phase 1 execution. All entries below preserve magnitude without closure-through-synthesis-assertion.

### 3.1 HOM candidates at architectural-load-bearing magnitude

**(a) HOM 1 — Commitment-vs-outcome 7th-instance candidate at G21-SD4-01 (D21 SD4 anchor hospital community benefit).** The relative magnitude of commitment-side mechanisms (501(r) FAP architecture; SEPA Regional CHNA collaboration; specific anchor financial contributions including Penn's documented $100M to SDP environmental management coincident with the SDP AHERA enforcement period; the federal community-benefit reporting requirement framework with Form 990 Schedule H disclosures; the operative SEPA Regional CHNA implementation strategy frameworks) vs outcome-side mechanisms (medical-debt collection pressure on PA-3 households; PA hospital Hospital Price Transparency Rule compliance at ~30% per independent audit; documented PA medical malpractice 2024 tort reform context including HUP $182.7M, Temple $44.9M, Jefferson $2.5M, and prior Hahnemann $32M verdicts; revenue-cycle architecture impact at the household financial-security layer; the CFPB medical-debt rulemaking trajectory disrupted by the July 2025 *Cornerstone Credit Union League v. CFPB* E.D. Tex. vacatur and uncertain Trump CFPB renewed-rulemaking trajectory) is **held open at magnitude**. Phase 3 institutional-retrieval items pending (F21-SD4-01 anchor FAP income thresholds and AGB methodologies; F21-SD4-02 aggregate community benefit Form 990 Schedule H; F21-SD4-03 medical-debt-collection-lawsuit volume; F21-SD4-04 Price Transparency Rule compliance status). The 7th-instance designation rests with lead direction at batch checkpoint review and Phase 3 verification empirical assessment per OL-4.

**(b) HOM 2 — Emergent-from-interaction 2nd-instance candidate at G21-SD5-01 (D21 SD5 FQHC + safety-net cumulative fiscal vulnerability) with within-shape sub-pattern variation.** The relative magnitude of six concurrent federal-policy-cycle mechanisms — OBBBA Section 71115/71117 provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown (FY2028-FY2032 phase-down); OBBBA Section 71107 6-month Medicaid redetermination procedural-loss (effective December 2026); 340B Rebate Model Pilot disposition trajectory (court-halted late 2025; HRSA RFI closed April 20, 2026; H.R. 7391 bipartisan legislative protection trajectory); Community Health Center Fund (CHCF) reauthorization at the December 31, 2026 cliff ($4.6B FY 2026; chronic short-term extension pattern since 2019; NACHC-requested $5.8B / 3 years); OBBBA Section 71109 noncitizen Medicaid restrictions (effective October 1, 2026); Hahnemann-precedent institutional-fragility context — interacting to produce PA-3 FQHC + safety-net fiscal vulnerability is **held open at magnitude**. The shape diagnostic holds that cumulative consequence emerges from the interaction of independently-legitimate mechanisms; no single mechanism's magnitude can be ranked from the synthesis level without analytical closure. Within-shape sub-pattern variation (cumulative-impact-on-institutions at G21-SD5-01 vs cumulative-impact-on-individuals at D11 SD7 school-to-prison) is documented as a distinct sub-pattern; the relative analytical weight of sub-pattern variation is itself held open at magnitude pending methodology absorption queue evaluation. Phase 3 institutional-retrieval items pending (F21-SD5-06 revenue-mix breakdown; F21-SD5-10 PA-3 FQHC 340B pharmacy revenue; F21-SD5-12 FY 2026 FQHC operating margin / cash on hand; F21-SD5-11 OBBBA Section 71109 affected population).

### 3.2 F-flag clusters constituting structural-pattern observations

**(c) SD5 six concurrent federal-policy-cycle mechanism cluster.** The six concurrent federal-policy-cycle mechanisms documented at G21-SD5-01 are themselves the structural pattern; the F-flag cluster (F21-SD5-01 PCMH-recognition; F21-SD5-02 PA-3 FQHC complete roster; F21-SD5-03 PA statewide FQHC count; F21-SD5-04 PDPH-clinic utilization; F21-SD5-05 PA-3 FQHC aggregate patient volume; F21-SD5-06 revenue-mix breakdown; F21-SD5-07 PA RHTF allocation; F21-SD5-08 sub-area utilization; F21-SD5-09 FPL thresholds; F21-SD5-10 PA-3 FQHC 340B pharmacy revenue; F21-SD5-11 OBBBA Section 71109 affected population; F21-SD5-12 FY 2026 FQHC operating margin / cash on hand) operates as institutional-retrieval scaffolding around the structurally-load-bearing HOM at G21-SD5-01. The pattern observation (six concurrent mechanisms cumulatively interacting with shared institutional impact target) is preserved as structural pattern; specific magnitudes carry F-flag designation.

**(d) SD7 convergence-layer architecture cluster.** D21 SD7 closes Phase 1 architecturally at the convergence of six concurrent federal-policy-cycle mechanisms (IIJA reauthorization September 30, 2026 inflection point; Medicare telehealth post-December 31, 2027 reversion risk; 340B Rebate Model Pilot post-court-vacatur HRSA RFI rulemaking trajectory; Title X 2025-2026 administrative-disruption sequence; OBBBA Medicaid delivery-side flow-through; MHPAEA 2024 Final Rule partial non-enforcement). The convergence-layer is itself a structural-pattern observation: the cross-cutting closure layer of Phase 1 is structurally an analytical position where multiple independently-operative federal-policy-cycle mechanisms surface simultaneously and produce cross-mechanism interaction effects at the healthcare-delivery-architecture interface. The convergence-layer-analysis pattern formalization (methodology absorption queue item 11) is itself **held open at methodological magnitude** pending lock-pass evaluation.

### 3.3 MC designation territory held open pending lead direction

**(e) MC56 PRIMARY-vs-non-PRIMARY designation (D21 SD6 G21-SD6-01 CBH single-MCO architecture).** Substructure note observed that MC56 "may downgrade to non-PRIMARY if substantive content does not support PRIMARY"; SD6 Phase 1 execution documented the Both/And shape with sustained substantive contribution (CBH innovation architecture; school-based BH since 2003; CCBHC participation; D-SNP behavioral integration) AND sustained structural concentration risk (single-MCO architecture; OBBBA fiscal-pressure flow-through). The substantive content per Q-WC-01 SD6 rationale appears to support PRIMARY designation, but final designation rests with lead direction. **Held open** pending lead determination at batch checkpoint review and lock-pass formalization.

**(f) MC57 / MC58 / MC59 / MC61 candidate-Both/And formalization.** MC57 (D21 SD7 G21-SD7-01 IIJA reauthorization delivery-side flow-through); MC58 (D21 SD7 G21-SD7-02 340B Rebate Model Pilot court vacatur + HRSA RFI rulemaking trajectory); MC59 (D21 SD7 G21-SD7-03 Title X family planning administrative-disruption sequence + Pennsylvania partial-affected exposure); MC61 (D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01 IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit expiration delivery-side coverage loss). Each carries operationalized Both/And content at the corresponding SD section and operative principal-anchor cross-references; formal candidate-to-confirmed designation rests with lead direction at batch checkpoint review and lock-pass formalization. **Held open** at the designation-formalization level; substantive Both/And content is documented in the SD files.

**(g) Telehealth Both/And designation at G21-SD7-04.** Medicare telehealth post-December 31, 2027 reversion-risk Both/And documented at SD7 with potential MC62 designation if formalized at lead direction. Substantive content includes CAA 2026 extension through December 31, 2027 (signed February 3, 2026), CY 2026 PFS Final Rule CMS-1832-F permanent direct supervision + virtual teaching physician + behavioral/mental audio-only permanent under conditions. **Held open** at the designation-formalization level.

### 3.4 Chronic-pattern observations operative across multiple SDs

**(h) Chronic short-term extension pattern.** The Community Health Center Fund (CHCF) has not been multi-year reauthorized since 2019; the program operates under chronic short-term extension architecture (most recently extended through December 31, 2026 in the 2026 Consolidated Appropriations Act at $4.6B FY 2026). Parallel patterns: Medicare telehealth post-PHE flexibility has operated under chronic short-term extension since CARES Act (most recently CAA 2026 extends through December 31, 2027); IIJA reauthorization is on a chronic short-term extension trajectory per D13 G13-SD7-01 cross-reference (current trajectory: no reauthorization bill introduced as of May 2026; T4America projection of extension rather than reauthorization mechanism); Title X grant-cycle disruption operates within the broader chronic short-term grant-cycle architecture at HHS. The pattern itself — whether chronic short-term extension constitutes a methodology-recognizable structural pattern type analogous to MC designation — is **held open at methodological magnitude** per OQ-D21-SD5-A and OQ-D21-SD7-A; methodology absorption queue item 9 candidate for lock-pass evaluation.

**(i) Within-shape sub-pattern variation pattern formalization.** The within-shape sub-pattern variation of the emergent-from-interaction HOM shape (cumulative-impact-on-individuals at D11 SD7 vs cumulative-impact-on-institutions at G21-SD5-01) operationally surfaces a sub-pattern-distinct analytical territory within a single HOM shape. Whether within-shape sub-pattern variation should be formalized as a methodology pattern type is **held open at methodological magnitude** per OL-3 and methodology absorption queue item 7; lock-pass evaluation pending.

# D21 Healthcare Delivery — Sub-Domain 1
## Medicare Delivery Architecture

**Date:** 2026-05-11
**Phase:** 1 (Per-SD Execution; fresh cycle)
**Domain:** 21 — Healthcare Delivery
**Sub-Domain:** 1 of 7
**Cycle position:** Final domain Phase 1+2+3 in WA3 scope before methodology lock pass; SD1 handled as calibration test (D6 pattern); SD2-N batch follows after SD1 admin review + lead approval
**Methodology stack:** M0 v2.2 · M2 v1.2.1 · Skills v3
**Status:** Phase 1 draft — SD1 checkpoint pending lead review

---

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD1 documents the Medicare delivery architecture governing the Medicare-eligible population in PA-3: the federal entitlement framework under Social Security Act Title XVIII that establishes the four Medicare parts (Part A hospital insurance; Part B medical insurance; Part C Medicare Advantage MCO architecture; Part D prescription drug coverage); the federal regulatory framework that conditions provider participation, plan operations, and pharmacy delivery; the state regulatory overlay (Pennsylvania Insurance Department MA plan regulation; PA Department of Aging Medicare counseling architecture); the local administration overlay (Philadelphia Corporation for Aging APPRISE/PA MEDI counselors); and the Inflation Reduction Act of 2022 Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program whose first negotiated prices for 10 Part D drugs became effective January 1, 2026. The sub-domain analyzes Medicare delivery as a clinical-and-pharmacy-access architecture rather than as a coverage-eligibility architecture. **Boundary 1** (per D21 substructure §2): D12 SD2 owns Medicare eligibility, enrollment, and Low-Income Subsidy / Medicare Savings Program eligibility architecture; D21 SD1 owns clinical delivery, plan operations, pharmacy delivery, and provider participation architecture.

Sub-Domains 2 through 7 analyze Medicaid delivery architecture (SD2), ACA marketplace and commercial insurance delivery (SD3), hospital institutional architecture (SD4), FQHC and safety-net delivery (SD5), behavioral health and SUD delivery (SD6), and specialty clinical and cross-cutting delivery (SD7).

**Regime declaration.** Internet-access regime. Current-state plan enrollment figures, Medicare Advantage market penetration, IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program status, Part D out-of-pocket cap levels, Pennsylvania Medigap underwriting rules, and CMS regulatory posture claims are expected to be retrieved; specific figures carry sources per Standard 12. The Social Security Act Title XVIII architecture, 42 C.F.R. Conditions of Participation framework, and Medicare Modernization Act Part D architecture are framework-level common-knowledge statutory references; specific section citations are sourced where claims are specific rather than framework-level. Mixed-regime drafting is prohibited per Standard 1; this SD operates entirely under internet-access regime.

**Analytical posture.** Both/And-primary at MC54 (Medicare Advantage architecture). Per D21 substructure §6, MC54 is the within-domain analytical-tension flagged at SD1: substantive Medicare Advantage choice architecture serves beneficiaries through predictable cost-sharing, supplemental benefits not available in Traditional Medicare (dental, vision, hearing, OTC allowances, transportation), and care coordination, AND structural impact reshapes the traditional Medicare beneficiary population through risk-adjustment dynamics, provider network restrictions, prior authorization burden, and one-way structural lock-in absent state guaranteed-issue Medigap return rights. Both/And designation operative through Phase 1 SD1 drafting; Phase 1 confirms or downgrades. No held-open-magnitude territory at SD1 (the substructure's HOM candidates concentrate at SD4 anchor community benefit and SD5 safety-net fiscal vulnerability).

**Thread declarations.** Three analytical threads operative at SD1:

- **D21-SD1-Thread A (federal entitlement floor with state-regulated MA-overlay architecture).** Medicare Title XVIII is a federal entitlement with statutory stability HIGH at the program level; provider participation, plan operations, and beneficiary clinical experience operate under federal regulation with Pennsylvania Insurance Department overlay on Medicare Advantage plan marketing and consumer protection. The federal-state interface is asymmetric: federal authority dominates at structural design; state authority operates at consumer protection and plan-licensing layers.
- **D21-SD1-Thread B (IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program delivery-side operationalization).** The IRA-authorized Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program's first negotiated prices for 10 Part D drugs became effective January 1, 2026 [Source 1]; the substantive architecture (statutory authority; drug selection; negotiation process) is D2 SD4 territory per D21 substructure §3 cross-reference; D21 SD1 owns delivery-side implementation (Part D plan formulary integration; pharmacy point-of-sale price reflection; beneficiary out-of-pocket cost impact). The Trump administration's Executive Order 14273 (April 15, 2025) kept the program intact while directing the Secretary of Health and Human Services to propose modifications [Source 14]; the 2025 budget reconciliation law modified the orphan drug exclusion in ways that affect future drug-selection eligibility [Source 2].
- **D21-SD1-Thread C (Medicare Advantage substantive-and-structural Both/And at MC54).** MC54 territory carries through SD1 drafting; the MA structural-displacement question — including the structural lock-in arising from Pennsylvania's lack of guaranteed-issue Medigap return rights outside the initial 6-month Open Enrollment Period — surfaces as a load-bearing gap finding at §7.

**PA-3 stakes framing.** Medicare delivery is the federal program through which the over-65 population and qualifying disabled adults receive most of their healthcare in PA-3. With approximately 2.6 million Pennsylvania residents aged 65 or older (about 20% of total state population) [Source 16] and approximately 2,981,142 Pennsylvania residents enrolled in Medicare for the 2026 plan year [Source 4; F21-SD1-01], Medicare delivery is structurally consequential for a large fraction of PA-3 households — directly for elderly and disabled-adult constituents, indirectly for the household members and caregivers whose health-economic decisions interact with a Medicare beneficiary's coverage. The federal House representation has direct advocacy leverage on Medicare appropriations, CMS regulatory posture, IRA drug-pricing protections, and Medicare Advantage plan oversight; representation does not directly control plan-by-plan benefit decisions or provider participation choices, but federal-program-condition pressure and committee oversight are operative levers.

---

## Section 2: Authority Chain

The Medicare delivery architecture is layered federal-statutory and federal-regulatory at the program-design layer; federal-agency at the implementation layer; state-regulatory at the MA plan oversight and consumer-protection layer; and local-agency at the counseling and beneficiary-navigation layer. The constituent layer (aggregate profiles introduced in Section 5) interfaces with the agency layer at the pathway points traced in Section 3.

**Constitutional Foundation.** Medicare operates under the Article I Section 8 Spending Clause (taxing-and-spending for the general welfare) and is administered through Title XVIII of the Social Security Act as amended. The Tenth Amendment's reservation of police powers to the states grounds Pennsylvania's regulatory overlay on Medicare Advantage plan marketing, consumer protection, and Medicare Supplement (Medigap) underwriting rules. No constitutional tension at the program-design level; constitutional tensions at the federal-state interface (such as those that surface in Medicaid expansion debates) do not operate at the Medicare level because Medicare is a federal program operating directly with beneficiaries and providers rather than through state administration.

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

*Social Security Act Title XVIII (Medicare; 42 U.S.C. § 1395 et seq.).* The foundational statutory architecture; establishes Medicare Part A (hospital insurance); Part B (Supplementary Medical Insurance); Part C (Medicare Advantage); Part D (Voluntary Prescription Drug Benefit Program). Key provisions: § 1395 establishes program framework; § 1395cc establishes provider agreements ("Conditions of Participation" basis); § 1395w-21 et seq. establishes Part C Medicare Advantage program (added by Balanced Budget Act of 1997, amended by Medicare Modernization Act of 2003); § 1395w-101 et seq. establishes Part D (added by MMA 2003); § 1395i et seq. establishes Part A trust fund. **Statutory stability: HIGH.** The Medicare entitlement structure has been continuously authorized since 1965; statutory revisions have expanded scope rather than constricted entitlement. **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE.** The statutory foundation is durable; administrative implementation (CMS rulemaking; coverage determinations; payment policies; Medicare Advantage capitation rates) is subject to executive-branch policy direction year-over-year. Cross-references: D12 SD2 (Medicare eligibility and enrollment); D2 SD4 (IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation substantive architecture); D24 SD1 (federal-veterans Title 38 healthcare delivery parallel).

*Inflation Reduction Act of 2022 (P.L. 117-169; Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program).* Adds Medicare Part E (Drug Price Negotiation) at § 1191 of the Social Security Act; authorizes the Secretary of Health and Human Services to negotiate prices for certain high-cost single-source drugs covered under Medicare Part D (and beginning 2028, Part B). Establishes annual out-of-pocket cap for Part D drug costs — $2,100 in 2026, up from $2,000 in 2025 [Source 14]; eliminates the prior catastrophic coverage cost-sharing; restructures Part D benefit phases; establishes inflation rebates for Part B and Part D drugs whose prices rise faster than inflation. Key provisions for SD1 delivery analysis: § 1192 drug selection criteria (high-expenditure single-source drugs without generic/biosimilar competition; small-molecule drugs eligible after 7 years FDA approval, biologics after 11 years); § 1194 negotiation process; § 1193 coverage requirement (Part D plans must cover selected drugs at the negotiated maximum fair price). **Statutory stability: HIGH-MODERATE.** Statutorily anchored; political contestation continues. The Trump administration's Executive Order 14273 (April 15, 2025) maintained the program but directed modification proposals [Source 14]; the 2025 budget reconciliation law (sometimes referred to as OBBBA) broadened the orphan drug exclusion, affecting future drug-selection eligibility [Source 2]. **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE.** CMS implementation guidance is subject to revision; the program's core structural design is statutorily protected. Cross-references: D2 SD4 (substantive architecture; D2 owns); D21 SD7 (pharmacy delivery interface).

*Medicare Modernization Act of 2003 (P.L. 108-173).* Restructured Part C as Medicare Advantage; created Part D voluntary prescription drug program; established Medicare Savings Account framework; established competitive bidding for durable medical equipment. **Statutory stability: HIGH.** Durable framework. **Administrative vulnerability: HIGH.** Annual Medicare Advantage capitation rate setting, star-rating methodology, prior-authorization regulation, and MA marketing rules are subject to substantial year-over-year administrative variation. Cross-references: SD4 (Medicare GME via hospital cost reports under MMA hospital-payment provisions).

*Emergency Medical Treatment and Active Labor Act (EMTALA; 42 U.S.C. § 1395dd).* Conditions Medicare provider agreement on emergency-medical screening and stabilization for any individual presenting at a Medicare-participating hospital emergency department, regardless of payer status. **Statutory stability: HIGH.** **Administrative vulnerability: LOW-MODERATE.** Statutory and judicially enforced; agency discretion narrow. Cross-references: D21 SD4 (hospital institutional architecture); D21 SD7 (specialty emergency clinical delivery).

**Federal Agency Layer.**

*Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS).* Operating division of HHS; primary federal agency administering Medicare. Address: 7500 Security Boulevard, Baltimore, MD 21244. Key sub-components for D21 SD1: Center for Medicare (Medicare fee-for-service and Medicare Advantage coverage and payment policy); Center for Program Integrity (Medicare fraud, waste, and abuse oversight); CMS Office of Communications; Center for Drug and Health Plan Choice. The CMS Administrator is a Senate-confirmed presidential appointee; the current Administrator's policy direction shapes CMS's annual Medicare Advantage capitation rate announcement, Part D Redesign Program Instructions, and MDPNP implementation guidance. **Administrative vulnerability: HIGH** — CMS's discretionary authority over rate setting, coverage determinations, prior-authorization rules, and MA plan marketing is broad; year-over-year executive-branch policy direction substantially affects beneficiary experience. The CY 2026 Part D Redesign Program Instructions issued by CMS on April 7, 2025 set the $2,100 OOP threshold and other Part D structural parameters for 2026 [Source 14]. Cross-references: D21 SD2 (Medicaid managed care under CMS oversight); D21 SD4 (CMS Conditions of Participation for hospitals).

*Department of Health and Human Services (HHS).* Cabinet-level department; CMS's parent agency. The Secretary of Health and Human Services is the statutory negotiator under the IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program (delegated to CMS for operational implementation). Address: 200 Independence Avenue, S.W., Washington, DC 20201. **Administrative vulnerability: HIGH** — Secretary-level policy direction sets CMS posture; recent Executive Orders (including EO 14273 on drug pricing, April 15, 2025) operate at this level.

*Office of Inspector General (OIG, HHS).* Independent oversight body; Medicare program integrity investigations; anchor healthcare institution False Claims Act enforcement; Stark Law and Anti-Kickback Statute civil monetary penalties. **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE.** Cross-references: SD4 (hospital institutional federal-enforcement engagement); D9 (anchor fiscal architecture intersections).

**State Statutory and Agency Layer.**

*Pennsylvania Insurance Department (PID) — Medicare Advantage and Medicare Supplement plan regulation.* PID regulates Medicare Advantage plans' state-licensing, marketing, and consumer-protection compliance under the PA Insurance Holding Companies Act (40 P.S. § 991.1401 et seq.) and the PA Unfair Insurance Practices Act (40 P.S. § 1171.1 et seq.); regulates Medigap underwriting and rate filings under 40 Pa. Code Chapter 89 (Medicare Supplement Insurance regulations); approved statewide Medigap rate adjustments effective July 1, 2025 [Source 9, framework reference]. **Statutory stability: HIGH.** **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE.** The Pennsylvania Insurance Commissioner is a gubernatorial appointee; consumer-protection enforcement intensity varies. Address: 1326 Strawberry Square, Harrisburg, PA 17120.

*Pennsylvania Department of Aging — PA MEDI Medicare Counseling Program (statewide SHIP).* Operates the federally-designated State Health Insurance Assistance Program (SHIP) for Pennsylvania under SHIP grant authority (Older Americans Act Title VII; CMS contract); brand name PA MEDI; central counseling line 1-800-783-7067 [Source 11]. **Statutory stability: HIGH.** SHIP statutory authority is durable; PA MEDI as the operating brand is administrative. **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE.** Annual SHIP grant funding levels and counselor capacity affect operational reach. Cross-references: D24 SD1 (parallel veteran-specific counseling under VBA architecture).

*Pennsylvania Medicare Supplement Insurance Act (40 Pa. Code Ch. 89).* Establishes Pennsylvania's Medigap consumer-protection framework operating in coordination with federal Medigap minimum standards (NAIC model law / 42 C.F.R. Part 403). **Pennsylvania does not have a state-level Medigap birthday rule** [Sources 9, 10]: outside the initial 6-month Medigap Open Enrollment Period that follows Medicare Part B effective date, Pennsylvania Medigap insurers may apply medical underwriting unless a federal guaranteed-issue trigger (Medicare Advantage plan termination, employer retiree coverage termination, etc.) applies. Proposed legislation (Co-Sponsorship Memo 47375, 2025-2026 Regular Session) would create a 60-day annual birthday-rule switching window for PA Medigap enrollees [Source 10]; the bill is not enacted as of May 2026. **Pennsylvania also prohibits Medicare excess charges** [Source 9]: providers in Pennsylvania may not charge above the Medicare-approved rate, irrespective of the federal 15% allowance for non-assignment providers. **Statutory stability: HIGH.** **Administrative vulnerability: LOW-MODERATE.**

*Livanta — Beneficiary and Family Centered Care Quality Improvement Organization (BFCC-QIO) for Pennsylvania.* CMS-contracted independent organization; handles first-level Medicare appeals for hospital-discharge timing, skilled nursing facility termination, home health agency termination, and outpatient observation status; receives quality-of-care complaints from Medicare beneficiaries [Source 15]. **Statutory stability: HIGH** (BFCC-QIO function is statutorily mandated under 42 C.F.R. Part 475). **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE** (contractor identity rotates on CMS contract cycles).

**Local Statutory and Agency Layer.**

*Philadelphia Corporation for Aging (PCA) — local APPRISE / PA MEDI counselor sponsor.* PCA is the Philadelphia Area Agency on Aging under the Older Americans Act (42 U.S.C. § 3001 et seq.); operates the local network of PA MEDI Medicare counselors who provide direct beneficiary navigation assistance for plan selection, claims questions, and dual-eligible coordination. Address: 642 N. Broad Street, Philadelphia, PA 19130. **Statutory stability: HIGH.** **Administrative vulnerability: MODERATE.** AAA capacity is grant-funded; counselor availability affects beneficiary outreach. Cross-references: D12 SD7 (elder-care interface); D24 SD1 (parallel veteran-specific counseling).

*Philadelphia Department of Public Health — Medicare-related preventive services interface.* Limited direct Medicare delivery role at the local-government layer; primary public-health role lives at D2; cross-references to D21 SD1 at the preventive-services interface (ACA § 2713 USPSTF preventive services applied through Medicare Part B — cross-reference D2 SD2 and D2 SD4 for substantive architecture).

**Constituent Layer.** The aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5 represent the range of PA-3 Medicare beneficiary experience: the dual-eligible Medicare-Medicaid recipient navigating Community HealthChoices and Medicare Advantage D-SNP architecture; the disabled-adult Medicare beneficiary completing the SSDI 24-month wait into Medicare entitlement; the Medicare-Advantage-enrolled beneficiary facing the MC54 substantive-and-structural Both/And. Placeholder reference here; Section 5 develops the profiles.


---

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

**The PA-3 Medicare Delivery Pathway: From Eligibility Through Plan Choice to Clinical Encounter**

The Medicare delivery pathway for a PA-3 constituent begins at the eligibility transition — typically the 65th birthday, the 25th month of Social Security Disability Insurance entitlement, or onset of End-Stage Renal Disease (ESRD) or Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS) diagnosis [Source 12]. D12 SD2 owns the eligibility and enrollment architecture per Boundary 1; D21 SD1 owns the delivery-side pathway that begins once enrollment is operative.

*Triggering condition.* A PA-3 constituent approaches Medicare entitlement under one of three documented pathways: (a) the age-65 pathway with a 7-month Initial Enrollment Period (IEP) starting 3 months before the 65th birthday month, including the birthday month, and ending 3 months after; (b) the disability pathway with automatic Medicare A/B entitlement at the 25th month of SSDI entitlement (ALS-eligible beneficiaries receive automatic enrollment at SSDI entitlement with no 24-month wait); (c) the ESRD pathway with a separate IEP architecture timed to dialysis initiation or kidney transplant [Source 12].

*First-stage decision: Original Medicare versus Medicare Advantage.* The PA-3 constituent at enrollment faces an initial structural choice between (a) Original Medicare (Parts A and B) supplemented by a stand-alone Part D prescription drug plan and optionally a Medicare Supplement (Medigap) policy; or (b) Medicare Advantage (Part C), most plans of which bundle Parts A, B, and D and may include supplemental benefits (dental, vision, hearing, transportation, OTC allowances). In Philadelphia County in 2026, the constituent chooses from approximately 66 non-SNP Medicare Advantage plans, with 42 plans available at $0 premium beyond the Part B premium [Source 5]; 19 Dual-Eligible Special Needs Plans (D-SNPs) for beneficiaries with both Medicare and Medicaid; and 7 Institutional Special Needs Plans (I-SNPs) for long-term care residents [Source 6]. The stand-alone Part D market in Pennsylvania offers 12 plans for 2026 with the lowest monthly premium at $6.60 [Source 4]. The choice is structurally consequential: Medicare Advantage enrollees are subject to plan networks, prior authorization, and (in Pennsylvania) the underwriting-required exit pathway back to Traditional Medicare with Medigap supplementation (analyzed under MC54 Both/And in §7).

*Medigap pathway architecture.* For the constituent choosing Original Medicare, the Medigap Open Enrollment Period is a one-time 6-month window starting the first month the beneficiary is 65 or older AND enrolled in Part B [Source 11; CMS framework]. During this window, Medigap insurers cannot apply medical underwriting. After the window closes, Pennsylvania's regulatory architecture permits insurers to require medical underwriting outside the limited set of federal guaranteed-issue triggers (Medicare Advantage plan termination; employer retiree coverage termination; etc.) [Source 9]. Pennsylvania does not have a state-level annual Medigap birthday rule; proposed legislation (Co-Sponsorship Memo 47375) would create such a rule but is not enacted as of May 2026 [Source 10].

*Annual reselection windows.* The Medicare Annual Enrollment Period (AEP) runs October 15 through December 7 each year; during AEP, beneficiaries can switch between Original Medicare and Medicare Advantage, switch among MA plans, or switch among stand-alone Part D plans. The Medicare Advantage Open Enrollment Period (MA OEP) runs January 1 through March 31 each year and permits a one-time MA-to-MA switch or MA-to-Original-Medicare switch (with conforming Part D enrollment). Special Enrollment Periods (SEPs) apply on qualifying events (move; loss of other coverage; gain or loss of Medicaid; 5-star plan SEP). For dual-eligible beneficiaries, the SEP architecture is more flexible: dual-eligibles may switch D-SNPs quarterly under integrated-care rules.

*Provider access and Medicare assignment.* Once enrolled, the constituent's clinical access depends on whether providers accept Medicare assignment (the agreement to accept Medicare-approved amounts as full payment). Pennsylvania's prohibition on Medicare excess charges [Source 9] means PA providers cannot charge above the Medicare-approved rate; however, providers may decline to accept Medicare entirely (opting out of the program), in which case the beneficiary pays the full charge. Provider Medicare participation rates in PA-3 specifically are not disaggregated in publicly retrievable data and are F-flagged for primary-source retrieval (F21-SD1-04).

*Dual-eligible coordination pathway.* For the PA-3 constituent with both Medicare and Medicaid, Pennsylvania's Community HealthChoices (CHC) is the Medicaid managed-care delivery vehicle (distinct from HealthChoices, which covers non-dual Medicaid for working-age adults and children) [Source 13]. The constituent navigates: Medicare enrollment + Part D OR a D-SNP that integrates Medicare and Medicaid coverage; AND a CHC Medicaid managed-care plan that handles Medicaid-side benefits (long-term services and supports; Medicare cost-sharing assistance). Coordination breakdowns occur when a provider accepts the Medicare-side coverage but not the secondary CHC plan; PA law requires the CHC plan to pay as secondary even when the provider is out of the CHC network, though providers retain discretion to refuse non-network patients absent EMTALA emergency obligations [Source 13]. Cross-reference D12 SD2 (Boundary 1; eligibility-side architecture); cross-reference D12 SD1 line 293 (Profile 2 disabled-adult coverage gap during disability determination).

*Pharmacy delivery and IRA implementation.* Part D pharmacy delivery operates through plan-formulary architecture: each Part D plan establishes a formulary, tier structure, prior-authorization rules, and pharmacy network. Effective January 1, 2026, all Part D plans must cover all 10 IRA-selected drugs at the negotiated Maximum Fair Price (MFP) under the IRA coverage requirement; KFF analysis confirms that all Part D enrollees in 2026 have coverage of all 10 selected drugs including all dosage forms and strengths [Source 2]. The pharmacy point-of-sale pathway: beneficiary presents prescription; pharmacy submits claim to Part D plan; plan applies tier-and-deductible logic; for IRA-negotiated drugs, the negotiated price (plus dispensing fee) becomes the plan-paid amount; beneficiary cost-sharing applies up to the $2,100 annual out-of-pocket cap (raised from $2,000 in 2025) [Source 14]. Once a beneficiary reaches the $2,100 OOP cap, all further Part D drug costs for the year are zero-cost-sharing — a structural protection that reshapes the pathway for high-cost-drug users.

*Decision points where the pathway breaks for PA-3 constituents.* The Medicare delivery pathway breaks down at several documented points: (a) at initial enrollment, where the structural complexity of the Original-Medicare-versus-MA choice combined with the one-time Medigap OEP creates a near-irreversible decision in Pennsylvania absent a federal guaranteed-issue trigger; (b) at the AEP, where the volume of plan choices (66 MA plans + 12 stand-alone Part D plans in 2026) and the complexity of comparing formulary tiers, network breadth, and supplemental benefits exceeds what most beneficiaries can navigate without counseling assistance — PA MEDI counselor capacity at 1-800-783-7067 and at the Philadelphia Corporation for Aging is the operational bottleneck [Sources 11, 12]; (c) at the post-MA-enrollment Medigap return-attempt, where Pennsylvania's underwriting requirement creates structural lock-in for beneficiaries whose health status has changed since the initial OEP; (d) at the dual-eligible coordination layer, where the Medicare-Medicaid-CHC navigation complexity falls disproportionately on low-income beneficiaries who lack the time, English-language proficiency, or cognitive capacity for self-navigation; (e) at Part D pharmacy delivery for non-formulary drugs requiring prior authorization or exception requests. The Beneficiary and Family Centered Care Quality Improvement Organization (BFCC-QIO) for Pennsylvania — Livanta — handles the first-level appeals when hospital, skilled-nursing-facility, or home-health-agency services are terminated by an MA plan [Source 15]; the appeals pathway is statutorily protected but operationally complex.

---

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**PA and Philadelphia County Medicare Enrollment Context (2026 plan year)**

Pennsylvania's total Medicare enrollment for the 2026 plan year is approximately 2,981,142 [Source 4; F21-SD1-01 for CMS primary-source confirmation]. Pennsylvania's population aged 65 or older is approximately 2.6 million, or about 20% of total state population per recent Census estimates [Source 16]. The PA Medicare-enrolled count exceeds the 65+ population because Medicare enrollment includes the disability-based and ESRD/ALS-based entitled population.

**Medicare Advantage market penetration.** Pennsylvania's statewide MA penetration was approximately 57% in 2024 per Kaiser Family Foundation analysis [Source 8]. Philadelphia County's MA penetration as calculated by Connie Health from CMS data is approximately 59.89% for 2026 [Source 7; F21-SD1-02 for CMS primary-source confirmation]. The Philadelphia County figure is approximately 3 percentage points above the PA statewide rate; structural inference is that urban concentration of MA plan marketing, dual-eligible D-SNP availability, and zero-premium plan presence (42 plans at $0 premium beyond Part B in Philadelphia County [Source 5]) contributes to higher penetration in dense urban markets.

**Pennsylvania has 334 Medicare Advantage plans available statewide in 2026** [Source 4]; **Philadelphia County has 66 non-SNP MA plans, 19 D-SNPs, and 7 I-SNPs** [Sources 5, 6]. The dominant non-SNP plan in Philadelphia County by enrollment is Keystone 65 Basic Rx (HMO) with 13,929 enrollees [Source 5]. The non-SNP MA enrollment in Philadelphia County is approximately 81,754; D-SNP enrollment is approximately 62,931; I-SNP enrollment is approximately 536 — aggregating to approximately 145,221 Philadelphia County beneficiaries in Medicare Advantage arrangements [Sources 5, 6; F21-SD1-03]. The 2026 average maximum out-of-pocket (MOOP) in-network for Philadelphia County MA plans is approximately $6,858, against the CMS 2026 in-network MA MOOP statutory maximum of $9,250 [Source 5]. The average monthly premium for plans charging a premium beyond Part B is approximately $35.26 [Source 7].

**Stand-alone Part D market.** Pennsylvania has 12 stand-alone Part D plans available in 2026; the lowest monthly premium is $6.60 [Source 4]. Approximately 22.21% of Pennsylvanians enrolled in stand-alone Part D plans receive Low-Income Subsidy (Extra Help) [Source 4; F21-SD1-05 for PA-3 specific LIS take-up rate]. The IRA Part D annual out-of-pocket cap is $2,100 in 2026, raised from $2,000 in 2025 [Source 14].

**Sub-area variation within PA-3 (the four-sub-area resolution per Standard 6).** PA-3's four sub-areas — North/Northwest Core; West Philadelphia Core; Northwest; South/Southwest — have substantially different demographic Medicare-eligibility profiles. PA-3-specific Medicare beneficiary counts disaggregated to sub-area resolution are not retrievable from publicly available CMS data products at SD1 execution (F21-SD1-06). Structural inference from prior-domain documented sub-area patterns: (a) the North/Northwest Core has documented higher rates of disability-based Medicare entitlement reflecting working-age disability prevalence linked to documented health disparities (D2 SD3 cross-reference; D6 SD4 cumulative-burden geography); (b) the West Philadelphia Core has documented older-population concentration in the longtime Black homeowner population (D7 SD1 Profile A cross-reference); (c) the Northwest sub-area has age-mix variation reflecting both established middle-class older population and lower-income disability-based entitled population; (d) the South/Southwest sub-area has documented immigrant population concentration that interacts with Medicare eligibility (the 5-year-residency requirement for premium-free Part A) and language-access pathway-breakdown points. The sub-area Medicare beneficiary distribution by Original-Medicare-versus-MA choice, dual-eligible status, and LIS take-up is not separately documented in CMS public data products at the four-sub-area resolution; the data limitation is stated explicitly per Standard 6 and flagged as F21-SD1-06 for institutional retrieval (CMS Office of Minority Health; CMS Chronic Conditions Data Warehouse) at Phase 3 verification attempt.
