## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

(Per Standard 13.A/B/C v2.2, profiles are aggregate composite illustrative pathways. Named individuals are prohibited at the constituent layer. Standard 13.C v2.2 carve-out for source-grounded officials in official capacity, case-name parties, and other source-grounded named individuals is preserved where it applies elsewhere in this SD; it does not apply at the constituent layer.)

**Profile 1: Dual-eligible Medicare beneficiary in the North/Northwest Core (aged 67; household income at or below 100% federal poverty level; recently transitioned from working-age Medicaid to Medicare-as-primary at age 65).**

*Profile type:* Composite illustrative pathway per Standard 13.

*Entry condition.* A North/Northwest Core PA-3 resident reached Medicare eligibility at age 65 having been enrolled in HealthChoices Medicaid (D12 SD2 territory) throughout late working-age. At age 65, Medicare became primary; the constituent's Medicaid eligibility continues but converted from HealthChoices to Community HealthChoices (CHC), Pennsylvania's Medicaid managed care vehicle for dual-eligibles [Source 13]. The constituent has multiple chronic conditions documented at the population level for the North/Northwest Core (D2 SD3 cross-reference) — including documented elevated hypertension and diabetes prevalence — and uses several prescription medications.

*Pathway through the institutional system.* At Medicare entitlement, the constituent received PA MEDI counseling assistance through a Philadelphia Corporation for Aging counselor [Source 11], which oriented the constituent to D-SNP options versus stand-alone Original Medicare with Medicare Savings Program assistance. The constituent enrolled in one of the 19 Philadelphia County D-SNPs [Source 6] for integrated Medicare-Medicaid coverage. The D-SNP covers Part A, Part B, Part D, and coordinates with the constituent's CHC Medicaid plan for Medicaid-side cost-sharing and long-term services. The constituent uses two of the IRA-negotiated drugs effective January 1, 2026, for diabetes and cardiovascular condition management; the IRA's coverage requirement [Source 2] means both medications are on the D-SNP formulary at the negotiated price.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance.* The constituent's pre-IRA 2025 out-of-pocket cost for prescription drugs reached the $2,000 Part D OOP cap; the 2026 OOP cap of $2,100 is approximately 5% higher, partly offset by the IRA-negotiated drug-price reductions on the specific medications used [Sources 14, 1]. Specific dollar magnitude of net OOP impact for this beneficiary in 2026 is not directly retrievable from PA-3-specific data and is structurally inferred from the national-aggregate $1.5B Part D beneficiary OOP savings estimate [Source 1; F21-SD1-07].

*Outcome.* The constituent receives integrated Medicare-Medicaid coverage through D-SNP architecture; clinical pathway is operational for the chronic conditions managed; pharmacy access is structurally protected by the IRA coverage requirement; cost-sharing exposure is capped at $2,100 OOP for Part D drugs. Pathway breakdown risk concentrates at the D-SNP-CHC provider-network mismatch (the documented mismatch pattern in [Source 13]).

**Profile 2: Disabled adult Medicare beneficiary in the West Philadelphia Core (aged 50; SSDI 24-month wait completed in 2025; entered Medicare January 2026).**

*Profile type:* Composite illustrative pathway per Standard 13; HOM guard-rail not applicable at SD1 (HOM territory concentrates at SD4 and SD5 per substructure). **Cross-reference D12 SD1 line 293 (Profile 2 disabled adult coverage gap during disability determination) and D12 SD1 line 452 (Profile 2 family-of-four actual care receipt).**

*Entry condition.* A West Philadelphia Core PA-3 resident completed the SSDI 24-month wait in 2025 and entered Medicare entitlement at the 25th month [Source 12]. The constituent had been enrolled in HealthChoices Medicaid during the 24-month wait period (after meeting Medicaid's disabled-adult eligibility criteria; D12 SD2 territory) following an earlier coverage gap during disability determination (D12 SD1 line 293). At Medicare entitlement, the constituent retained Medicaid as secondary coverage and transitioned to CHC.

*Pathway through the institutional system.* The constituent's pre-Medicare provider relationships (established under HealthChoices Medicaid) face continuity questions when Medicare becomes primary: providers must accept Medicare assignment to continue in-network status for the Medicare-primary period. Pennsylvania's excess-charge prohibition [Source 9] simplifies the cost calculus, but providers who do not accept Medicare entirely cannot continue billing under Medicare-primary architecture. The constituent's mental health and substance-use service providers (cross-reference D3 and D4 substantive architectures; D21 SD6 owns delivery-side architecture) face higher discontinuity risk because behavioral health provider Medicare participation rates are documented as systematically lower than primary-care participation. The constituent enrolled in a D-SNP at AEP-equivalent SEP for dual-eligibles; D-SNP integration partly mitigates the provider-continuity question by aligning Medicare and Medicaid networks.

*Illustrative figures with explicit data provenance.* PA-3-specific disability-based Medicare entitlement counts disaggregated from age-65-based counts are not retrievable from public CMS data at SD1 (F21-SD1-06); national structural reference is that approximately 14% of all Medicare beneficiaries are entitled on disability rather than age basis (Source citation requires F-flag; structural inference range from CMS/MedPAC historical data).

*Outcome.* The constituent receives Medicare-primary coverage with CHC Medicaid secondary; mental health provider continuity is partially operational subject to documented Medicare-behavioral-health-provider participation patterns; clinical pathway depends on D-SNP network architecture. The disability-based Medicare population's specific delivery-side pathway issues are partly D21 SD6 territory (behavioral health and SUD delivery) and partly D21 SD7 territory (specialty service-line cross-cutting delivery).

**Profile 3: Medicare Advantage choice navigation profile (MC54 Both/And; aged 66; middle-income retiree; not dual-eligible; enrolled in MA in 2025 at IEP; now considering plan change at AEP 2026).**

*Profile type:* Both/And profile illustrating MC54 substantive-and-structural cost-and-contribution-before-gap-conclusion architecture.

*Entry condition.* A South/Southwest sub-area PA-3 resident enrolled in Medicare Advantage at the IEP following the 65th birthday in 2025. The constituent selected an MA plan (one of the 66 Philadelphia County non-SNP plans [Source 5]) that included dental, vision, and over-the-counter benefit allowances not available under Original Medicare with Medigap.

*Substantive contribution (MC54 left side).* The MA plan delivered as designed: predictable monthly premium ($0 plan beyond the Part B premium; one of the 42 Philadelphia County $0-premium plans [Source 5]); supplemental dental and vision benefits the constituent used; transportation benefit to medical appointments; OTC allowance for over-the-counter medications and personal-care items. The MA plan's care-coordination architecture connected the constituent with a primary care provider in the plan network. The MA in-network MOOP cap at approximately $6,858 (average for Philadelphia County) [Source 5] provided a structural ceiling on out-of-pocket exposure that Original Medicare without Medigap does not provide.

*Structural displacement (MC54 right side).* Over the first year, the constituent encountered several MA structural features that did not surface at enrollment: (a) the plan's provider network restricted access to certain specialty providers used by family members and recommended for specific conditions (cross-reference D2 SD7 cancer care architecture); (b) prior authorization requirements delayed initiation of one therapy; (c) the plan's formulary did not include a specific medication the constituent had used pre-Medicare under employer coverage, requiring a transition to a formulary alternative. At AEP 2026 the constituent considered returning to Original Medicare with Medigap; the constituent learned at PA MEDI counseling [Source 11] that Pennsylvania's lack of state-level guaranteed-issue Medigap rules outside the initial 6-month OEP [Sources 9, 10] meant the Medigap return would require medical underwriting; given health-status changes during the MA year, the underwriting outcome was uncertain. The constituent's effective choice set at AEP 2026 narrowed to either (i) remaining in the current MA plan, (ii) switching to a different MA plan with potentially different network and formulary restrictions, or (iii) returning to Original Medicare without Medigap supplementation (accepting Part A and B cost-sharing exposure without supplemental coverage).

*MC54 Both/And status:* Confirmed at SD1 execution. The substantive MA choice architecture serves beneficiaries through documented mechanisms (supplemental benefits; care coordination; predictable cost-sharing). The structural displacement mechanism is documented (the one-way structural lock-in created by the interaction of MA enrollment-side flexibility and Medigap return-side underwriting requirement in states without guaranteed-issue protections like Pennsylvania). Both sides operate simultaneously; neither side erases the other.


---

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The Medicare delivery architecture is often described as a federal entitlement that offers simple, comprehensive coverage to anyone who reaches age 65 or qualifies through disability. The structure of the actual delivery experience in PA-3 does not match that description. At every transition point — initial enrollment, plan reselection at AEP, the dual-eligible coordination layer, and the post-Medicare-Advantage Medigap return attempt — the constituent encounters a structural complexity that the entitlement framing does not anticipate, and the consequences of that complexity fall most heavily on the constituents whose income, language proficiency, cognitive capacity, or social support network is least suited to navigating it.

The single most consequential structural feature of Medicare delivery for PA-3 constituents in Pennsylvania is the asymmetry between Medicare Advantage enrollment and Medicare Advantage exit. A 65-year-old beneficiary in 2026 can enter Medicare Advantage with one telephone call during their Initial Enrollment Period and need not answer a single health question. The same beneficiary, two years later, having developed a chronic condition or experienced a hospitalization, cannot reliably exit Medicare Advantage back to Traditional Medicare with Medigap supplementation in Pennsylvania without going through medical underwriting that may decline coverage or impose substantially higher premiums based on the health-status changes that occurred during the MA enrollment period. The structural lock-in is not a feature of Medicare Advantage itself; it is a feature of the interaction between federal MA enrollment rules and Pennsylvania's lack of a state-level guaranteed-issue Medigap protection — a structural feature shared with most states but addressed in approximately 17 states through annual birthday-rule or anniversary-rule protections that Pennsylvania has considered but not enacted [Source 10]. Federal House representation has no direct control over Pennsylvania's state Medigap underwriting rules, but federal representation can engage the federal-program-design dimension: the IRA improved the Part D out-of-pocket cap structure to $2,100 in 2026; federal policy could comparably address the MA-to-Medigap return architecture by establishing federal guaranteed-issue rights for Medigap return after a defined MA enrollment period.

The second feature worth naming explicitly is the IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program's delivery-side operationalization. The substantive architecture (drug selection, negotiation, maximum fair price establishment) is D2 SD4 territory; the delivery-side operationalization is D21 SD1 territory. For PA-3 beneficiaries using any of the 10 IRA-selected drugs in 2026, the structural change is concrete: the negotiated price reaches the pharmacy point of sale; the Part D plan covers all 10 selected drugs under the coverage requirement; the $2,100 annual out-of-pocket cap operates as a ceiling regardless of the specific drug used. Approximately 9 million Medicare enrollees nationally used these 10 drugs in 2023 [Source 3]; the PA-3 share is not separately documented but is structurally inferable from PA's approximately 6% share of national Medicare enrollment. The substantive consumer protection is real; the political contestation (industry litigation; Executive Order 14273 modification directives; 2025 reconciliation orphan-drug exclusion expansion) continues. Federal House representation has direct advocacy leverage at the appropriation, statutory-protection, and CMS-implementation-guidance layers.

The third feature is the federal-state-local pathway dependency. Original Medicare and Medicare Advantage are federal programs, but the PA-3 constituent's access to Medicare-participating providers, Medigap supplementation, dual-eligible coordination, and Part D pharmacy networks depends on Pennsylvania's regulatory architecture and Philadelphia's local navigation infrastructure. PA MEDI counselor capacity — at 1-800-783-7067 statewide, and at the Philadelphia Corporation for Aging locally — is the operational bottleneck for beneficiary navigation. The capacity is grant-funded under the federal SHIP authority; counselor-to-beneficiary ratios determine how much of the structural complexity each beneficiary is helped to navigate. Federal House representation can engage at the SHIP appropriation layer to expand counselor capacity for the PA-3 beneficiary population.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**<a id="g21-sd1-01"></a>G21-SD1-01: Medicare Advantage one-way structural lock-in absent Pennsylvania state guaranteed-issue Medigap rules (MC54 BOTH/AND PRIMARY).**
*Finding — substantive contribution:* Medicare Advantage in Philadelphia County delivers documented substantive benefits: 42 plans at $0 premium beyond Part B; supplemental dental, vision, hearing, transportation, and OTC benefits not available under Original Medicare; in-network MOOP cap averaging $6,858 against the 2026 statutory maximum of $9,250 [Source 5]; care coordination infrastructure; D-SNP integration for dual-eligibles. Approximately 59.89% of Philadelphia County Medicare beneficiaries are enrolled in MA arrangements [Source 7; F21-SD1-02 for CMS primary].
*Finding — structural lock-in:* Pennsylvania does not have a state-level annual Medigap birthday rule or anniversary rule [Sources 9, 10]. After the one-time 6-month Medigap Open Enrollment Period that follows initial Part B effective date, Pennsylvania Medigap insurers may apply medical underwriting outside the limited federal guaranteed-issue triggers (MA plan termination, employer coverage termination, etc.). The interaction between federal MA enrollment-side flexibility and Pennsylvania's underwriting-required exit-side creates a one-way structural lock-in: beneficiaries who enroll in MA at IEP cannot reliably return to Traditional Medicare with Medigap supplementation in subsequent years if their health status changes. Proposed PA legislation (Co-Sponsorship Memo 47375) would establish a 60-day birthday-rule window; the bill is not enacted as of May 2026 [Source 10].
*Evidence type:* [D] for both substantive contribution (CMS plan-availability documentation) and structural lock-in (PA regulatory documentation; co-sponsorship memo confirming PA Medigap rule status).
*Confidence:* HIGH.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation cannot directly intervene in Pennsylvania's state-level Medigap underwriting rules, but federal policy could address the asymmetry through federal guaranteed-issue Medigap return rights legislation. *T21-SD1-01: PA Medigap Birthday Rule legislation status — Co-Sponsorship Memo 47375 enactment trajectory; any 2026-2027 enactment will partially close the structural lock-in.*

**<a id="g21-sd1-02"></a>G21-SD1-02: IRA Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program delivery-side operational status (D21 owns delivery side; D2 SD4 owns substantive architecture).**
*Finding:* The first 10 IRA-negotiated drug prices became effective January 1, 2026 for Medicare Part D delivery [Source 1]. All Part D plans cover all 10 selected drugs at the negotiated Maximum Fair Price under the IRA coverage requirement [Source 2]. Estimated $1.5B annual beneficiary out-of-pocket savings and $6B annual Medicare program savings nationally [Source 1]; discounts range 38%-79% from 2023 list prices [Source 3]. Trump Executive Order 14273 (April 15, 2025) maintained the program while directing modification proposals [Source 14]; the 2025 budget reconciliation law broadened the orphan drug exclusion affecting 2028-and-beyond drug selection [Source 2]; 15 drugs selected for 2027 negotiated prices; 15 drugs selected for 2028 (first Part B inclusion).
*Evidence type:* [D] for program-level facts; [SD] for PA-3 share of delivery-side benefit (structural inference from PA's ~6% national Medicare share).
*Confidence:* HIGH for federal program facts; MEDIUM for PA-3 specific impact magnitude pending F21-SD1-07.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct advocacy on IRA appropriation, statutory protection, and CMS implementation guidance; the 2025 reconciliation's orphan-drug-exclusion expansion is a legislative-action trajectory item; CMS implementation guidance under Executive Order 14273 is administrative-engagement territory.

**<a id="g21-sd1-03"></a>G21-SD1-03: Provider Medicare assignment and participation gap.**
*Finding:* Pennsylvania prohibits Medicare excess charges [Source 9], simplifying the cost calculus for beneficiaries who see Medicare-participating providers. However, providers may opt out of Medicare entirely. PA-3-specific Medicare provider participation rates by specialty (primary care; behavioral health; specialty care) are not retrievable from publicly available data products at SD1 (F21-SD1-04). Documented national patterns establish behavioral health Medicare participation rates are systematically lower than primary-care rates; D21 SD6 owns the behavioral-health-delivery analysis.
*Evidence type:* [D] for PA excess-charge prohibition; [SI] for documented national behavioral-health-participation patterns applied to PA-3 by structural inference; [F] for PA-3-specific participation rates pending F21-SD1-04.
*Confidence:* HIGH for PA structural feature; MEDIUM for PA-3 specifics.
*Representation implication:* Federal CMS can address provider participation through Medicare physician fee schedule adjustments and value-based purchasing initiatives.

**<a id="g21-sd1-04"></a>G21-SD1-04: Dual-eligible coordination complexity at the Medicare-Medicaid-CHC architecture interface.**
*Finding:* Pennsylvania's approximately 19 Philadelphia County D-SNPs serve approximately 62,931 dual-eligible beneficiaries in Philadelphia County [Source 6; F21-SD1-03]; D-SNPs provide partial Medicare-Medicaid integration. For dual-eligibles not enrolled in D-SNPs, coordination across Medicare-primary (Original or non-SNP MA) and Community HealthChoices (CHC) Medicaid-secondary architecture requires navigation across separate plan networks, separate provider participation lists, and separate appeals pathways [Source 13]. The structural coordination burden falls disproportionately on low-income beneficiaries who lack the navigation capacity. Cross-reference D12 SD2 (eligibility-side; D12 owns); cross-reference D12 SD1 line 293 (Profile 2 disabled-adult coverage gap during disability determination).
*Evidence type:* [D] for documented coordination architecture; [SD] for documented coordination-burden distribution.
*Confidence:* HIGH for structural feature; MEDIUM for specific magnitude in PA-3.
*Representation implication:* Federal CMS has direct authority on D-SNP architecture, Medicare-Medicaid integration program design, and dual-eligible navigation support; federal House representation can engage at appropriation and CMS oversight layers.

**<a id="g21-sd1-05"></a>G21-SD1-05: Part D Low-Income Subsidy (Extra Help) take-up gap.**
*Finding:* Approximately 22.21% of Pennsylvania stand-alone Part D plan enrollees receive Low-Income Subsidy (Extra Help) [Source 4]. National literature (not directly retrieved at SD1; F21-SD1-05) documents an LIS take-up gap — eligible-but-not-enrolled populations consistently above 10% of the eligible pool. PA-3-specific LIS take-up rates by sub-area are not disaggregated in public data products; the sub-area limitation is stated explicitly per Standard 6.
*Evidence type:* [D] for PA-level take-up rate; [SI] for documented national LIS take-up gap pattern applied to PA-3.
*Confidence:* HIGH for documented PA rate; MEDIUM for PA-3 sub-area patterns.
*Representation implication:* Federal CMS and SSA share LIS administration responsibility; outreach intensity and procedural complexity affect take-up. Cross-reference D12 SD2 (eligibility-side architecture); D12 owns LIS architecture.

**<a id="g21-sd1-06"></a>G21-SD1-06: Medicare beneficiary navigation infrastructure capacity gap (PA MEDI / SHIP).**
*Finding:* PA MEDI is Pennsylvania's federally-designated SHIP operating under PA Department of Aging [Source 11]; central counseling line 1-800-783-7067; local capacity in Philadelphia delivered through Philadelphia Corporation for Aging. The SHIP grant funding from CMS sets statewide counselor capacity. PA-3 beneficiary-to-counselor ratio is not separately documented (F21-SD1-08); structural inference from documented SHIP capacity patterns is that PA MEDI provides counseling reach below what the structural complexity of plan choice warrants — the 66 MA plans + 12 stand-alone Part D plans + 10 Medigap lettered plans plus D-SNP and CHC coordination is a navigation problem that exceeds many beneficiaries' independent capacity.
*Evidence type:* [D] for SHIP architecture; [SI] for capacity-gap structural inference.
*Confidence:* HIGH for structural feature; MEDIUM for specific PA-3 capacity magnitude.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct advocacy on SHIP appropriation; appropriation increases can directly expand counselor capacity.


---

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**Element 1 — Formal provision.** Medicare delivery in PA-3 is formally provided through a federal entitlement architecture: Social Security Act Title XVIII establishes Medicare A, B, C, and D for the over-65 population, qualifying disabled adults (after the 24-month SSDI wait), ESRD-affected individuals, and ALS-affected individuals. The Inflation Reduction Act of 2022 adds the Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program with first negotiated prices for 10 Part D drugs effective January 1, 2026, and a Part D annual out-of-pocket cap of $2,100 in 2026. Pennsylvania's regulatory overlay covers Medicare Advantage plan licensing, Medigap underwriting and rate filings, excess-charge prohibition, and SHIP counseling architecture. The formal provision is comprehensive; every PA-3 Medicare-eligible resident has a federal-entitlement right to A and B and a structured market for C and D.

**Element 2 — Actual receipt.** Actual Medicare delivery receipt in PA-3 is structurally varied along multiple documented dimensions. Approximately 59.89% of Philadelphia County Medicare beneficiaries are enrolled in MA arrangements; approximately 40% remain in Original Medicare with or without Medigap supplementation; among MA enrollees, approximately 62,931 are dual-eligibles in D-SNPs [Sources 5, 6, 7]. Beneficiaries who enrolled in MA face the one-way structural lock-in created by the interaction between MA enrollment flexibility and Pennsylvania's underwriting-required Medigap return architecture. Beneficiaries who entered Medicare through the disability pathway (after the 24-month SSDI wait) face provider-continuity questions at the Medicare-Medicaid transition. Beneficiaries who require any of the 10 IRA-selected drugs in 2026 receive structural cost-protection through the coverage requirement and the $2,100 OOP cap. Dual-eligibles navigate Medicare-Medicaid-CHC coordination with variable success. The constituents who navigate Medicare delivery most successfully are those who have access to PA MEDI counseling, social support networks with experienced navigators, English-language proficiency, and continuous insurance histories that simplify the underwriting questions at Medigap return decisions.

**Element 3 — Accounting for the gap.** The gap between formal Medicare entitlement and actual delivery experience for PA-3 constituents is structural, not administrative. Five mechanisms account for it: (a) the MA-Medigap asymmetry under Pennsylvania's regulatory architecture (G21-SD1-01); (b) federal IRA implementation contestation at the political-administrative layer (G21-SD1-02); (c) provider Medicare participation variability across specialties (G21-SD1-03); (d) dual-eligible coordination complexity (G21-SD1-04); and (e) Low-Income Subsidy take-up gap (G21-SD1-05). Each mechanism operates independently; the cumulative effect on a low-income, language-limited, or cognitively-burdened beneficiary is substantially greater than the sum of the parts. SHIP counselor capacity (G21-SD1-06) is the operational variable that determines how much of the structural complexity each beneficiary is helped to navigate.

**Element 4 — Representation implication.** Federal House representation has direct advocacy leverage on Medicare appropriations, CMS regulatory posture, IRA drug-pricing statutory protections, SHIP appropriation, and Medicare Advantage plan oversight legislation. The state-level Medigap underwriting question is outside direct federal House control but is addressable through federal guaranteed-issue Medigap return legislation. The dual-eligible architecture is jointly federal-state but federal CMS has direct authority on Medicare-side coordination program design. Medicare is the project domain where federal advocacy maps most directly to PA-3 beneficiary experience — among the most direct mappings in the project's representation framework.

# D21 Healthcare Delivery — Sub-Domain 2
## Medicaid Delivery Architecture

**Date:** 2026-05-11
**Phase:** 1 (Per-SD Execution; fresh cycle)
**Domain:** 21 — Healthcare Delivery
**Sub-Domain:** 2 of 7
**Cycle position:** Phase 1 SD2-N batch execution; SD1 approved 2026-05-11 (Option A cap-as-guideline; MC54 PRIMARY Medicare Advantage Both/And operationalized)
**Methodology stack:** M0 v2.2 · M2 v1.2.1 · Skills v3
**Status:** Phase 1 draft — SD2 of SD2-N batch

---

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD2 documents the Medicaid delivery architecture governing the Medicaid-enrolled population in PA-3: the federal entitlement framework under Social Security Act Title XIX that establishes federal Medicaid floor design plus state-administrative flexibility; the federal regulatory framework that conditions managed care plan operations (42 C.F.R. Part 438), provider participation, and dual-eligible coordination; the Pennsylvania administrative architecture (PA Department of Human Services Office of Medical Assistance Programs; Office of Long-Term Living; PA Insurance Department) that operates HealthChoices Physical Health (PH-MCO) and Community HealthChoices (CHC-MCO) as the two principal managed-care delivery vehicles for Medicaid in the Southeast Zone; the Children's Health Insurance Program (CHIP) delivery architecture; and the One Big Beautiful Bill Act of 2025 (OBBBA; P.L. 119-21) Medicaid restructuring whose delivery-side flow-in begins October 1, 2026 (provider-tax freeze) and continues through 2027 (6-month redeterminations; work requirements; retroactive-coverage reduction; cost-sharing) with safe-harbor stepdown phasing from FY2028 through FY2032. The sub-domain analyzes Medicaid delivery as a clinical-and-managed-care-operations architecture rather than as a coverage-eligibility architecture. **Boundary 1** (per D21 substructure §2): D12 SD2 owns Medicaid eligibility, enrollment, OBBBA procedural-loss architecture, and Low-Income Subsidy / Medicare Savings Program eligibility; D21 SD2 owns clinical delivery, managed-care operations, provider network architecture, and OBBBA delivery-side flow-through. Behavioral health Medicaid delivery via Community Behavioral Health Philadelphia (CBH) BH-MCO is carved out to SD6 per D21 substructure §1.

**Regime declaration.** Internet-access regime. Current-state HealthChoices Physical Health managed-care plan rosters (Southeast Zone PH-MCOs; CHC-MCOs), CHC re-procurement implementation status, OBBBA implementation timeline and CMS guidance posture, PA Medicaid enrollment figures, and Pennsylvania-specific provider-tax architecture status are expected to be retrieved; specific figures carry sources per Standard 12. The Social Security Act Title XIX framework, 42 C.F.R. Part 438 managed-care regulatory architecture, CHIP Title XXI framework, and PA Medical Assistance regulatory architecture at 55 Pa. Code Ch. 1101 et seq. are framework-level statutory references; specific section citations are sourced where claims are specific rather than framework-level. Mixed-regime drafting is prohibited per Standard 1; this SD operates entirely under internet-access regime.

**Analytical posture.** Both/And-primary at MC60 (Medicaid delivery — provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown delivery-side flow-through). Per D21 substructure §6, MC60 is the within-domain analytical-tension flagged at SD2: substantive Medicaid delivery architecture serves beneficiaries through federal-floor design, MCO care coordination, CHC long-term services and supports, and CHIP coverage continuity, AND structural fiscal pressure on safety-net institutions develops from the OBBBA Section 71115 / 71117 / 71119 provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown architecture (delivery-side flow-through that compounds with Medicaid procedural-loss documented at D12 SD2/SD8). MC60 Both/And designation operative through Phase 1 SD2 drafting; substantive Medicaid delivery continues at federal-floor + state-administrative-overlay architecture AND structural fiscal-vulnerability mechanisms compound on the institutional side documented at SD5 (FQHC/safety-net) and SD4 (hospital institutional). No held-open-magnitude territory at SD2 (the substructure's HOM candidates concentrate at SD4 anchor community benefit and SD5 safety-net fiscal vulnerability).

**Thread declarations.** Three analytical threads operative at SD2:

- **D21-SD2-Thread A (federal-floor + state-administrative-overlay architecture).** Medicaid Title XIX is a federal entitlement with statutory stability HIGH at the program level; provider participation, managed-care plan operations, and beneficiary clinical experience operate under federal 42 C.F.R. Part 438 framework with Pennsylvania Department of Human Services Office of Medical Assistance Programs (OMAP) administrative architecture overlaying program operations across the five HealthChoices Physical Health zones. The federal-state interface is asymmetric: federal authority dominates at program-design structural features (eligibility categories; mandatory and optional benefits; federal medical assistance percentage); state authority dominates at managed-care contracting, provider network adequacy, and rate-setting layers. Pennsylvania operates HealthChoices PH (mandatory for under-21 and non-LTSS adult Medicaid) and Community HealthChoices CHC (mandatory for dual-eligibles and LTSS-eligible adults) as two parallel managed-care architectures with overlapping enrollee populations [Source 17].

- **D21-SD2-Thread B (OBBBA Section 71115 / 71117 / 71119 / 71107 delivery-side flow-through architecture).** OBBBA's Medicaid provisions affect delivery architecture in PA through four principal mechanisms operative October 2026 through January 2027 and beyond: (a) Section 71115 provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown from 6.0% to 3.5% over FY2028-FY2032 (expansion-state phase-down) plus federal effective freeze on existing provider-tax rates beginning October 1, 2026 [Sources 21, 22, 24]; (b) Section 71117 MCO-tax tightening (taxes on MCOs cannot be higher for Medicaid MCOs than commercial MCOs effective July 4, 2025; three-year CMS transition period available) [Source 30]; (c) Section 71119 community-engagement work requirements (80 hours/month for expansion-state able-bodied adults effective January 1, 2027) [Source 22]; (d) Section 71107 procedural-loss architecture (6-month eligibility redeterminations effective December 2026; retroactive coverage reduced to 60 days traditional / 30 days expansion effective January 1, 2027) [Sources 22, 25]. CBO estimates 9.1 million Medicaid recipients impacted by the provider-tax provisions by FY2034 [Source 27]. Pennsylvania is a Medicaid expansion state and is affected by all four mechanisms. The provider-tax architecture is D9 SD7 federal/state tax burden distribution territory at the structural level; D21 SD2 owns delivery-side flow-through.

- **D21-SD2-Thread C (D12 boundary architecture maintained at delivery side).** D12 SD2 (verified 2026-05-01) owns Medicaid eligibility, enrollment, and OBBBA procedural-loss architecture [Source 18]; D12 SD8 owns OBBBA federal-policy-cycle architecture comprehensive analysis; D12 SD1 line 293 documents Profile 2 (disabled-adult coverage gap during disability determination); D12 SD1 line 452 documents Profile 2 family-of-four actual care receipt analysis. D21 SD2 cross-references the D12 architecture at points of operational interaction (where eligibility architecture and delivery architecture interact at the beneficiary interface) rather than re-extending the D12 analysis. The D21 SD1 cross-reference convention (Standard 9 specificity at G21-SD1-{nn} stable IDs) applies bidirectionally between D21 SDs.

---

## Section 2: Authority Chain

The Medicaid delivery authority chain in PA-3 follows the federal-state-local layered architecture (DOMAIN_FRAMEWORK Cross-Cutting Thread 3) with substantial state-administrative discretion at the managed-care contracting and operations layer.

**Constitutional Foundation.** U.S. Constitution Article I, Section 8 (General Welfare Clause) authorizes congressional spending on the Medicaid grant-in-aid architecture; Tenth Amendment reserves state administrative discretion at program operations subject to federal conditions. No constitutional tension specific to Medicaid delivery is operative at PA-3 in 2026 (the constitutionality of the Medicaid program is settled; the *NFIB v. Sebelius* 2012 ruling preserved state discretion on ACA Medicaid expansion, which Pennsylvania accepted in 2015).

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

- **Social Security Act Title XIX (42 U.S.C. § 1396 et seq.).** Authorizes the federal Medicaid grant-in-aid program; sets mandatory eligibility categories, mandatory benefits, federal medical assistance percentage (FMAP) architecture, and provider-tax framework. *Statutory stability HIGH* at program level; *administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH* at federal regulatory implementation given the 2025 OBBBA reconciliation modifications and the Trump administration's CMS regulatory posture per Source 14 (EO 14273 architecture pattern from SD1 Source 14).

- **Children's Health Insurance Program Title XXI (42 U.S.C. § 1397aa et seq.).** Authorizes CHIP; Pennsylvania operates a separate CHIP program (PA CHIP) for children up to 314% FPL; delivery-side architecture overlaps with HealthChoices managed-care infrastructure. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **One Big Beautiful Bill Act of 2025 (P.L. 119-21).** Signed July 4, 2025 [Source 21]. Sections 71107, 71109, 71115, 71117, 71119 directly affect Medicaid delivery architecture. Section 71115 establishes the expansion-state provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown from 6.0% to 5.5% beginning FY2028 and to 3.5% by FY2032 [Source 27]. Section 71117 establishes the MCO-tax tightening (Medicaid MCOs cannot be taxed at higher rates than commercial MCOs) [Source 30]. Section 71119 establishes community-engagement work requirements [Source 22]. Section 71107 establishes 6-month eligibility redeterminations effective December 2026 [Source 25]. *Statutory stability HIGH at enactment (reconciliation-passed; difficult to amend); administrative vulnerability HIGH at CMS implementation given the November 2025 CMS preliminary guidance posture and ongoing rulemaking* [Source 23]. Cross-reference D12 SD8 for OBBBA federal-policy-cycle comprehensive analysis; D9 SD7 for federal/state tax burden distribution; D9 SD4 for provider-tax structural architecture at fiscal-anchor analysis.

- **42 C.F.R. Part 438 (Medicaid Managed Care).** CMS-administered federal regulatory framework governing all Medicaid managed-care contracts; network adequacy; actuarial soundness rate-setting; enrollee protections; appeals architecture; medical loss ratio requirements. *Statutory stability MODERATE-HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH at CMS rulemaking posture (the 2024 Final Rule streamlining Medicaid/CHIP eligibility was placed on moratorium by OBBBA Section 71101 through September 30, 2034)* [Source 30].

- **Section 1115 demonstration waivers and Section 1915(b)/(c) waivers.** Pennsylvania operates CHC under concurrent 1915(b) (mandatory managed care) and 1915(c) (home and community-based services) waivers [Source 7]; Pennsylvania does not currently operate a Section 1115 demonstration affecting general Medicaid managed care. *Statutory stability HIGH at waiver authority; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at CMS approval renewal cycles*.

**Federal Agency Layer.**

- **Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS).** Mailing address: 7500 Security Boulevard, Baltimore, MD 21244-1850. CMS administers federal Medicaid oversight; approves state plans; reviews managed-care contracts under 42 C.F.R. Part 438.812; issues guidance on OBBBA implementation including the November 2025 preliminary guidance on provider-tax restrictions [Source 23]. CMS Region 3 (Philadelphia) is the regional office covering Pennsylvania: 801 Market Street, Suite 9400, Philadelphia, PA 19107. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH* — the Trump administration's CMS regulatory posture on Medicaid managed care, work requirements, and provider-tax implementation is the dominant administrative-vulnerability variable for SD2 delivery architecture; CMS rulemaking through 2026-2027 will determine the precise contour of OBBBA delivery-side effects.

- **U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS).** 200 Independence Avenue, S.W., Washington, D.C. 20201. Parent agency to CMS; sets Medicaid policy direction at the Secretarial level. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH*.

- **HHS Office of Inspector General (OIG).** Audits Medicaid program integrity; managed-care fraud and abuse oversight. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

**State Statutory Layer.**

- **Pennsylvania Public Welfare Code (62 P.S. § 101 et seq.).** Establishes PA Medical Assistance; authorizes PA DHS to operate the Medicaid program; authorizes provider-tax architecture (currently 6% on hospital net inpatient revenue; expected to be affected by OBBBA stepdown) [Sources 21, 28]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at PA General Assembly biennial budget cycle*.

- **55 Pa. Code Ch. 1101 et seq. (PA Medical Assistance regulations).** Implements 62 P.S. through PA DHS regulations; sets provider participation requirements; managed-care contracting standards; provider rate-setting methodologies. *Statutory stability MODERATE; administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH at PA DHS rulemaking under OBBBA-driven adjustments*.

- **PA Act 22 of 2011 ("Welfare Code amendments").** Codified the Pennsylvania HealthChoices managed-care mandatory enrollment framework. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW*.

**State Agency Layer.**

- **Pennsylvania Department of Human Services (DHS), Office of Medical Assistance Programs (OMAP).** 625 Forster Street, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Administers HealthChoices Physical Health program; contracts with PH-MCOs by zone; reviews managed-care actuarial rates; oversees provider participation [Source 19]. *per Standard 13.C carve-out: PA DHS Secretary identification in official capacity is source-grounded where appearing in DHS communications; PAMCO June 2025 communication notes "DHS will keep Medicaid recipients updated about their benefits" [Source 20] but does not name the Secretary in the retrieved excerpt; named-leadership citations deferred to source-grounded surfacing in execution.* *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH* — PA DHS implementation discretion is the principal state-level variable affecting OBBBA delivery-side flow-through.

- **PA DHS Office of Long-Term Living (OLTL).** Administers Community HealthChoices for dual-eligibles and LTSS-eligible adults across all five zones [Source 13]. Oversees the three statewide CHC-MCOs (AmeriHealth Caritas / Keystone First; PA Health & Wellness; UPMC CHC) and the August 2024 re-procurement adding Aetna Better Health and Health Partners Plans (readiness review pending) [Source 18]. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **Pennsylvania Insurance Department.** 1326 Strawberry Square, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Licenses Medicaid managed-care plans operating in Pennsylvania; reviews market-conduct examinations; coordinates with PA DHS on managed-care plan financial solvency. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **PA Independent Enrollment Broker (IEB).** 1-844-824-3655. Contracted with PA DHS to provide choice counseling and assistance with eligibility and enrollment for individuals seeking LTSS services [Source 7]; operates the enrollchc.com enrollment website; assigns default MCOs where the beneficiary does not select. *Administrative vulnerability LOW*.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

- **Philadelphia Code (Title 6 — Health).** Authorizes the Philadelphia Department of Public Health and Philadelphia Department of Behavioral Health and Intellectual disAbility Services (DBHIDS) operational architecture. The Philadelphia behavioral-health-Medicaid carve-out is administered through CBH per Philadelphia Code architecture and HealthChoices BH carve-out contracts (cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive analysis). *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW*.

**Local Agency Layer.**

- **Philadelphia Department of Public Health (PDPH).** 1101 Market Street, Philadelphia, PA 19107. Operates the Philadelphia health center system delivering safety-net primary and preventive care to Medicaid beneficiaries and uninsured populations (cross-reference D21 SD5 for substantive analysis). Provides public-health functions independent of Medicaid managed-care delivery architecture. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **Community Behavioral Health Philadelphia (CBH).** 801 Market Street, Suite 7000, Philadelphia, PA 19107. County-managed BH-MCO administering the Medicaid HealthChoices behavioral-health carve-out for Philadelphia County residents; the structural distinctiveness is documented in D12 verified file line 111 (cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive analysis). CBH is the BH delivery vehicle for all HealthChoices PH-MCO enrollees in Philadelphia regardless of physical-health plan selection [Source 13]. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **Philadelphia County Assistance Office (CAO).** Multiple locations (e.g., 1410 W. Erie Avenue, Philadelphia, PA 19140; 1701 S. Broad Street, Philadelphia, PA 19148). Implements PA DHS-administered Medicaid eligibility determinations at the county level (D12 boundary territory) and serves as the operational interface for redeterminations including the OBBBA 6-month redetermination architecture beginning December 2026 [Source 25]. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

**Constituent Layer.** Aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5: PA-3 Medicaid-enrolled household navigating HealthChoices PH-MCO selection; PA-3 dual-eligible navigating CHC plan selection with Medicare coordination; PA-3 CHIP-enrolled household navigating annual recertification and OBBBA procedural-loss exposure; PA-3 PH-MCO enrollee navigating BH carve-out coordination through CBH (cross-reference SD6).

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

The Medicaid delivery pathway for a PA-3 constituent begins at the eligibility transition point owned by D12 SD2 — typically an application at COMPASS (online at compass.state.pa.us; by phone at 1-866-550-4355) or at the local Philadelphia County Assistance Office [Source 35]. D12 SD2 owns the eligibility and enrollment architecture per Boundary 1; D21 SD2 owns the delivery-side pathway that begins once enrollment is operative.

*Triggering condition.* A PA-3 constituent enters Medicaid delivery architecture under one of four documented pathways: (a) the categorical-eligibility pathway (Modified Adjusted Gross Income / MAGI categories — pregnant women up to 220% FPL; children 0-1 to 220% FPL; older children to 160% FPL plus the CHIP-overlap up to 319% FPL; parents/caretaker relatives at lower threshold; Group VIII expansion adults to 138% FPL since January 2015) [Source 34]; (b) the disability-based pathway (Modified Adjusted Gross Income exempt for SSI-related categories; medical eligibility determination required); (c) the dual-eligible pathway (Medicare beneficiaries who also qualify for Medicaid; LIS/MSP eligibility via D12 SD2; clinical-delivery handoff to CHC managed care); (d) the LTSS-eligible pathway (medical need for nursing-facility level of care; clinical determination through the PA Independent Enrollment Broker at 1-877-550-4227 via an in-home Functional Eligibility Determination) [Source 35].

*First-stage decision: PH-MCO selection (HealthChoices) versus CHC-MCO selection.* The PA-3 constituent at enrollment is routed by category to one of two managed-care architectures. For categorical-eligibility adults and children (HealthChoices Physical Health), the constituent selects from four Southeast Zone PH-MCOs — Aetna Better Health, Health Partners Plans, Keystone First (operated by AmeriHealth Caritas), and UnitedHealthcare Community Plan [Source 15] — through PA Enrollment Services at 1-800-440-3989 (TTY 1-800-618-4225) or at enrollnow.net [Source 10]. If the constituent does not select, PA DHS auto-assigns a plan. The selection is structurally consequential: each MCO operates a separate provider network, specialty care authorization architecture, prescription drug formulary, and care management infrastructure [Source 18]. For dual-eligibles and LTSS-eligible adults age 21+ (Community HealthChoices), the constituent selects from three statewide CHC-MCOs — AmeriHealth Caritas (Keystone First brand in Southeast Zone), PA Health & Wellness, and UPMC Community HealthChoices — through the Independent Enrollment Broker at 1-844-824-3655 or at enrollchc.com [Sources 13, 14]. The August 2024 CHC re-procurement added Aetna Better Health and Health Partners Plans (Vista Health Plan / AmeriHealth Caritas remains the SE Zone brand) to a five-plan statewide CHC architecture; readiness review and contract execution were pending as of late 2025 [Sources 16, 20].

*Behavioral-health-carve-out auto-enrollment to CBH.* All HealthChoices PH-MCO enrollees in Philadelphia County are automatically enrolled in Community Behavioral Health Philadelphia (CBH) for behavioral-health and SUD coverage regardless of PH-MCO selection [Source 13]. The constituent has no choice in BH-MCO; the county selects the BH plan as a structural feature of the HealthChoices BH carve-out. Cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive analysis of CBH architecture and the structural distinctiveness documented at D12 verified file line 111.

*CHIP delivery pathway.* For PA-3 children whose households are above Medicaid eligibility (160% FPL for school-aged children; 220% for age 0-1) but below CHIP eligibility (up to 314% FPL per the federal threshold; PA operates up to 319% with built-in 5% disregard) [Source 34], the CHIP delivery pathway operates through CHIP-contracted MCOs. CHIP renewal architecture is annual (vs. the OBBBA-modified 6-month Medicaid redetermination architecture taking effect December 2026 per Section 71107) [Source 25]. CHIP enrollment statewide was 195,320 as of August 2024 [Source 39].

*Annual reselection and renewal windows.* HealthChoices enrollees may change PH-MCO at any time (Pennsylvania's continuous open-enrollment architecture per PHLP) [Source 10]. Annual renewal of Medicaid eligibility was historically a single annual redetermination through the Continuous Coverage Requirement provisions; the OBBBA Section 71107 architecture reduces this to 6-month redeterminations effective December 2026 [Source 25]. Cross-reference D12 SD2 (eligibility-side architecture) and D12 SD8 (G12-SD8-07 OBBBA procedural-loss territory) for the structural analysis of the redetermination architecture; D21 SD2 documents the delivery-side flow-through impact at managed-care plan continuity.

*Provider participation and Medicaid network access.* Once enrolled, the constituent's clinical access depends on which providers participate in the selected PH-MCO or CHC-MCO. Provider participation varies substantially by specialty: primary care has highest participation; behavioral health, specialty surgical care, and some dental care have systematically lower participation per documented national patterns (PA-3-specific participation rates by specialty are F-flagged for retrieval; F21-SD2-01). Penn Medicine accepts all four SE Zone PH-MCOs plus all three current CHC-MCOs [Source 19]; Temple Health, Jefferson Health, and CHOP participate broadly across HealthChoices and CHC architectures (cross-reference D21 SD4 for hospital institutional architecture analysis).

*Dual-eligible coordination pathway.* For the PA-3 constituent with both Medicare and Medicaid, Community HealthChoices is the Medicaid-side delivery vehicle and integrates with Medicare-side coverage [Source 37]. CHC covers Medicare premiums, deductibles, and copays plus the Medicaid-only benefits (LTSS; non-Medicare-covered services) [Source 37]. The constituent navigates: Medicare enrollment + Part D (or a D-SNP that integrates Medicare and Medicaid coverage — cross-reference D21 SD1 G21-SD1-04); AND a CHC-MCO that handles Medicaid-side benefits. CHC service coordination is a structural protection: the assigned MCO assigns a service coordinator who creates a person-centered care plan [Source 37]. Coordination breakdowns occur at provider-network misalignment (a provider accepts Medicare but not the CHC plan; or the converse) and at the Medicare-CHC coordination of cost-sharing.

*LTSS service-delivery pathway under CHC.* CHC covers personal care assistance, adult day services, home health services, home modifications, meal delivery, personal emergency response systems, and skilled nursing facility care [Source 37]. The "Services My Way" self-direction option permits participants to hire their own caregivers including some adult family members (spouses and legal guardians excluded) [Source 37]. Enrollment slots in some HCBS waivers are limited and waitlists may form [Source 37]; the LIFE/PACE program (Pennsylvania's PACE program) is a related architecture providing integrated Medicare-Medicaid coverage through a single PACE provider. CHC HCBS-receiving population was 34% of the 383,000+ enrolled as of June 2024 [Source 20]; nursing-facility-resident population was 11% [Source 20].

*OBBBA delivery-side flow-through.* The OBBBA Medicaid architecture flows through delivery in four sequenced disruptions: (a) October 1, 2026: provider-tax freeze takes effect; Pennsylvania's hospital provider tax (a principal Medicaid financing mechanism) is frozen at the rate in place as of July 4, 2025 [Source 23]; the freeze begins the stepdown to 3.5% by FY2032 [Sources 24, 27]; cross-reference D9 SD4 for provider-tax structural architecture analysis. (b) December 2026: 6-month eligibility redeterminations begin for expansion-state enrollees [Source 25]; delivery-side impact is procedural-loss disruption to continuity of care, cross-reference D12 SD2/SD8 for eligibility-side architecture. (c) January 1, 2027: retroactive coverage reduced from 90 days to 60 days for traditional enrollees and 30 days for expansion enrollees [Source 22]; delivery-side impact is provider risk on services rendered before eligibility verification, which structurally pushes providers toward greater service-delay-pending-verification posture. (d) January 1, 2027: 80 hours/month community-engagement work requirements for expansion-state able-bodied adults [Source 22]; delivery-side impact is coverage instability for the working-poor population among PH-MCO enrollees; (e) FY2028-FY2032 phased stepdown of safe-harbor threshold to 3.5% [Source 27]; delivery-side impact is sustained downward pressure on hospital and managed-care payment rates that compounds with the upstream coverage-loss impact (the analytical core of the MC60 Both/And designation operationalized at this SD).

*Decision points where the pathway breaks for PA-3 constituents.* The Medicaid delivery pathway breaks down at several documented points: (a) at initial enrollment, where the PH-MCO selection complexity (four plans; separate provider networks; separate formularies) exceeds what many beneficiaries navigate without assistance; (b) at the BH-PH care-coordination interface, where the structural carve-out to CBH means physical-health and behavioral-health authorizations operate on separate authorization tracks (cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive analysis); (c) at the annual redetermination (transitioning to 6-month redetermination December 2026), where procedural-loss is documented at high magnitude — particularly affecting limited-English-proficiency households, households facing housing instability, and households whose income fluctuates around the Medicaid threshold (cross-reference D12 SD2 / SD8 for structural analysis); (d) at the dual-eligible coordination layer, where Medicare-CHC integration breakdowns fall disproportionately on lower-income beneficiaries who lack the navigation capacity; (e) at the provider-network adequacy layer, where structurally lower behavioral-health and specialty participation rates in Medicaid networks (compared to commercial networks) create access bottlenecks; (f) at the LTSS application layer, where the Functional Eligibility Determination wait combined with HCBS waiver waitlist capacity creates documented delays in service initiation. The Pennsylvania Health Law Project (PHLP) operates as the principal legal-services backstop for HealthChoices enrollees experiencing pathway breakdowns [Source 10].

---


## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**PA and Philadelphia County Medicaid Enrollment Context (2026 plan year)**

Pennsylvania's total Medicaid enrollment is approximately 2.99 million per the KFF May 2025 fact sheet [Source 36] and PA DHS's "approximately 3 million people" framing [Source 32]. The HealthChoices managed care program enrolls "over 2.2 million" of these per PHLP [Source 10]; CHC enrolled 383,000+ as of June 2024 [Source 20]; remaining enrollment is in CHIP (195,320 as of August 2024) [Source 39] and in fee-for-service (FFS) for populations not yet enrolled in managed care or in transitional eligibility status. Medicaid Expansion (Group VIII) covered approximately 1.1 million by 2023 and approximately 750,000 in steady-state per PA DHS [Source 32].

**Philadelphia County share.** Approximately 22% of statewide Medicaid enrollment is in Philadelphia County per the Pennsylvania Office of Rural Health August 2024 data [Source 39]. Applied to the 2.99 million statewide total, the Philadelphia County Medicaid-enrolled population is approximately 658,000. Of this, structural inference from the statewide 58% adult / 42% child split [Source 39] is that Philadelphia County Medicaid includes approximately 382,000 adults and 276,000 children. PA-3-specific disaggregation (the specific Congressional District boundary within Philadelphia County) is not available in publicly retrievable Medicaid data products at SD2; PA-3-specific Medicaid enrollment is F-flagged for institutional retrieval (F21-SD2-02).

**PA-3 sub-area variation.** Sub-area-disaggregated Medicaid enrollment at the four-sub-area level (North/Northwest Core; West Philadelphia Core; Northwest; South/Southwest) is not retrievable from public-facing Medicaid data products. Per Standard 6 (geographic specificity), the limitation is stated explicitly. Structural inference from documented Philadelphia poverty-rate-by-tract patterns (ACS-derived; cross-reference D12 SD2 and D13 SD2/SD4/SD6 for cumulative-burden geography) supports that North/Northwest Core and West Philadelphia Core have higher Medicaid enrollment concentration relative to the South/Southwest and Northwest sub-areas, consistent with documented poverty-concentration patterns [Source 39 + structural inference]. PA-3-specific sub-area Medicaid enrollment magnitudes are F-flagged for institutional retrieval (F21-SD2-03).

**CHC enrollment in PA-3.** Statewide CHC enrollment was 383,000+ as of June 2024, with three statewide CHC-MCOs (Keystone First / AmeriHealth Caritas; PA Health & Wellness; UPMC CHC) [Sources 16, 20]. CHC enrolled population: 34% receiving HCBS, 11% receiving care in a nursing facility [Source 20]. PA-3-specific CHC enrollment is not separately disaggregated; structural inference from Philadelphia County's high proportion of dual-eligible beneficiaries (cross-reference D21 SD1 Source 6: 62,931 Philadelphia County D-SNP enrollees) supports that PA-3 has substantial CHC penetration. PA-3-specific CHC enrollment is F-flagged (F21-SD2-04).

**CHIP enrollment in PA-3.** Pennsylvania CHIP statewide enrollment was 195,320 as of August 2024 [Source 39]. Philadelphia County's CHIP share would be approximately 22% applied to statewide if the geographic distribution matches Medicaid (~43,000 children); the actual share may differ given the household-income-distribution differences between CHIP-eligible (160-319% FPL) and Medicaid-eligible (under 160% FPL) populations [Source 34]. PA-3-specific CHIP enrollment is F-flagged (F21-SD2-05).

**OBBBA-affected population estimates.** The CBO estimates that the OBBBA provider-tax provisions will impact at least 9.1 million Medicaid recipients nationally in FY2034 [Source 27]. Pennsylvania's share of the national Medicaid population is approximately 4% (PA's 2.99M of national ~72M Medicaid enrollment); applied as structural inference, the PA share of the impacted population would be approximately 360,000-400,000. PA-3-specific OBBBA-affected enrollment (provider-tax stepdown delivery-side flow-through + 6-month redetermination procedural-loss + work-requirement-affected expansion adults) is F-flagged for primary-source magnitude (F21-SD2-06). The Group VIII expansion-state population is the principal work-requirement-affected category; structural inference from PA's ~750,000 expansion adults in steady-state [Source 32] and Philadelphia County's 22% share supports PA-3-specific work-requirement exposure of approximately 30,000-40,000 working-age expansion-population adults, the load-bearing magnitude for SD2 delivery-side gap analysis (F21-SD2-07).

**Aggregate Medicaid-enrolled household characteristics.** Per KFF May 2025 [Source 36] and PA DHS dashboard [Source 32], the Pennsylvania Medicaid-enrolled population includes the following structural categories: children (0-18); adults age 19-64 (including Group VIII expansion adults); aged adults (65+); blind and disabled adults. The four-category breakdown applied to Philadelphia County's ~658,000 enrolled population (structural inference from statewide proportions) is approximately: 276,000 children; 290,000 working-age adults; 50,000 aged adults; 42,000 blind/disabled adults. The MAGI-based and disability-based eligibility pathways have different procedural-loss vulnerability profiles per documented patterns; cross-reference D12 SD2 for the eligibility-side analysis.
