## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

Profiles per M0 Standard 13 are aggregate, not named individuals. Each profile illustrates a category of PA-3 constituent experience with the Medicaid delivery architecture, with illustrative figures and explicit data provenance disclosure.

**Profile 1: HealthChoices working-age family of four navigating PH-MCO selection in North Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* A household of four (two working-age parents at approximately $35,000 combined household income; two children under age 12) in the North/Northwest Core sub-area of PA-3. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Core. *Triggering event:* Loss of employer-sponsored coverage when one parent's hours are reduced below the 30-hour ACA-FT threshold; household income falls below 138% FPL (approximately $42,000 for a family of four per HHS 2026 federal poverty guidelines; illustrative threshold; verify current FPL at HHS before public use, F21-SD2-08); household qualifies for Medicaid under Group VIII expansion and the children remain Medicaid-eligible under standard categorical thresholds.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* The household applies at COMPASS or at the local Philadelphia CAO (illustrative locations: 1410 W. Erie Avenue or 1701 S. Broad Street) [Source 35]. Eligibility determined at D12 boundary; delivery side begins at PH-MCO selection. Household receives notice from PA Enrollment Services; if no selection within the choice window, plan auto-assigned. If selection made, household chooses among Aetna Better Health, Health Partners Plans, Keystone First, or UnitedHealthcare Community Plan [Source 15]. Provider-network research required: household must check whether existing pediatrician, primary-care provider, and pharmacy participate in the selected PH-MCO. Auto-enrolled in Community Behavioral Health (CBH) for any behavioral-health services [Source 13]; no choice in BH plan.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Combined household income: $35,000 (illustrative example at stated income level). Federal Poverty Level for family of four (2026): approximately $32,150 ([SI] structural calculation from HHS 2026 federal poverty guidelines architecture; F21-SD2-08). Group VIII expansion threshold: 138% FPL = approximately $44,367 (illustrative calculation). Number of PH-MCO choices in SE Zone: 4 [Source 15; D]. Annual redetermination historically; transitions to 6-month redetermination beginning December 2026 under OBBBA Section 71107 [Source 25; D]. CBH BH carve-out auto-enrollment: no PA-3-specific choice [Source 13; D].

*Outcome.* Family receives Medicaid coverage at HealthChoices PH-MCO with separate CBH coverage for any behavioral-health services. Initial 12-month redetermination cycle disrupts to 6-month cycle in December 2026; procedural-loss exposure at each renewal cycle, cross-reference D12 SD8 G12-SD8-07. PH-MCO provider-network adequacy is the principal delivery-side variable; if the family's existing primary care provider does not accept the selected plan, the family must either change providers or change plans (continuous open enrollment permits switching). The MC60 Both/And operationalizes here: substantive Medicaid delivery serves the family AND structural OBBBA-driven procedural-loss + fiscal-architecture flow-through compounds at each renewal cycle plus at the provider-network adequacy layer.

**Profile 2: CHC dual-eligible navigating LTSS need in West Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* A widowed beneficiary age 72 (with Medicare Parts A and B coverage from age-65 entitlement; with SSI plus a small private pension placing household income at approximately $1,400/month or ~$16,800/year; assets within Medicaid eligibility limits) in the West Philadelphia Core sub-area. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core. *Triggering event:* Onset of moderate dementia plus chronic obstructive pulmonary disease requiring substantial activities-of-daily-living (ADL) assistance; functional eligibility for CHC LTSS becomes operative.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Beneficiary's existing Medicare coverage is administered separately (cross-reference D21 SD1 G21-SD1-04 dual-eligible architecture). For Medicaid-side coverage and LTSS, beneficiary applies via COMPASS or CAO; eligibility determined at D12 boundary [Source 35]. Calls PA Independent Enrollment Broker at 1-877-550-4227 to request in-home Functional Eligibility Determination [Source 35]. IEB assessor confirms nursing-facility clinical level of care. Beneficiary selects CHC-MCO from three current options: Keystone First CHC (Southeast Zone brand under AmeriHealth Caritas); UPMC Community HealthChoices; PA Health & Wellness [Sources 13, 14]. (The August 2024 CHC re-procurement adds Aetna Better Health and Health Partners Plans to a 5-plan statewide architecture pending readiness review and contract execution [Source 16].) CHC-MCO assigns a service coordinator within 14 days. Service coordinator develops person-centered care plan addressing personal care assistance, medical equipment, home modifications [Source 37]. Beneficiary or family may select "Services My Way" self-direction to hire a family caregiver (spouse and legal guardian excluded) [Source 37].

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Statewide CHC enrollment June 2024: 383,000+ [Source 20; D]. HCBS-receiving share of CHC: 34% [Source 20; D]. Nursing-facility-resident share of CHC: 11% [Source 20; D]. Number of current CHC-MCO options: 3 [Sources 13, 14; D]; expanded to 5 pending re-procurement implementation [Source 16; D]. Income illustrative: $16,800/year ($1,400/month combined SSI plus pension at illustrative example; varies by household). Functional Eligibility Determination provider: PA IEB at 1-877-550-4227 [Source 35; D]. Service-coordinator-developed care plan architecture [Source 37; D].

*Outcome.* Beneficiary receives integrated dual-eligible coverage with Medicare-side clinical delivery and Medicaid CHC-side personal care, home health, and HCBS-supplemental services. Service coordinator handles cross-program coordination including transportation to medical appointments, durable medical equipment authorizations, and meal-delivery coordination [Source 37]. Cross-reference D21 SD1 G21-SD1-04 (dual-eligible Medicare-CHC coordination architecture). The OBBBA delivery-side flow-through is less immediate for dual-eligibles compared to expansion adults — the work-requirement architecture (Section 71119) does not apply to age 65+ beneficiaries [Source 22]; the 6-month redetermination architecture (Section 71107) applies but is less procedurally disruptive given the stability of dual-eligible status. However, the provider-tax stepdown (Section 71115) flow-through affects CHC managed-care plan financial architecture (the same hospitals and providers serving CHC beneficiaries are affected by the provider-tax stepdown), creating downstream LTSS provider-rate pressure and network-adequacy concern.

**Profile 3: CHIP-eligible household navigating renewal architecture in South Philadelphia (MC60 BOTH/AND PRIMARY).**

*Constituent type:* A working household with two children (one parent at approximately $52,000 income; household at approximately 200% FPL for a family of three after the 5% disregard; children eligible for CHIP at 220% FPL threshold for age 0-1 / up to 319% for school-age children) [Source 34]. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest. *Triggering event:* Annual CHIP renewal cycle; household must verify continued income eligibility under PA CHIP's MAGI architecture; OBBBA does not modify CHIP renewal cadence (CHIP renewal cadence is annual; 6-month modification applies to Medicaid) [Source 25].

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Household receives annual CHIP renewal notice; must verify income via COMPASS submission or paper renewal [Source 35]. CHIP-contracted MCO administers clinical delivery. Provider network architecture is parallel to HealthChoices PH-MCO architecture but the specific CHIP plan options differ. Children receive comprehensive medical, dental, vision, and behavioral-health coverage under PA CHIP architecture.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Statewide CHIP enrollment August 2024: 195,320 [Source 39; D]. CHIP eligibility threshold for school-age children: 319% FPL (with 5% disregard) [Source 34; D]. Illustrative household income: $52,000 / family of three / approximately 215% FPL (illustrative calculation; verify current FPL at HHS, F21-SD2-08). Annual renewal cycle preserved at CHIP (no OBBBA modification to CHIP renewal cadence) [Source 25; D].

*Outcome.* Children retain CHIP coverage at renewal. The MC60 Both/And operationalizes here: CHIP delivery architecture is substantively functional AND the OBBBA-driven Medicaid-side disruption (6-month redetermination; provider-tax stepdown; work requirements) affects the household's adult Medicaid coverage decisions in parallel — if a parent is on Medicaid expansion and loses coverage at 6-month redetermination, the household-level continuity of care is disrupted even though the children retain CHIP. The structural Both/And is: substantive Medicaid-and-CHIP architecture serves families AND the OBBBA delivery-side flow-through creates discrete coverage-instability events at the adult-Medicaid interface that operate independently from CHIP-side continuity, complicating household-level care planning. The Both/And designation is not symmetric — substantive delivery continues at CHIP architecture more reliably than at the adult Medicaid architecture.

**Profile 4: PH-MCO enrollee navigating BH-PH care coordination through CBH carve-out in Kensington.**

*Constituent type:* A single working-age adult (Group VIII expansion eligible; income approximately $22,000/year or ~150% FPL) with a documented substance use disorder requiring MAT treatment plus stable employment requiring continuity of care across PH-MCO providers and behavioral-health services. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Core. *Triggering event:* Enrollment in HealthChoices Medicaid with concurrent behavioral-health need; PH-MCO and CBH carve-out architecture creates parallel delivery tracks.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Beneficiary enrolls in HealthChoices Medicaid (Group VIII expansion) via D12 boundary process; selects PH-MCO; automatically enrolled in CBH for behavioral-health [Source 13]. Physical-health services authorized through PH-MCO; SUD MAT treatment authorized through CBH (the structural carve-out architecture). Coordination between PH-MCO primary care and CBH SUD treatment is operationally complex — separate authorization tracks, separate prior-authorization rules, separate prescription drug formularies (PH-MCO formulary for physical-health medications; CBH formulary for SUD-MAT medications including buprenorphine) [Sources 13, 18]. Cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive analysis of CBH architecture.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Group VIII expansion threshold: 138% FPL (~$22,000 for single adult at 150% FPL is illustrative). CBH BH carve-out: county-selected; no PH-MCO-side choice in BH-MCO [Source 13; D]. Separate authorization tracks: PH-MCO authorizations through PH-MCO call line; CBH authorizations through CBH call line [Sources 13, 18; D]. SUD MAT through CBH: methadone, buprenorphine, naltrexone (cross-reference D21 SD6).

*Outcome.* Beneficiary receives Medicaid coverage with effective access at the primary-care layer and at the CBH-administered SUD treatment layer, but coordination across the two architectures requires the beneficiary to navigate parallel delivery tracks. The OBBBA Section 71119 work-requirement architecture (effective January 1, 2027) creates a downstream coverage-instability risk for this beneficiary if SUD treatment limits work-hour capacity below the 80 hours/month threshold; exemption architecture exists but is procedurally complex [Source 22]. The MC60 Both/And operationalizes here at the SUD-treatment-continuity dimension: substantive Medicaid delivery enables SUD treatment continuity AND the OBBBA work-requirement architecture creates structural disruption risk at exactly the population that requires care continuity (cross-reference D12 SD8 procedural-loss territory).

---


## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

What the Medicaid delivery architecture does to PA-3 residents is harder to see than what the eligibility architecture does. The eligibility architecture decides who is in and who is out at a discrete moment: an application is approved or denied, a renewal succeeds or fails. The delivery architecture by contrast operates continuously across the period of coverage — through provider-network selection, plan-formulary decisions, prior-authorization processes, care-coordination handoffs between physical health and behavioral health, and the ongoing operational interactions between the beneficiary and the managed-care plan administering their benefits. Most of the visible quality variation in Medicaid for PA-3 residents is delivery-architecture variation, not eligibility-architecture variation.

The most common misunderstanding about Pennsylvania's Medicaid managed care is that "Medicaid" is a single program with a single provider network. It is not. A PA-3 resident enrolled in Medicaid is enrolled in one of four physical-health plans (Aetna Better Health, Health Partners Plans, Keystone First, UnitedHealthcare) and, if they live in Philadelphia, is also enrolled in Community Behavioral Health for behavioral-health coverage — a separate plan with a separate provider network, separate formulary, and separate authorization processes. Their primary-care physician may participate in one PH-MCO but not another; their psychiatrist may participate in CBH but their primary care may require coordination across plans for any shared services. This is the structural feature of Pennsylvania's HealthChoices architecture: it operates as multiple parallel managed-care delivery systems rather than as a single Medicaid delivery system. For dual-eligible beneficiaries with both Medicare and Medicaid, the architecture multiplies further — Medicare coverage operates separately from Community HealthChoices Medicaid coverage, and integration depends on plan-by-plan and provider-by-provider configuration.

The human consequence visible in 2026 is dual. The HealthChoices architecture has measurably extended healthcare access to working-age PA-3 residents through Medicaid expansion (approximately 750,000 Pennsylvanians covered by Group VIII expansion in steady-state) and has supported the LTSS independence of approximately 130,000 CHC HCBS recipients statewide. The same architecture is under sustained federal pressure beginning in late 2026 and continuing through the FY2032 horizon: the 6-month redetermination architecture taking effect December 2026 creates procedural-loss disruption (cross-reference D12 SD8 G12-SD8-07 for the structural analysis), the work-requirement architecture beginning January 2027 creates coverage-instability risk for the working-poor expansion population, and the provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown beginning FY2028 creates sustained downward pressure on Pennsylvania's Medicaid financing architecture that flows through to delivery-side payment rates. These mechanisms operate independently — each has its own legislative pathway, its own CMS implementation timeline, its own delivery-side flow-through — but they accumulate against the same PA-3 Medicaid-enrolled population. The MC60 Both/And designation operationalized in this SD captures both halves of the human consequence: substantive delivery continues for those who navigate the architecture successfully, AND structural disruption compounds on a sustained basis for those whose lives don't fit the assumptions encoded in the redetermination calendars, the work-requirement exemptions, and the provider-network architectures.

The most analytically important feature visible at SD2 is the asymmetry between federal and state authority. Federal design sets the floor (eligibility categories; mandatory benefits; provider-tax framework; managed-care regulatory architecture under 42 C.F.R. Part 438) and federal legislation (OBBBA) modifies the floor. State implementation determines the texture of delivery (which plans participate; how provider networks are structured; how procedural protections at redetermination are designed; how CBH's BH carve-out coordinates with PH-MCO administration). Federal House representation operates at the floor — appropriation, statutory protection, CMS oversight on the federal implementation of OBBBA. PA state-level engagement operates at the texture — managed-care contracting, the CHC re-procurement implementation, the PA DHS administrative discretion on procedural-loss mitigation. Both layers are operative for PA-3 constituents; neither is sufficient alone.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**<a id="g21-sd2-01"></a>G21-SD2-01: HealthChoices PH-MCO multi-plan provider-network fragmentation in PA-3 (MC60 BOTH/AND substantive-and-structural).**
*Finding — substantive contribution:* HealthChoices Physical Health in the Southeast Zone delivers Medicaid coverage to approximately 658,000 Philadelphia County residents (structural inference from statewide 22% share applied to 2.99M total Medicaid [Sources 36, 39]) across four PH-MCOs (Aetna Better Health, Health Partners Plans, Keystone First, UnitedHealthcare Community Plan) [Source 15]. Each PH-MCO provides a separately-contracted provider network across primary care, specialty care, hospital services, and prescription drug delivery [Source 18].
*Finding — structural fragmentation:* The four-plan architecture means PA-3 Medicaid-enrolled households face PH-MCO selection that determines their entire provider-network access; switching plans (continuous open enrollment is permitted [Source 10]) requires re-verifying provider participation for every existing provider relationship. The behavioral-health carve-out to Community Behavioral Health Philadelphia (CBH) — county-administered, no choice in plan [Source 13] — operates as a separate authorization track for any behavioral-health services. The integration burden across PH-MCO and CBH falls on the beneficiary or their care manager, not on the plans.
*Evidence type:* [D] for PH-MCO architecture and four-plan SE Zone roster; [D] for CBH carve-out structural feature; [SD] for documented coordination-burden distribution.
*Confidence:* HIGH.
*Representation implication:* Federal CMS oversight of 42 C.F.R. Part 438 managed-care contract architecture is direct authority; federal House representation can engage at CMS appropriation and oversight layers. PA-state-level engagement on PH-MCO and BH-MCO contract architecture (network adequacy standards; cross-plan care-coordination requirements) is the principal texture-level locus. *T21-SD2-01: PH-MCO 2026 Agreement (pending Commonwealth signatures and CMS approval per PA DHS [Source 12]) — final contract architecture trajectory.*

**G21-SD2-02: OBBBA Section 71107 / 71115 / 71117 / 71119 delivery-side compounding flow-through (MC60 BOTH/AND PRIMARY).**
*Finding:* OBBBA's Medicaid delivery-side flow-through compounds across four mechanisms operative October 2026 through FY2032+: (a) provider-tax freeze October 1, 2026 [Source 23]; (b) 6-month redetermination December 2026 [Source 25]; (c) work-requirement January 2027 [Source 22]; (d) safe-harbor stepdown to 3.5% by FY2032 [Source 27]. Aggregate national impact estimated at 9.1 million Medicaid recipients affected by provider-tax provisions by FY2034 per CBO [Source 27]; $11.9B annual federal Medicaid funding decline across 18 expansion states once caps fully implemented [Source 23]. Pennsylvania expansion-state Medicaid architecture is affected by all four mechanisms; PA-3 affected magnitude is F-flagged (F21-SD2-06; F21-SD2-07).
*Evidence type:* [D] for federal statutory architecture; [D] for CBO impact magnitude; [SI] for PA-3 magnitude (structural inference pending primary-source confirmation).
*Confidence:* HIGH for federal architecture; MEDIUM for PA-3-specific magnitude.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct advocacy on OBBBA technical-corrections legislation, CMS implementation guidance posture, and Rural Health Transformation Fund allocation ($10B annually 2026-2030 [Source 22]) — all are legislative or oversight territory. PA-state-level engagement on implementation discretion (how 6-month redetermination notice and renewal procedures are operationalized; whether the 3-year CMS MCO-tax transition [Source 30] is requested and granted) is the texture-level locus. *T21-SD2-02: CMS final guidance on Section 71107 / 71115 / 71117 / 71119 implementation timeline through 2027 — rulemaking trajectory will determine the precise contour of delivery-side flow-through.*

*Verification update (2026-05-15): Phase 3 confirms Phase 1 framing on OBBBA CMS implementation guidance trajectory with precision additions. CMS now uses administrative naming "Working Families Tax Cut" (WFTC) legislation for OBBBA per Medicaid.gov implementation documentation. CMS implementation sequence as of verification date: (i) November 18, 2025 CMCS Informational Bulletin (initial guidance on eligibility, financing, and lawful enrollment reforms); (ii) December 8, 2025 Community Engagement CMCS Informational Bulletin (Section 71119; ~16-page document; provides operational guidance on demonstration-period architecture); (iii) early February 2026 CMS Final Rule "Preserving Medicaid Funding for Vulnerable Populations-Closing a Health Care-Related Tax Loophole" (statistical-test tightening on provider-tax architecture; CBO scoring $35B FY 2026-2034; CMS internal scoring $78B base / up to $313B counterfactual); (iv) January 29, 2026 CMS Fact Sheet on Medicaid Technology Companies pledges ($600M pledged to support community engagement systems); (v) $200M Government Efficiency Grants FY 2026 to states for community-engagement system development. Statutory implementation deadline architecture confirmed: states must implement community-engagement requirements by January 1, 2027 (early adoption permitted); CMS Interim Final Rule due by June 1, 2026 (some sources reference June 6, 2026 per OBBBA statutory text); beneficiary outreach must begin no later than December 31, 2026; retroactive coverage reduction (Section 71107) to 60 days traditional / 30 days expansion effective January 1, 2027; provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown to 3.5% over five years FY 2028-2032 (expansion states); Rural Health Transformation Fund $50B total ($10B annually FY 2026-2030; $5B equal-state allocation + $5B rurality-and-application-based allocation; first distributions in 2026 with December 29, 2025 initial state-application decisions per CLA source). Named officials per Standard 13.C carve-out: HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.; CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz; CMCS Director Dan Brillman. AMA submitted recommendations to CMS via March 9, 2026 letter to CMCS Director on community-engagement guidance; AMA March 12, 2026 letter to HHS Secretary on 2027 marketplace plan benefits/payments proposals. No Section 1115 demonstration waiver activity needed for OBBBA work requirements per statutory mandate. T21-SD2-02 through T21-SD2-08 carries to embedded Temporal-Changes Sidecar as pre-window-date-locked vector points per Standard 18.A with next observation points at CMS Interim Final Rule publication (target June 1, 2026), state-implementation activities through January 1, 2027, and CMS implementation-guidance issuance through FY 2027.*

**<a id="g21-sd2-03"></a>G21-SD2-03: Community HealthChoices re-procurement implementation status (5-plan architecture pending).**
*Finding:* PA DHS announced selection of five CHC-MCOs in August 2024 — Aetna Better Health, Health Partners Plans, PA Health & Wellness, UPMC for You, Vista Health Plan (AmeriHealth Caritas / Keystone First) — for the next CHC contract cycle [Source 16]. As of late 2025, readiness review and contract execution were pending [Source 16]. CHC enrollment of 383,000+ statewide (June 2024) is the affected population [Source 20]. Cross-reference D21 SD1 dual-eligible architecture (G21-SD1-04).
*Evidence type:* [D] for CHC re-procurement public announcement and 5-plan roster.
*Confidence:* HIGH for announcement; MEDIUM for current operational status.
*Representation implication:* PA state DHS administrative authority; federal CMS approval at the 1915(b)/(c) waiver renewal layer. Federal House representation engagement is at the CMS waiver-approval-cycle level. *T21-SD2-03: CHC re-procurement contract execution and operational rollout timeline 2026-2027; the 5-plan architecture's network and pricing dynamics will determine CHC delivery characteristics for the next contract cycle.*

**<a id="g21-sd2-04"></a>G21-SD2-04: PA-3-specific Medicaid sub-area-disaggregated data gap.**
*Finding:* PA-3-specific Medicaid enrollment disaggregated by the four sub-areas (North/Northwest Core; West Philadelphia Core; Northwest; South/Southwest) is not retrievable from public-facing data products. Structural inference from documented Philadelphia poverty-rate-by-tract patterns supports North/Northwest Core and West Philadelphia Core concentration; PA-3-specific magnitudes are F-flagged for institutional retrieval (F21-SD2-02; F21-SD2-03). Cross-reference D13 SD2/SD4/SD6 cumulative-burden geography and D12 SD2 eligibility-side analysis.
*Evidence type:* [SI] for structural inference from documented poverty-rate patterns; [F] for sub-area magnitudes pending institutional retrieval.
*Confidence:* MEDIUM (high for structural pattern; medium for specific magnitudes).
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation can engage at federal data-product accessibility (CMS Office of Minority Health Geographic Variation Public Use File; ACS Congressional District estimates).

**<a id="g21-sd2-05"></a>G21-SD2-05: Provider-network adequacy and Medicaid participation rate gap in PA-3.**
*Finding:* Documented national patterns establish Medicaid provider participation rates vary by specialty — primary care has highest participation; behavioral health, specialty surgical care, and dental have systematically lower participation. PA-3-specific participation rates by PH-MCO by specialty are not retrievable at SD2 (F21-SD2-01). Cross-reference D21 SD6 for behavioral-health specific architecture.
*Evidence type:* [SI] for documented national pattern applied to PA-3 by structural inference; [F] for PA-3 specifics pending primary-source.
*Confidence:* HIGH for structural feature; MEDIUM for PA-3-specific magnitudes.
*Representation implication:* Federal CMS authority on Medicaid network adequacy regulations under 42 C.F.R. Part 438.68; federal House representation has direct advocacy on CMS rulemaking trajectory.

**<a id="g21-sd2-06"></a>G21-SD2-06: HealthChoices BH-PH care-coordination architecture gap at CBH carve-out interface.**
*Finding:* The HealthChoices behavioral-health carve-out to CBH in Philadelphia County creates structural parallel delivery tracks: PH-MCO administers physical-health authorizations on one track; CBH administers BH/SUD authorizations on a separate track [Source 13]. Cross-plan care coordination is the structural coordination challenge; the burden falls on the beneficiary or care manager rather than on the plans. Cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive CBH architecture analysis.
*Evidence type:* [D] for CBH carve-out structural feature.
*Confidence:* HIGH for structural feature.
*Representation implication:* PA state-level engagement on cross-plan care-coordination requirements; federal CMS engagement on MHPAEA enforcement (cross-reference D21 SD3 MHPAEA commercial implementation and D21 SD6 SUD delivery architecture).

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**What the legal framework provides or promises.** Social Security Act Title XIX and 42 C.F.R. Part 438 establish a federal floor for Medicaid managed-care delivery — minimum eligibility categories, mandatory benefits, managed-care plan operational requirements (network adequacy, prior authorization standards, appeals architecture, actuarial soundness rate-setting, medical loss ratio requirements), and a structural framework that permits states substantial administrative discretion at contracting and operations. Pennsylvania's HealthChoices Physical Health and Community HealthChoices architectures operationalize this framework for the working-age, child, dual-eligible, and LTSS-eligible Medicaid-enrolled populations across the five HealthChoices zones; CHIP Title XXI extends comparable architecture to children in families with incomes above Medicaid thresholds up to 319% FPL.

**What PA-3 constituents actually receive.** Approximately 658,000 Philadelphia County Medicaid-enrolled residents (structural inference from 22% statewide share applied to 2.99M PA total) receive Medicaid coverage through one of four Southeast Zone PH-MCOs, with CHIP coverage for children in income-eligible households, and with CHC architecture for dual-eligibles and LTSS-eligible adults. The delivery architecture is operationally functional at the primary-care and routine-care layers, with documented gaps at the behavioral-health-physical-health coordination interface, the specialty-care provider participation layer, and at procedural-loss disruption points around renewal cycles. The OBBBA delivery-side flow-through introduces compounding structural disruption beginning October 2026 — 6-month redeterminations, work-requirements for expansion adults, retroactive-coverage reductions, and provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown — that affects continuity of care and provider-rate architecture independently of any individual eligibility decision.

**What accounts for the gap.** Four structural features account for the gap between framework and received delivery: (a) the multi-plan PH-MCO architecture introduces fragmentation across separately-contracted provider networks, formularies, and authorization tracks, with switching costs for beneficiaries; (b) the BH carve-out to CBH creates parallel delivery tracks requiring beneficiary-led or care-manager-led coordination across plans; (c) the asymmetric federal-state authority allocation places texture-level decisions about plan contracting, network adequacy, and procedural protection at state administrative discretion, with PA state-level engagement determining how protective or disruptive the delivery experience is; (d) the OBBBA delivery-side flow-through introduces sustained federal-level structural pressure beginning October 2026 that compounds across multiple mechanisms (provider-tax stepdown; 6-month redetermination; work-requirement; retroactive-coverage reduction) operating on the same PA-3 Medicaid-enrolled population, producing the MC60 Both/And designation operationalized in this SD.

**What the gap means for the representation analysis.** Federal House representation operates at the OBBBA implementation interface (technical-corrections legislation; CMS rulemaking oversight; appropriation for the $50B Rural Health Transformation Fund and SHIP/State Medicaid Agency program continuity) and at the federal floor (Medicaid statutory architecture; 42 C.F.R. Part 438 regulatory framework; MHPAEA enforcement). PA-state-level texture engagement is the principal complementary locus. Both layers are required for substantive engagement with the SD2 delivery architecture. The MC60 Both/And captures the central analytical posture: substantive Medicaid delivery continues operative at federal-floor + PA-administrative-overlay architecture, AND structural fiscal-pressure mechanisms from OBBBA compound on the institutional side (SD4 hospital institutional; SD5 FQHC/safety-net) and on the procedural-loss side (D12 SD2/SD8) and the work-requirement side, producing predictable downstream disruption to delivery continuity through FY2032.
