# D21 Healthcare Delivery — Sub-Domain 3
## ACA Marketplace and Commercial Insurance Delivery

**Date:** 2026-05-11
**Phase:** 1 (Per-SD Execution; fresh cycle)
**Domain:** 21 — Healthcare Delivery
**Sub-Domain:** 3 of 7
**Cycle position:** Phase 1 SD2-N batch execution; SD1 + SD2 complete
**Methodology stack:** M0 v2.2 · M2 v1.2.1 · Skills v3
**Status:** Phase 1 draft — SD3 of SD2-N batch

---

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD3 documents the ACA marketplace and commercial insurance delivery architecture governing the privately-insured population in PA-3: the federal entitlement framework under the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act (ACA, 42 U.S.C. § 18001 et seq.) that established the federal marketplace architecture, Premium Tax Credit (APTC) delivery system, Cost-Sharing Reduction (CSR) architecture, essential health benefits framework, and pre-existing-condition exclusion prohibition; the Pennsylvania state-based exchange (Pennie) authorized under 35 P.S. § 449.6101 et seq. that operates as Pennsylvania's official ACA marketplace administered by the Pennsylvania Health Insurance Exchange Authority (PHIEA); the federal regulatory framework for commercial group health insurance delivery including the No Surprises Act (P.L. 116-260 Div. BB) surprise-billing architecture, Mental Health Parity and Addiction Equity Act (MHPAEA, 29 U.S.C. § 1185a) commercial implementation, ERISA framework for employer-sponsored insurance, and ACA Section 2713 USPSTF preventive services commercial implementation; the Pennsylvania Insurance Department regulatory architecture; and the One Big Beautiful Bill Act of 2025 (OBBBA; P.L. 119-21) ACA marketplace modifications affecting eligibility and verification architecture. The sub-domain analyzes ACA marketplace + commercial as a clinical-and-coverage-delivery architecture rather than as a coverage-eligibility architecture. **Boundary 1** (per D21 substructure §2): D12 SD2 owns marketplace eligibility and enrollment architecture; D21 SD3 owns clinical delivery, provider network architecture, and post-2025 enhanced-subsidy expiration delivery-side flow-through.

**Regime declaration.** Internet-access regime. Current-state Pennie enrollment figures, IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit expiration delivery-side effects, OBBBA-driven Pennie eligibility modifications, MHPAEA enforcement administrative posture, No Surprises Act implementation status, and commercial-insurance market structure claims are expected to be retrieved; specific figures carry sources per Standard 12. The ACA Title I architecture, ERISA framework, MHPAEA enforcement framework, and No Surprises Act statutory architecture are framework-level statutory references; specific section citations are sourced where claims are specific. Mixed-regime drafting is prohibited per Standard 1; this SD operates entirely under internet-access regime.

**Analytical posture.** Both/And-primary at MC61 candidate (ACA marketplace post-EPTC-expiration delivery-side flow-through). SD3 surfaces a new within-domain analytical-tension Both/And: substantive ACA marketplace architecture continues operative through Pennie state-based exchange with continued (smaller) federal Premium Tax Credit availability for income-qualifying enrollees, AND structural disruption from the December 31, 2025 expiration of IRA-enhanced Premium Tax Credits has produced documented coverage loss at scale (~85,000 Pennie enrollees terminated coverage during 2026 Open Enrollment; additional 60,000+ dropped through May 1, 2026; total enrollment declined from ~500,000 in 2025 to 452,525 as of May 1, 2026) [Sources 41, 44, 47]. The Both/And designation parallels MC60 SD2 in structural shape (substantive delivery + OBBBA-or-IRA-driven disruption); SD3 MC61 candidate surfaced for methodology absorption queue pending lead direction. No held-open-magnitude territory at SD3 (the substructure's HOM candidates concentrate at SD4 anchor community benefit and SD5 safety-net fiscal vulnerability).

**Thread declarations.** Three analytical threads operative at SD3:

- **D21-SD3-Thread A (federal-floor + state-based-exchange-overlay architecture).** ACA is a federal entitlement framework with statutory stability HIGH at program level; the federal Premium Tax Credit / APTC delivery architecture operates under 26 U.S.C. § 36B with eligibility set by household income relative to federal poverty level; the state-based-exchange model permits states to operate their own marketplace under federal certification with operational authority over consumer protection, plan certification, and marketplace technology. Pennsylvania established Pennie under Act 42 of 2019 (35 P.S. § 449.6101 et seq.) and operates Pennie as a state-based exchange [Sources 49, 41]. The federal-state interface is asymmetric: federal authority dominates at the Premium Tax Credit substantive architecture; state authority operates at marketplace design, consumer protection, and verification procedures.

- **D21-SD3-Thread B (IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit December 31, 2025 expiration delivery-side flow-through).** The American Rescue Plan Act of 2021 established Enhanced Premium Tax Credits (EPTCs) for ACA marketplace enrollees; the Inflation Reduction Act of 2022 extended EPTCs through 2025. Congress did not extend EPTCs for 2026 [Sources 41, 45, 49]; EPTCs expired December 31, 2025. Average Pennie premium increased 102% in 2026 [Sources 41, 43, 47]; total Pennie enrollment declined from approximately 500,000 in 2025 to 452,525 as of May 1, 2026 [Source 44]; total cancellations through May 1, 2026 exceeded 145,000 [Source 44]. The US House voted to extend EPTCs for three years in early 2026; the measure was pending in the Senate and required presidential signature [Source 42]. Pennsylvania House Representatives Fitzpatrick (PA-1), Mackenzie (PA-7), and Bresnahan (PA-8) crossed party lines to vote for the extension; the remaining PA Republican delegation voted against [Source 42]. The EPTC expiration is delivery-side architecture at SD3 (the substantive eligibility and architectural design is D12 SD2 / D9 SD7 territory).

- **D21-SD3-Thread C (OBBBA H.R. 1 of 2025 Pennie / ACA marketplace modifications).** OBBBA modifies ACA marketplace verification procedures, increases penalties for inaccurate income reporting at the marketplace, and modifies eligibility for "lawfully present" immigrants — lawfully present immigrants may no longer qualify for Premium Tax Credit savings under OBBBA [Source 45]. Pennie has communicated these changes to enrollees through the 2026 Open Enrollment process [Source 45]. The OBBBA marketplace modifications are operative for 2026 enrollment and beyond. Cross-reference D12 SD2 for the substantive eligibility architecture; D9 SD7 for fiscal-architecture-side impact.

---

## Section 2: Authority Chain

The ACA marketplace and commercial insurance delivery authority chain in PA-3 follows the federal-state-local layered architecture (DOMAIN_FRAMEWORK Cross-Cutting Thread 3) with substantial state regulatory authority at the commercial-insurance and marketplace-operations layer.

**Constitutional Foundation.** U.S. Constitution Article I, Section 8 (Commerce Clause and General Welfare Clause) authorizes federal regulation of interstate insurance markets and federal Premium Tax Credit architecture; Tenth Amendment reserves state insurance regulatory authority subject to federal preemption where Congress has acted. The McCarran-Ferguson Act of 1945 (15 U.S.C. § 1011) preserves state insurance regulation; ERISA preempts state regulation of self-funded employer health plans. *NFIB v. Sebelius* (2012) preserved state discretion on Medicaid expansion but upheld the ACA individual market architecture; *King v. Burwell* (2015) preserved federal Premium Tax Credits in states using federal HealthCare.gov; *California v. Texas* (2021) preserved the ACA when challenged through the individual mandate post-zero-penalty amendment.

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

- **Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act (ACA; 42 U.S.C. § 18001 et seq.).** Establishes ACA marketplace; APTC architecture (26 U.S.C. § 36B); CSR architecture (42 U.S.C. § 18071); essential health benefits framework; pre-existing-condition exclusion prohibition; medical loss ratio requirements; rate review architecture. *Statutory stability HIGH at program level (multiple Supreme Court rulings preserved the architecture); administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH at CMS regulatory implementation given OBBBA modifications and the Trump administration's CMS regulatory posture on marketplace verification, enrollment-prevention, and immigrant eligibility*.

- **American Rescue Plan Act of 2021 / Inflation Reduction Act of 2022 (Enhanced PTC architecture).** Established and extended the Enhanced Premium Tax Credits removing the 400% FPL eligibility cliff and increasing PTC subsidy amounts for all income bands. *EXPIRED December 31, 2025 per Congress's non-extension* [Sources 41, 45, 49]. *Statutory architecture is in current law as expired; the US House passed an extension in early 2026 [Source 42]; legislative status pending Senate action through 2026-2027*.

- **No Surprises Act (P.L. 116-260 Div. BB).** Effective January 1, 2022; protects consumers from surprise medical bills for emergency services and certain out-of-network services at in-network facilities; establishes Independent Dispute Resolution (IDR) process for payer-provider billing disputes. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at IDR implementation and CMS rulemaking*.

- **Mental Health Parity and Addiction Equity Act (MHPAEA; 29 U.S.C. § 1185a).** Requires commercial group health plans and ACA marketplace plans to provide mental health and substance use disorder benefits at parity with medical/surgical benefits — same financial requirements, treatment limitations, network access, and prior authorization standards. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability HIGH at federal enforcement (DOL, HHS, Treasury share enforcement authority) and at state insurance department enforcement*. Cross-reference D21 SD6 for behavioral-health delivery architecture; D3 prior-method verified content for substantive MHPAEA implementation analysis (out of immediate scope but accessible).

- **ERISA (29 U.S.C. § 1001 et seq.).** Governs employer-sponsored health insurance plans; preempts state regulation of self-funded employer plans (federal regulatory floor only); self-funded employer plans cover approximately 60% of employer-sponsored coverage nationally. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **ACA Section 2713 USPSTF preventive services commercial implementation (42 U.S.C. § 300gg-13).** Requires commercial group health plans and ACA marketplace plans to cover USPSTF-rated preventive services at no cost-sharing. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at USPSTF recommendation updates and HHS guidance*. Cross-reference D2 SD2 for substantive USPSTF preventive services architecture.

- **One Big Beautiful Bill Act of 2025 (P.L. 119-21).** OBBBA modifies ACA marketplace verification procedures, increases income-misreporting penalties, and modifies immigrant eligibility for Premium Tax Credits [Source 45]. *Statutory stability HIGH at enactment; administrative vulnerability HIGH at CMS implementation through 2026-2027*. Cross-reference D12 SD8 for OBBBA comprehensive analysis.

**Federal Agency Layer.**

- **Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS), Center for Consumer Information and Insurance Oversight (CCIIO).** 7500 Security Boulevard, Baltimore, MD 21244. Administers federal ACA marketplace; certifies state-based exchanges including Pennie; oversees Premium Tax Credit eligibility verification; rate review oversight; medical loss ratio enforcement. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH*.

- **U.S. Department of Labor (DOL) Employee Benefits Security Administration (EBSA).** 200 Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, D.C. 20210. Enforces ERISA framework for employer-sponsored health plans; jointly enforces MHPAEA with HHS and Treasury; jointly enforces No Surprises Act provisions affecting ERISA plans. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH*.

- **U.S. Department of the Treasury, Internal Revenue Service.** Administers Premium Tax Credit (APTC) verification at tax filing; income-reconciliation architecture; OBBBA-modified income-misreporting penalties [Source 45]. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS).** Joint enforcement with DOL and Treasury for MHPAEA; rulemaking authority on No Surprises Act IDR. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH*.

**State Statutory Layer.**

- **Pennsylvania Insurance Department Act (40 P.S. § 1 et seq.).** Authorizes PA Insurance Department to regulate the Pennsylvania commercial insurance market; license insurance companies; review rates; enforce consumer protection. *Statutory stability HIGH*.

- **Pennsylvania Health Insurance Exchange Authority Act (Act 42 of 2019; 35 P.S. § 449.6101 et seq.).** Establishes PHIEA as a public agency to operate Pennie [Source 49]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **PA Mental Health Parity Act (PA Act 50 of 2004; 40 P.S. § 908-1 et seq.).** Pennsylvania state MHPAEA implementation; PA Insurance Department enforcement authority on commercial plans regulated under PA insurance code; ERISA-preemption applies to self-funded employer plans. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at PA Insurance Department enforcement*.

- **Act 54 of 2024 — State Health Insurance Exchange Affordability Program.** Pennsylvania's pending state-level affordability program; established but unfunded as of May 2026 per Pennie communications [Source 41]. *Statutory stability MODERATE at enactment; appropriation pending in PA budget cycles 2025-2027*.

**State Agency Layer.**

- **Pennsylvania Health Insurance Exchange Authority (PHIEA, dba Pennie).** 312-A Forster Street, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Operates Pennsylvania's state-based ACA exchange; certifies plans; administers Premium Tax Credit eligibility verification at state-level interface with federal IRS reconciliation; operates pennie.com; manages customer service line at 1-844-844-8040 [Sources 41, 48]. *per Standard 13.C carve-out: Pennie Director Devon Trolley identified in official capacity per Pennie communications and Pennsylvania Capital-Star reporting* [Sources 42, 46, 47]. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE for marketplace operations; HIGH for affordability program funding*.

- **Pennsylvania Insurance Department.** 1326 Strawberry Square, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Regulates commercial insurance market; reviews rates; enforces consumer protections; enforces PA Mental Health Parity Act; coordinates with federal regulators on No Surprises Act IDR implementation. *per Standard 13.C carve-out: Pennsylvania Insurance Commissioner Michael Humphreys identified in official capacity per Pennie press communications and PHIEA documents* [Source 49]. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

- Local layer is operationally light for SD3 territory; PA preemption on insurance regulation places authority at state and federal levels. Philadelphia Code provides anti-discrimination protections (Title 9, Section 9-1100 et seq.) applicable to health-insurance and healthcare contexts at the employer-employee interface but does not regulate commercial-insurance carriers directly.

**Local Agency Layer.**

- **Philadelphia Commission on Human Relations (PCHR).** Enforces Philadelphia anti-discrimination ordinances applicable to employer health benefits and certain healthcare contexts; cross-reference D11 cross-domain anti-discrimination architecture; out of immediate SD3 commercial-insurance scope but operationally relevant for cross-domain anti-discrimination engagement.

- **Philadelphia Corporation for Aging (PCA).** 642 N. Broad Street, Philadelphia, PA 19130. Provides Medicare counseling (PA MEDI / SHIP architecture; cross-reference D21 SD1) and limited support for ACA marketplace navigation; operates limited capacity for the marketplace navigation territory compared to dedicated Pennie call center capacity.

**Constituent Layer.** Aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5: PA-3 marketplace-enrolled household navigating Pennie 2026 post-EPTC-expiration cost increase; PA-3 commercial-group-health-enrolled employee navigating ERISA-preempted self-funded plan; PA-3 MHPAEA enforcement-affected enrollee navigating mental-health parity claim; PA-3 lawfully-present-immigrant household navigating OBBBA-modified Pennie eligibility.

---

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

The ACA marketplace and commercial insurance delivery pathway for a PA-3 constituent operates through two principal channels: the Pennie state-based exchange for individual-market coverage (with Premium Tax Credit eligibility for income-qualifying households) and employer-sponsored insurance for employee-eligible households. D12 SD2 owns the marketplace eligibility architecture per Boundary 1; D21 SD3 owns the delivery-side pathway that begins once coverage is operative.

*Triggering condition.* A PA-3 constituent enters ACA marketplace + commercial coverage architecture under several documented pathways: (a) at job loss or hours-reduction below ACA-FT 30-hour threshold triggering loss of employer-sponsored insurance and qualifying-life-event Special Enrollment Period; (b) at marriage, divorce, birth, or move triggering qualifying-life-event SEP; (c) at Annual Open Enrollment (November 1 - January 15 federally; Pennie operates November 1 - January 31 with December 31 deadline for January 1 coverage extended for 2026) [Source 48]; (d) at Medicaid disenrollment or CHIP transition where the household's income places them in marketplace-eligible territory (cross-reference D12 SD2 / SD8 procedural-loss flow-through); (e) at age 26 when adult dependent loses parental plan coverage; (f) at COBRA exhaustion or premium-default. The OBBBA-modified income-misreporting penalty architecture (Source 45) increases the stakes of accurate income reporting at each enrollment step.

*Pennie enrollment pathway.* A PA-3 constituent enrolls through pennie.com (web portal) or by calling Pennie Customer Service at 1-844-844-8040 [Source 48]. Enrollment requires: (a) Pennie account creation with verified income, household size, and tax-filing status; (b) Premium Tax Credit eligibility determination via federal IRS reconciliation framework; (c) plan selection from marketplace-certified plans (bronze, silver, gold, platinum metal tiers; HDHP-eligible plans; catastrophic plans for under-30 and hardship-exempt enrollees); (d) effectuation through first premium payment to the selected carrier. Pennie certified plans in the Southeast region include Independence Blue Cross (Keystone Health Plan East and Personal Choice products), AmeriHealth Pennsylvania, Highmark Blue Shield, UPMC Health Plan, Geisinger Health Plan, Aetna, and limited-network Oscar; the specific 2026 plan roster varies by county and tier [Source 19 plan roster as cross-reference; F21-SD3-01 for current Pennie 2026 plan inventory by Philadelphia County]. After enrollment, the carrier administers clinical delivery through its commercial provider network; Pennie's role is marketplace operations rather than delivery-side care administration.

*Post-EPTC-expiration enrollment dynamics.* The December 31, 2025 expiration of Enhanced Premium Tax Credits produced documented enrollment disruption [Sources 41, 43, 44, 47]: average Pennie premium increased 102% in 2026 [Sources 41, 43, 47]; new enrollments dropped 12% during 2026 OE compared to 2025 OE [Source 41]; total enrollment declined from approximately 500,000 in 2025 to 452,525 as of May 1, 2026 [Source 44] with 145,000+ cumulative cancellations [Source 44]. The terminated population cited cost as the principal reason for leaving coverage [Source 47]. The 102% average premium increase concentrated impact on households earning above the EPTC cutoff (approximately $62,600 for single; $84,600 for couple; the prior EPTC architecture removed the 400% FPL cliff and capped premium at 8.5% of household income) [Source 42]. For a 60-year-old couple, the 2026 premium increase was reported at $500-$600/month (2025) → $2,000-$3,000/month (2026) [Source 42]. Pennie surveyed terminated enrollees who reported cost as principal reason for leaving [Source 47]; older and rural enrollees were over-represented in the terminated population [Source 47].

*Pending federal legislative action.* In early 2026, the U.S. House voted to extend EPTCs for three years [Source 42]; the measure was pending in the Senate. Three Pennsylvania House Republicans (Fitzpatrick PA-1, Mackenzie PA-7, Bresnahan PA-8) crossed party lines to advance the bill; remaining PA Republican delegation voted against [Source 42]. Senate action and presidential signature were pending as of the SD3 drafting date; Pennie has committed to providing enrollment-modification opportunity if legislative action occurs [Source 42].

*Commercial group health pathway (employer-sponsored insurance).* For PA-3 constituents with employer-sponsored coverage, the pathway operates outside the Pennie marketplace: employer selects plan(s); employee enrolls through HR; employer administers payroll deduction for employee premium share; insurance carrier administers clinical delivery through commercial provider network. Self-funded employer plans (approximately 60% of employer-sponsored coverage nationally per documented patterns) are ERISA-regulated rather than state-regulated; fully-insured employer plans are jointly state-and-federal-regulated [PA Insurance Department; cross-reference D10 employment architecture]. Provider network access varies by plan; large academic medical centers (Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health, CHOP) participate in most major commercial networks [Source 19]; the specific network roster for any given employer's plan is plan-and-product specific.

*MHPAEA delivery-side pathway.* A PA-3 constituent with commercial coverage seeking behavioral-health or substance-use-disorder treatment encounters the MHPAEA framework at three operational points: (a) plan-coverage scope (the plan must cover MH/SUD if it covers medical/surgical); (b) financial-requirement and treatment-limitation parity (deductibles, co-pays, prior authorization standards must be at parity); (c) network adequacy parity (provider-network access must be at parity). Documented patterns establish that commercial behavioral-health network participation rates are systematically lower than medical/surgical participation rates despite MHPAEA's parity requirement; the gap is the principal documented enforcement territory [cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive analysis]. PA Insurance Department enforces PA Mental Health Parity Act on commercial plans regulated under PA insurance code; ERISA-preempted self-funded employer plans are federally enforced by DOL, HHS, and Treasury jointly.

*No Surprises Act delivery-side pathway.* A PA-3 constituent seeking emergency services or scheduled services at an in-network facility encounters the No Surprises Act framework when ancillary providers (anesthesiologists, radiologists, pathologists, emergency-physician groups) at the in-network facility are out-of-network. The Act prohibits balance billing for these services; protects consumer against out-of-network cost-sharing above in-network levels for the protected categories; establishes IDR architecture for payer-provider billing disputes. Cross-reference D21 SD4 for hospital institutional architecture; CMS administers IDR.

*Decision points where the pathway breaks for PA-3 constituents.* The ACA marketplace + commercial delivery pathway breaks down at several documented points: (a) at the post-EPTC-expiration affordability cliff, where households earning above the $62,600 single / $84,600 couple threshold lose all Premium Tax Credit eligibility and face full unsubsidized premium architecture — the 102% average premium increase has produced documented coverage loss at scale [Sources 41, 43, 47]; (b) at the OBBBA-modified income-misreporting penalty layer, where households facing income variability across the calendar year face increased reconciliation risk; (c) at the OBBBA-modified immigrant-eligibility layer, where lawfully-present immigrants may lose PTC eligibility — Pennie has communicated these changes to affected enrollees [Source 45]; (d) at the MHPAEA enforcement layer, where structurally lower behavioral-health network participation persists despite parity requirements; (e) at the No Surprises Act ancillary-provider layer, where the IDR architecture is operationally complex and arbitration outcomes have produced documented friction in the payer-provider relationship; (f) at the ERISA-preempted self-funded plan layer, where state-level consumer protections do not apply and federal enforcement is the only remedy. The PA Insurance Department Consumer Services Bureau, Pennsylvania Health Law Project (PHLP), and Pennie Customer Service constitute the principal navigation backstops; the navigation capacity is substantially smaller than the affected population.

---


## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**Pennie state-based exchange enrollment (Pennsylvania).** Pennie 2025 peak enrollment was approximately 500,000 [Sources 41, 44]. Pennie 2026 Open Enrollment closed January 31, 2026 with 85,000 terminations during OE and total enrollment ending at approximately 486,000 [Sources 41, 47]. As of May 1, 2026, Pennie total enrollment was 452,525 [Source 44]; an additional 60,000+ have dropped coverage since the close of OE 2026 [Source 44]; cumulative cancellations through May 1, 2026 exceeded 145,000 [Source 44]. New enrollments during OE 2026 totaled approximately 80,000 (12% decrease vs. 2025 OE) [Source 47].

**Philadelphia County and PA-3 share of Pennie enrollment.** PA-3-specific Pennie enrollment magnitude is not separately disaggregated in public-facing Pennie data products at SD3. Pennie has provided county-level cost-impact data and Congressional District-level rate-increase data [Source 43]; the PA-3 (Philadelphia 3rd Congressional District) specific enrollee count is F-flagged for primary-source retrieval (F21-SD3-02). Structural inference: Philadelphia County's lower-income demographics relative to PA statewide concentrate the lower-income population in Medicaid rather than marketplace coverage; PA-3 share of statewide Pennie enrollment is structurally lower than PA-3 share of Medicaid enrollment. Estimated PA-3 Pennie enrollment: approximately 30,000-50,000 (F21-SD3-02 for primary-source confirmation).

**Coverage loss concentration.** Pennie county-level data establishes rural counties experienced disproportionately high financial impact per dollar of EPTC loss; urban counties had more enrollees and higher absolute disenrollment counts [Source 43]. Older enrollees and rural enrollees over-represented in terminated population [Source 47]. Income-band breakdown: 19,571 PA enrollees earning less than 150% FPL dropped coverage (16.7% of total in that income range) [Source 43]; substantial drops also occurred just over 400% FPL (the EPTC-cliff threshold population) [Source 44]. The age 55+ population in PA-3 is structurally exposed at higher magnitudes than younger populations given ACA marketplace age-rating architecture.

**Commercial group health enrollment in PA-3.** Not separately disaggregated by Congressional District in public data; Pennsylvania statewide employer-sponsored insurance covers approximately 55% of the under-65 population per Census ACS (cross-reference D10 SD2). Philadelphia County's commercial-coverage share is structurally lower than statewide given concentrated Medicaid and uninsured population. PA-3 commercial-coverage magnitude is F-flagged for institutional retrieval (F21-SD3-03).

**MHPAEA-affected population estimate.** All Pennie-enrolled and commercial-group-health-enrolled populations are subject to MHPAEA parity requirements. Structural inference supports approximately 150,000-200,000 working-age PA-3 residents with commercial coverage subject to MHPAEA parity (F21-SD3-04 for primary-source).

**PA-3 sub-area variation.** Sub-area-disaggregated Pennie and commercial enrollment at the four-sub-area level is not retrievable from public-facing data products. Per Standard 6, the limitation is stated explicitly. Structural inference from documented Philadelphia income-distribution patterns supports that South/Southwest and Northwest sub-areas have higher proportional Pennie and commercial coverage relative to North/Northwest Core and West Philadelphia Core, consistent with documented income-distribution patterns. PA-3-specific sub-area magnitudes F-flagged (F21-SD3-05).

---

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

Profiles per M0 Standard 13 are aggregate, not named individuals.

**Profile 1: Pennie-enrolled household losing EPTC subsidy in West Philadelphia (MC61 BOTH/AND PRIMARY candidate).**

*Constituent type:* A self-employed household of two (one self-employed worker at approximately $70,000 income; one non-working spouse age 58; no employer coverage available) in the West Philadelphia Core sub-area; household income above the new EPTC-expiration affordability threshold; household enrolled in Pennie coverage 2024-2025 with substantial EPTC subsidy. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core. *Triggering event:* December 31, 2025 EPTC expiration; 2026 plan-year premium increase approximately doubled.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Household receives 2026 Pennie renewal notice with substantial premium increase; explores Pennie 2026 plan options through pennie.com or 1-844-844-8040; encounters new federal-level affordability cliff with no PTC subsidy available above the threshold; PA Act 54 of 2024 State Health Insurance Exchange Affordability Program enacted but unfunded as of May 2026 [Source 41]. Household compares Pennie plan options to alternative coverage routes (COBRA from prior employer if applicable; short-term limited-duration insurance; employer-provided coverage via spouse). Household either enrolls in bronze-tier Pennie plan with higher cost-sharing, terminates Pennie coverage and becomes uninsured (joining the documented ~85,000 OE 2026 terminations [Sources 41, 47]), or holds pending 2026 congressional action [Source 42].

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* 2025 enhanced PTC subsidy for similar household structure: $600-$800/month subsidy (illustrative based on documented EPTC architecture; F21-SD3-06 for primary-source confirmation). 2026 unsubsidized premium for 60-year-old couple: $2,000-$3,000/month per Pennie Director Trolley [Source 42; D]. EPTC cutoff: $62,600 single / $84,600 couple [Source 42; D]. Pennie average premium increase 2026 vs 2025: 102% [Sources 41, 43, 47; D]. PA Act 54 of 2024 affordability program: enacted but unfunded as of May 2026 [Source 41; D].

*Outcome.* If household elects bronze-tier coverage, continued ACA architecture access but with higher out-of-pocket exposure; preventive services covered at no cost-sharing per ACA Section 2713 [cross-reference D2 SD2]; behavioral-health coverage at MHPAEA parity. If household elects to terminate, joins the uninsured population with deferred-care exposure; documented per Pennie statement that terminating households "are now uninsured" [Source 41]. The MC61 Both/And operationalizes here: substantive ACA marketplace architecture continues operative through Pennie state-based exchange AND structural disruption from EPTC expiration produces documented coverage loss at scale.

**Profile 2: Commercial group health enrollee navigating ERISA-preempted self-funded plan with MH need in South Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* A working household with employer-sponsored coverage through one parent's mid-sized employer (self-funded ERISA-preempted plan administered by a major insurance carrier); household income approximately $85,000; two school-age children; one parent newly diagnosed with depression requiring outpatient behavioral-health treatment. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Enrollee accesses behavioral-health provider directory through plan portal; finds limited in-network options for psychiatrist (waitlist of 8-12 weeks documented as common pattern); finds therapist via in-network roster; encounters prior-authorization requirements for medication management and ongoing therapy. MHPAEA framework requires parity between MH/SUD and medical/surgical benefits [29 U.S.C. § 1185a]; enforcement at ERISA-preempted plans is federal (DOL EBSA + HHS + Treasury joint) rather than state (PA Insurance Department lacks jurisdiction over self-funded ERISA plans). Enrollee may pursue MHPAEA enforcement complaint with DOL EBSA at askebsa.dol.gov or file private MHPAEA claim through ERISA Section 502(a) judicial enforcement.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Self-funded ERISA plan share of employer-sponsored coverage: ~60% nationally [SI: structural inference; F21-SD3-07 for PA-specific magnitude]. MHPAEA parity at financial requirements, treatment limitations, network access [29 U.S.C. § 1185a; D]. Behavioral-health in-network waitlist patterns: 8-12 weeks common nationally [SI; F21-SD3-08 for PA-3-specific]. ERISA Section 502(a) judicial enforcement [29 U.S.C. § 1132; D].

*Outcome.* Enrollee receives covered MH services but at delayed access; cost-sharing at parity with medical-surgical per MHPAEA; out-of-pocket exposure governed by deductible-and-coinsurance architecture. If enrollee pursues out-of-network MH treatment due to access barriers, in-network parity remedy under MHPAEA is available but procedurally complex.

**Profile 3: Lawfully-present-immigrant household navigating OBBBA-modified Pennie eligibility in Kensington.**

*Constituent type:* A working household of three (two working-age adults; one school-age child; lawfully-present immigrant status; household income approximately $48,000 placing them above Medicaid expansion threshold but below pre-OBBBA EPTC cliff); enrolled in Pennie 2025 with EPTC subsidy. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Core (Kensington area). *Triggering event:* OBBBA H.R. 1 of 2025 modification to ACA marketplace immigrant eligibility; Pennie has communicated to lawfully-present immigrants that they may no longer qualify for financial savings [Source 45].

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Household receives Pennie communication explaining the OBBBA-driven immigrant-eligibility modification [Source 45]; reviews 2026 Pennie account; if eligibility-affected, faces unsubsidized premium architecture comparable to Profile 1 above. Household options: full-premium Pennie plan if affordable; Pennie plan termination → uninsured status; coverage through alternative routes (employer-sponsored if available; FQHC sliding-fee scale per D21 SD5 architecture). Pennie operates customer service in multiple languages and maintains community-based-organization referral architecture.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* OBBBA immigrant-eligibility modification: lawfully-present immigrants may no longer qualify for Premium Tax Credit savings under OBBBA per Pennie communication [Source 45; D]. Pre-OBBBA architecture: lawfully-present immigrants were eligible for Pennie / federal marketplace coverage with PTC eligibility per income-based criteria. Specific magnitude of PA-3 affected population F-flagged (F21-SD3-09).

*Outcome.* Household faces coverage-architecture disruption analogous to Profile 1 plus the additional structural layer of immigrant-eligibility modification; cross-reference D12 SD2 for substantive eligibility-architecture analysis. The MC61 Both/And operationalizes in modified form: substantive ACA marketplace architecture continues at the program level AND structural OBBBA-driven eligibility-modification affects subgroups including lawfully-present immigrants in addition to the EPTC-expiration affected population.

**Profile 4: PA-3 small-business owner navigating Pennie individual market with self-employment income in Northwest Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* A self-employed small business owner (single-person LLC; income approximately $58,000; no employees; below the pre-OBBBA EPTC eligibility cutoff for some subsidy but above the Medicaid expansion threshold). *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* Northwest. *Triggering event:* Annual Pennie 2026 plan renewal at altered subsidy architecture; small-business-tax-architecture decisions interact with Pennie coverage decisions.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Self-employed individual evaluates Pennie 2026 plan options; eligible for some PTC subsidy but at substantially smaller amount than 2025 EPTC; faces approximately doubled premium per Pennie data [Source 41]. Considers alternative routes: Pennie plan at reduced subsidy + higher net premium; terminating Pennie coverage and going uninsured (the cost-cutting decision documented in Pennie reporting per the Franklin County PA case of cancelling a $12,000 salesperson contract after premium jump from $70 to $240) [Source 42]; joining a spouse's employer-sponsored plan if available.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Self-employed share of total Pennie population not separately disaggregated; structural inference from Pennie reporting that self-employed and small-business population is a substantial Pennie demographic [Source 42; SI]. Pennie premium increases for self-employed: documented case showing $70 → $240 premium ($170 monthly increase or 243%) [Source 42; D].

*Outcome.* Individual either enrolls at higher net premium (with associated economic-activity-reducing impact including the documented $12,000 contract termination cited in Source 42) or terminates Pennie coverage. The economic-impact dimension is the additional analytical layer for the small-business / self-employed population: coverage decisions affect business-investment decisions in ways not visible at household-only analysis.

---

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most visible feature of ACA marketplace delivery in PA-3 in 2026 is what didn't happen in Washington. For nearly five years, Pennsylvania marketplace enrollees benefited from enhanced premium tax credits totaling approximately $600 million annually in Pennsylvania alone and drove Pennie enrollment up by 50% between 2021 and 2025 [Source 41]. The enhanced tax credits expired at midnight December 31, 2025. Congress did not act to extend them before the deadline. The U.S. House voted in early 2026 to extend the credits for three years, but the Senate did not act in time, and the substantive delivery-side flow-through to PA-3 households was already operative for the 2026 plan year — average premium increased 102%, 85,000 Pennie enrollees terminated coverage during Open Enrollment, and 60,000 more dropped coverage between OE close and May 1, 2026 [Sources 41, 44, 47]. The mechanism is unusual in the project's analytical territory: most delivery-side disruption emerges from administrative-vulnerability decisions at federal or state regulatory agencies; this disruption emerged from congressional inaction at a discrete legislative-deadline moment.

The most common misunderstanding about ACA marketplace coverage in Pennsylvania in 2026 is that "marketplace" and "Medicaid" operate on similar delivery architectures. They do not. Medicaid managed care operates through a closed roster of state-contracted MCOs with state-administered provider networks and a single managed-care framework (Section 2 of SD2). Marketplace coverage operates through a wider menu of commercial carriers (Independence Blue Cross, AmeriHealth Pennsylvania, Highmark, UPMC, Geisinger, Aetna, Oscar) each operating their own commercial provider networks with substantial overlap but also substantial product-by-product variation. Each carrier offers multiple metal tiers (bronze, silver, gold, platinum) at substantially different cost-sharing structures. Pennie's role is marketplace operations — eligibility verification, plan certification, customer service, federal-IRS reconciliation interface — rather than delivery-side care administration. This is why the EPTC expiration's delivery-side flow-through is so visible at the financial-architecture layer (premiums, subsidies, cost-sharing) rather than at the operational-delivery layer (provider networks, prior authorization, coverage scope) — the substantive provider-side delivery architecture is largely unchanged from 2025 to 2026 plan year; what changed is the financial-architecture overlay that determined who could afford the coverage.

The human consequence visible in 2026 is documented at scale and concentrated at three populations. First, households above the prior EPTC cutoff (approximately $62,600 single / $84,600 couple) who lost all subsidy: 102% average premium increase landed full-force on this population, and the over-55 portion — given ACA age-rating architecture — faced the largest absolute premium increases (60-year-old couple from $500-$600/month to $2,000-$3,000/month [Source 42]). Second, households just below 150% FPL whose income reporting and verification became more demanding under OBBBA-modified architecture: 19,571 PA enrollees in this income band dropped coverage during OE 2026 (16.7% of the income-band total) [Source 43]. Third, lawfully-present immigrant households who may no longer qualify for any subsidy under OBBBA architectural modifications [Source 45]. The three populations are non-overlapping; the cumulative coverage-loss magnitude flows through to safety-net institutions at SD5 (where uninsured patients seek FQHC and emergency-department care) and to academic medical centers at SD4 (where uncompensated care and emergency care fall on hospital cost structures).

The most analytically important feature visible at SD3 is the legislative-deadline mechanism. Most healthcare-delivery-architecture analytical territory operates at slowly-shifting regulatory or administrative variables — CMS rulemaking trajectories, agency staffing levels, MHPAEA enforcement intensity. The EPTC expiration was a discrete legislative event: Congress had until December 31, 2025 to act; Congress did not act; delivery-side flow-through began immediately at OE 2026 enrollment. Federal House representation in this territory is unusually direct — appropriation legislation, statutory extension legislation, or technical-corrections legislation can immediately modify the delivery architecture in ways that administrative engagement cannot. The pending Senate action on the House-passed three-year EPTC extension is the most operative federal-engagement variable at SD3 as of the drafting date.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G21-SD3-01: IRA Enhanced Premium Tax Credit December 31, 2025 expiration delivery-side coverage loss (MC61 BOTH/AND PRIMARY candidate).**
*Finding — substantive contribution:* Pennie operates as Pennsylvania's official ACA marketplace under PA Act 42 of 2019; 2025 peak enrollment ~500,000 [Sources 41, 44]; OE 2026 delivered ~80,000 new enrollments [Source 47]; standard (non-enhanced) Premium Tax Credit architecture continues operative for income-qualifying households under 400% FPL [Source 45].
*Finding — structural disruption:* IRA Enhanced PTCs expired December 31, 2025 per congressional non-extension [Sources 41, 45, 49]. 2026 average Pennie premium increase 102% [Sources 41, 43, 47]; Pennie enrollment declined from ~500,000 in 2025 to 452,525 as of May 1, 2026 with 145,000+ cumulative cancellations [Source 44]; affected population concentrated at age 55+, EPTC-cliff (above $62,600/$84,600 thresholds), and rural [Sources 42, 43, 47]. US House passed three-year extension in early 2026; Senate action pending [Source 42].
*Evidence type:* [D] for documented Pennie enrollment data and 102% premium increase; [D] for EPTC expiration statutory architecture; [SD] for documented demographic concentration of affected population.
*Confidence:* HIGH for documented coverage-loss magnitude; MEDIUM for forward trajectory pending Senate action.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct legislative authority on EPTC extension; pending Senate trajectory + presidential signature is the principal federal-engagement variable. PA Republican Representatives Fitzpatrick (PA-1), Mackenzie (PA-7), and Bresnahan (PA-8) crossed party lines on House extension vote [Source 42]. *T21-SD3-01: US Senate action on House-passed three-year EPTC extension; presidential signature posture; final-form architecture trajectory.*

*Verification update (2026-05-15): Phase 3 confirms Phase 1 framing on EPTC Senate-disposition trajectory. House-passed three-year extension (H.R. 1834 "Health Subsidies Extension Measure"; January 8, 2026; 230–196; 17 Republicans crossing) remains pending in Senate as of verification date; Senate alternative CARE Act ("Consumer Affordability and Responsibility Enhancement Act"; 2-year extension + minimum premium payments + income caps + lawfully-present-noncitizen exclusion) draft circulating since January 2026 but not advanced to Senate floor as of May 2026; earlier December 2025 Senate proposals (S. 3385 Lower Health Care Costs Act 3-year extension; S. 3386 HSA alternative) failed to clear 60-vote threshold. Pennie figures confirmed to four-day-window precision via Pennie self-reported data (ACA Signups aggregation, May 11, 2026): total enrollment 452,525 as of May 1, 2026; 85,000 cancellations during OE 2026 + ~60,000 post-OE cancellations through May 1, 2026 = 145,000+ cumulative; 102% average premium increase 2026. Structural-direction of finding confirmed at HIGH for documented coverage-loss magnitude; MEDIUM for forward trajectory pending Senate action. MC61 candidate Both/And designation remains pending lead direction at batch checkpoint review; T21-SD3-01 carries to embedded Temporal-Changes Sidecar as pre-window-date-locked vector point per Standard 18.A; see MC61 binding-scope walk.*

**<a id="g21-sd3-02"></a>G21-SD3-02: OBBBA H.R. 1 of 2025 ACA marketplace verification and immigrant-eligibility modifications delivery-side flow-through.**
*Finding:* OBBBA modifies ACA marketplace verification procedures, increases income-misreporting penalties, and modifies eligibility for lawfully-present immigrants who may no longer qualify for Premium Tax Credit savings [Source 45]. Pennie has communicated these changes to affected enrollees during OE 2026 [Source 45].
*Evidence type:* [D] for OBBBA statutory architecture and Pennie communication; [F] for PA-3 magnitude of affected lawfully-present-immigrant population pending primary-source.
*Confidence:* HIGH for federal architecture; MEDIUM for PA-3-specific magnitude.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct authority on OBBBA technical corrections and on CMS implementation oversight. PA-state-level engagement on Pennie's affected-enrollee outreach and on Act 54 of 2024 affordability program funding (cross-reference Source 41) is the state-level locus.

**<a id="g21-sd3-03"></a>G21-SD3-03: Pennsylvania State Health Insurance Exchange Affordability Program (Act 54 of 2024) appropriation gap.**
*Finding:* Pennsylvania enacted the State Health Insurance Exchange Affordability Program through Act 54 of 2024 [Source 41]; the program is established but unfunded as of May 2026. Once funded, the program would provide state-level premium assistance supplementing whatever federal PTC architecture is in effect [Source 41].
*Evidence type:* [D].
*Confidence:* HIGH.
*Representation implication:* State-level engagement (PA General Assembly budget cycle 2025-2027) is the principal locus. Federal House representation engagement at federal PTC-architecture level would interact with state-level affordability program design; PA Act 54 is intended to supplement (not replace) federal PTC architecture.

**G21-SD3-04: MHPAEA commercial implementation network-adequacy and enforcement gap.**
*Finding:* Mental Health Parity and Addiction Equity Act requires commercial group health plans and ACA marketplace plans to provide MH/SUD benefits at parity with medical/surgical [29 U.S.C. § 1185a]. Documented national patterns establish that commercial behavioral-health network participation rates are systematically lower than medical/surgical despite MHPAEA's parity requirement. PA-3-specific MHPAEA enforcement disposition F-flagged (F21-SD3-08); cross-reference D21 SD6 for substantive behavioral-health delivery analysis; D3 prior-method verified for substantive MHPAEA implementation territory.
*Evidence type:* [D] for MHPAEA statutory architecture; [SI] for documented national network-participation patterns; [F] for PA-3 magnitudes.
*Confidence:* HIGH for structural feature; MEDIUM for PA-3-specific magnitudes.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct legislative authority and oversight responsibility on MHPAEA enforcement at DOL EBSA, HHS, and Treasury; PA Insurance Department enforces PA Mental Health Parity Act on PA-regulated commercial plans. *T21-SD3-02: MHPAEA federal enforcement administrative posture under Trump CMS / DOL / HHS / Treasury — 2026-2027 rulemaking and enforcement trajectory.*

**<a id="g21-sd3-05"></a>G21-SD3-05: No Surprises Act IDR architecture operational complexity at PA-3 hospital-affiliated ancillary providers.**
*Finding:* No Surprises Act effective January 1, 2022 protects consumers from balance billing for emergency services and out-of-network ancillary providers at in-network facilities [P.L. 116-260 Div. BB]; IDR architecture handles payer-provider disputes. Documented patterns establish IDR is operationally complex; specific PA-3 NSA disposition F-flagged (F21-SD3-10).
*Evidence type:* [D] for statutory architecture; [SI] for documented operational complexity.
*Confidence:* HIGH for statutory feature; MEDIUM for PA-3 specifics.
*Representation implication:* Federal CMS has rulemaking authority on IDR architecture; federal House representation can engage at CMS oversight layer and NSA technical-corrections legislation. Cross-reference D21 SD4.

**<a id="g21-sd3-06"></a>G21-SD3-06: PA-3 ACA marketplace and commercial coverage sub-area-disaggregated data gap.**
*Finding:* PA-3-specific Pennie and commercial-coverage enrollment, premium-cost variation, and EPTC-affected population are not retrievable at the four-sub-area resolution from public-facing data products. Pennie's released Congressional District-level data includes rate-increase analysis [Source 43] but not enrollee-count disaggregation. Per Standard 6 the limitation is stated; PA-3 magnitudes F-flagged (F21-SD3-02, F21-SD3-03, F21-SD3-05).
*Evidence type:* [SI] for structural inference; [F] for sub-area magnitudes.
*Confidence:* MEDIUM.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation engagement at federal data-product accessibility (CMS marketplace data; ACS); state-level engagement at Pennie data publication architecture.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**What the legal framework provides or promises.** The Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act establishes a federal architecture for individual-market and small-group-market health coverage: marketplace operations (federal or state-based exchange); Premium Tax Credit (26 U.S.C. § 36B) and Cost-Sharing Reduction (42 U.S.C. § 18071) financial-assistance architecture; essential health benefits framework; pre-existing-condition exclusion prohibition; medical loss ratio requirements; rate review architecture. Pennsylvania's state-based exchange (Pennie) operates under PA Act 42 of 2019. MHPAEA requires parity between MH/SUD and medical-surgical benefits at commercial plans; the No Surprises Act protects against balance billing; ERISA governs the self-funded employer-plan portion of the commercial market.

**What PA-3 constituents actually receive.** As of May 1, 2026, approximately 452,525 Pennsylvanians are enrolled in Pennie [Source 44]; PA-3-specific share is approximately 30,000-50,000 (structural inference; F21-SD3-02). The 2026 plan-year coverage continues operative through Pennie with smaller standard (non-enhanced) PTC available for income-qualifying enrollees and unsubsidized premium architecture above the $62,600/$84,600 cutoff. The December 31, 2025 EPTC expiration produced documented coverage loss at scale — 145,000+ cumulative Pennie cancellations through May 1, 2026 [Source 44]; affected population concentrates at age 55+, EPTC-cliff income bands, and rural populations [Sources 42, 43, 47]. Commercial-group-health coverage continues operative through employer-sponsored plans (~60% self-funded ERISA; remainder fully insured under state regulation). MHPAEA parity continues operative at the statutory level but documented network-participation gaps persist. No Surprises Act consumer protections continue operative with IDR operational complexity.

**What accounts for the gap.** Four structural features account for the gap between framework and received delivery: (a) congressional non-extension of IRA Enhanced PTCs produced a discrete delivery-side affordability cliff at January 1, 2026 — average 102% premium increase, 145,000+ Pennie cancellations [Sources 41, 44]; (b) OBBBA H.R. 1 of 2025 modifies marketplace verification procedures, income-misreporting penalties, and lawfully-present-immigrant eligibility, adding administrative-vulnerability layers [Source 45]; (c) MHPAEA network-participation enforcement gap means parity at the statutory level does not translate to parity at operational delivery for behavioral-health services; (d) ERISA preemption means self-funded employer plans (~60% of employer-sponsored coverage) are federally-enforced-only, placing state-level consumer-protection backstops out of reach. The cumulative effect: substantive ACA framework continues operative AND the financial-architecture overlay (subsidies; verification; immigrant eligibility) has been structurally disrupted by the 2025-2026 legislative cycle, producing the MC61 candidate Both/And designation operationalized in this SD.

**What the gap means for the representation analysis.** Federal House representation operates at the EPTC extension-legislation interface (the most operative federal-engagement variable as of SD3 drafting), at OBBBA technical-corrections and CMS-implementation-oversight layers, and at MHPAEA federal-enforcement-engagement and No Surprises Act IDR-rulemaking layers. PA-state-level texture engagement at Pennie operations, at PA Act 54 of 2024 affordability program funding, and at PA Insurance Department MHPAEA and consumer-protection enforcement is the complementary locus. The pending US Senate action on House-passed three-year EPTC extension is the principal federal-engagement-trajectory variable at SD3. Federal House representation engagement directly affects whether the 2027 plan-year affordability architecture reverts to the prior EPTC framework or continues at the post-expiration architecture; this is the most consequential representation-engagement question at SD3.

# D21 Healthcare Delivery — Sub-Domain 4
## Hospital Institutional Architecture

**Date:** 2026-05-11
**Phase:** 1 (Per-SD Execution; fresh cycle)
**Domain:** 21 — Healthcare Delivery
**Sub-Domain:** 4 of 7
**Cycle position:** Phase 1 SD2-N batch execution; SD1 + SD2 + SD3 complete
**Methodology stack:** M0 v2.2 · M2 v1.2.1 · Skills v3
**Status:** Phase 1 draft — SD4 of SD2-N batch

---

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD4 documents the hospital institutional architecture governing PA-3's academic medical centers and community hospitals: the federal regulatory regime for hospital participation in Medicare and Medicaid (CMS Conditions of Participation, 42 C.F.R. Parts 482-485); the ACGME private accreditation framework for graduate medical education with Medicare hospital-cost-report funding architecture (cross-reference D21 SD1); the IRC § 501(r) tax-exempt nonprofit hospital community-benefit framework (Treasury Reg 1.501(r); ACA § 9007) including financial assistance policy + community health needs assessment + billing and collection requirements + charity care; EMTALA emergency medical treatment requirements (42 U.S.C. § 1395dd); HIPAA institutional architecture (45 C.F.R. Parts 160, 164); the Pennsylvania regulatory regime including PA Department of Health hospital licensing (28 Pa. Code Ch. 101 et seq.), PA Patient Safety Authority and MCARE Act (40 P.S. § 1303.301 et seq.) medical malpractice architecture, and PA Act 77 charity care architecture; and the No Surprises Act delivery-side hospital-institutional interface (cross-reference D21 SD3). The principal regulated entities in PA-3 are the four academic medical center systems: Penn Medicine (Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania / HUP; Penn Presbyterian Medical Center; Pennsylvania Hospital; CHOP-Penn affiliation); Temple Health (Temple University Hospital; Episcopal Campus); Jefferson Health post-2021-Einstein merger (Thomas Jefferson University Hospitals; Einstein Medical Center; Jefferson Methodist; Jefferson Bucks); and Children's Hospital of Philadelphia (CHOP). SD4 also addresses community hospitals operating in the PA-3 geographic area. **Boundary**: D21 SD4 owns hospital institutional architecture and the healthcare-delivery dimension of anchor accountability; D9 SD4 owns PILOET fiscal architecture (principal anchor); D6 Synthesis Section 2 owns five-dimensional anchor framework completion architecture; D11 SD7 owns Penn-SDP institutional partnership architecture; D6 MC-03 SDP AHERA DOJ DPA at G6-SD4-02 owns AHERA criminal enforcement architecture; D8 SD3 owns anchor procurement architecture; D7 SD1 owns anchor real-estate architecture.

**Anchor framework inheritance.** D21 SD4 INHERITS the five-dimensional anchor accountability framework completion documented at D6 Synthesis Section 2 as ESTABLISHED CONTEXT and does NOT re-extend the framework at framework level. D21 SD4 adds the sixth dimension (healthcare-delivery role of anchor institutions) to the five-dimensional framework (D6 environmental compliance + D7 real-estate + D8 procurement + D9 fiscal + D10 employment). The six-dimensional anchor framework treats Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health, and CHOP as multi-role anchor institutions operating across all six accountability dimensions simultaneously; the healthcare-delivery role at SD4 is the most heavily-regulated and most accountability-engaged of the six roles (federal Medicare/Medicaid participation requirements; ACGME accreditation; 501(r) community benefit; EMTALA; state licensure; HIPAA; ACA Section 9007). The six-dimensional framework is completed at D21 SD4; subsequent domains (D15-D17, D21-D26) will inherit the framework rather than re-extend it.

**Regime declaration.** Internet-access regime. Current-state hospital community benefit magnitudes, CHNA cycle status, medical-malpractice litigation activity, ACGME training architecture, anchor partnership program scope, Hospital Price Transparency Rule compliance rates, and CMS regulatory posture claims are expected to be retrieved; specific figures carry sources per Standard 12. The 501(r) statutory architecture, EMTALA framework, CMS CoP architecture at 42 C.F.R. Parts 482-485, and PA hospital licensing framework are framework-level statutory references; specific section citations are sourced where claims are specific rather than framework-level. Mixed-regime drafting prohibited per Standard 1.

**Analytical posture.** Both/And-primary at MC53 (Hospital institutional architecture — 501(r) community benefit + medical debt within-domain analytical-tension). Per D21 substructure §6, MC53 is the within-domain analytical-tension flagged at SD4: substantive 501(r) community benefit + financial assistance policy + community health needs assessment architecture serves PA-3 communities AND structural impact on PA-3 household financial security via revenue cycle / debt collection. Additional Both/And candidate at ACGME training architecture: substantive ACGME architecture serves medical workforce pipeline AND structural impact on specialty distribution + primary-care-pipeline + geographic concentration; ACGME within-domain analytical-tension was deferred to Phase 1 evaluation per substructure §6. **Hold-open-magnitude territory operative** at SD4 per D21 substructure §6 OQ1 — commitment-vs-outcome shape diagnostic for anchor community benefit; 5th confirmed-pending instance after G7-SD1-03 + D8-Q2 + D10-Q1 + D24-Q1 + D11 SD1 Penn Alexander candidate; would constitute 7th confirmed commitment-vs-outcome HOM instance project-wide if confirmed at SD4 (after D6-Q2 as 6th). Phase 1 SD4 confirms or downgrades the HOM diagnostic; closure-by-analytical-assertion of "biggest cause" of community-benefit-commitment-vs-outcome gap remains unsupported per HOM discipline.

**Thread declarations.** Four analytical threads operative at SD4:

- **D21-SD4-Thread A (federal-regulatory anchor architecture for hospital institutional participation).** CMS Conditions of Participation (42 C.F.R. Parts 482-485) condition Medicare/Medicaid hospital participation on compliance with patient rights, quality assurance, infection control, medical staff governance, and discharge planning standards. ACGME accreditation conditions GME participation. IRC § 501(r) (added by ACA § 9007; Treasury Reg 1.501(r)) conditions tax-exempt status on community health needs assessment every three years, financial assistance policy, billing and collection requirements, and emergency medical care policy. EMTALA conditions Medicare participation on emergency-stabilization-and-screening obligations. HIPAA institutional architecture conditions hospital data practices. The federal regulatory floor is the architectural backbone; PA state licensure and PA Patient Safety Authority oversight add state-level texture.

- **D21-SD4-Thread B (six-dimensional anchor accountability framework completion at SD4 — healthcare delivery dimension).** Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health, and CHOP operate as multi-role anchor institutions across the six accountability dimensions (environmental compliance per D6; real estate per D7; procurement per D8; fiscal per D9; employment per D10; healthcare delivery per D21 SD4). The healthcare-delivery dimension is the most heavily-regulated of the six (the federal regulatory architecture at Thread A is uniquely dense for this dimension). Cross-reference D6 Synthesis Section 2 for the five-dimensional anchor framework architecture inherited as ESTABLISHED CONTEXT; D21 SD4 adds the healthcare-delivery dimension without re-extending the framework at framework level.

- **D21-SD4-Thread C (MC53 within-domain analytical-tension at 501(r) community benefit + medical debt).** Substantive 501(r) community-benefit architecture serves PA-3 communities through documented financial assistance policy applications, charity care provision, CHNA-informed implementation programs (the 2025 SEPA Regional CHNA included Penn Medicine, Temple, Jefferson/Einstein, CHOP plus 7 other SEPA health systems [Sources 50, 52, 57]), and community partnerships AND structural impact on PA-3 household financial security via revenue-cycle architecture: medical debt remains a leading cause of personal bankruptcy [Sources 59, 67]; approximately 1 million Pennsylvanians carry medical debt per Shapiro [Source 68]; only 30% of PA hospitals are compliant with federal Hospital Price Transparency Rule per independent audit [Source 64]; the CFPB rule to remove all medical debt from credit reports was vacated by federal court in July 2025 (*Cornerstone Credit Union League v. CFPB*, E.D. Tex.) [Source 60]. The Both/And designation is operative across the SD4 analytical content.

- **D21-SD4-Thread D (commitment-vs-outcome HOM 7th-instance candidate at anchor community benefit).** Per D21 substructure §6 OQ1, anchor community-benefit commitment-vs-outcome shape diagnostic is operative at SD4 — multiple documented commitment mechanisms (501(r) Schedule H community benefit reporting; CHNA implementation strategies; charity care policies with eligibility thresholds; financial assistance policies; community engagement program commitments; uncompensated care reporting) with multiple documented outcome dimensions (actual community benefit dollars; charity care provision; medical debt collection volumes; lawsuits filed; wage garnishments; CHNA implementation outcomes; community-engagement program participation). Multiple mechanisms could each contribute to outcomes; no evidence to rank by primacy. Closure-by-analytical-assertion would distort. Phase 1 SD4 confirms or downgrades. Would constitute 7th confirmed commitment-vs-outcome HOM instance project-wide if confirmed (after G7-SD1-03 + D8-Q2 + D10-Q1 + D24-Q1 + D11 SD1 Penn Alexander candidate + D6-Q2 as 6th).

## Section 2: Authority Chain

The hospital institutional authority chain in PA-3 follows the federal-state-local layered architecture with substantial federal regulatory density at hospital-institutional layer.

**Constitutional Foundation.** U.S. Constitution Article I, Section 8 (Spending Clause) authorizes federal conditions on Medicare/Medicaid hospital participation; Tenth Amendment reserves state hospital licensure authority. No constitutional tension specific to SD4 territory operative at PA-3 in 2026.

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

- **Social Security Act Titles XVIII and XIX (42 U.S.C. § 1395 et seq.; § 1396 et seq.).** Federal Medicare and Medicaid program authorization includes federal authority to set Conditions of Participation for hospital participation. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH at CMS regulatory implementation*.

- **EMTALA (42 U.S.C. § 1395dd).** Emergency Medical Treatment and Active Labor Act requires Medicare-participating hospitals to provide medical screening examinations to all individuals presenting to the emergency department and to stabilize emergency conditions before transfer or discharge. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at CMS enforcement and at HHS OIG investigations*.

- **IRC § 501(r) (added by ACA § 9007; codified at 26 U.S.C. § 501(r)).** Tax-exempt status conditions for nonprofit hospitals: (a) community health needs assessment every three years with implementation strategy [Source 52]; (b) financial assistance policy (eligibility criteria, application procedures, basis for amounts charged); (c) billing and collection requirements (no extraordinary collection actions without effort to determine FAP eligibility); (d) emergency medical care policy. Treasury Regulation 1.501(r) implements. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH at IRS enforcement (Form 990 Schedule H reporting) and at Treasury rulemaking*.

- **No Surprises Act (P.L. 116-260 Div. BB).** Hospital institutional interface: protects consumers from balance billing for emergency services and certain out-of-network services at in-network facilities; establishes IDR architecture for payer-provider billing disputes. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE*. Cross-reference D21 SD3.

- **HIPAA (45 C.F.R. Parts 160, 164).** Hospital data practices framework; Privacy Rule, Security Rule, Breach Notification Rule. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **Hospital Price Transparency Rule (45 C.F.R. § 180.40 et seq.).** Requires hospitals to publish payer-specific negotiated rates, gross charges, and cash discount prices. Only 30% of PA hospitals compliant per independent audit [Source 64]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at CMS enforcement*.

- **Affordable Care Act § 9007.** The 501(r) tax-exempt hospital framework authorization. *Statutory stability HIGH per *NFIB v. Sebelius* / *King v. Burwell* / *California v. Texas* ACA-preservation rulings*.

- **OBBBA H.R. 1 of 2025 hospital-affecting provisions.** Provider-tax architecture (Section 71115 / 71117; cross-reference D21 SD2 + D9 SD4) affects hospital-side Medicaid financing architecture; $50B Rural Health Transformation Fund ($10B annually 2026-2030) provides offset funding [Source 22]. Cross-reference D9 SD4 and D21 SD2 for substantive analysis. *Statutory stability HIGH at enactment; administrative vulnerability HIGH at CMS implementation*.

**Federal Agency Layer.**

- **Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS).** 7500 Security Boulevard, Baltimore, MD 21244. Administers Conditions of Participation, EMTALA enforcement (delegated to State Survey Agencies plus CMS regional offices), Hospital Price Transparency Rule, IDR architecture under NSA. *per Standard 13.C carve-out: CMS Region 3 (Philadelphia) Regional Administrator and CMS state-survey-agency liaison identification deferred to source-grounded surfacing in execution; named CMS officials in enforcement actions per Federal Register notices and OIG investigations carry source-grounded carve-out per Standard 13.C v2.2*. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH*.

- **U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) Office of Inspector General (OIG).** Audits hospital community benefit compliance with 501(r); investigates EMTALA enforcement and Medicare fraud-and-abuse at hospital institutional level. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **U.S. Department of Treasury, Internal Revenue Service Exempt Organizations Division.** Administers 501(r) compliance through Form 990 Schedule H review; potential revocation of tax-exempt status for substantial non-compliance. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **U.S. Department of Justice (DOJ).** Investigates EMTALA violations; investigates anti-kickback statute and Stark law violations at hospital institutional level; investigates AHERA criminal-enforcement architecture per D6 MC-03 SDP DOJ DPA at G6-SD4-02 (cross-reference D6 verified file for PRINCIPAL ANCHOR substantive analysis). *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **Accreditation Council for Graduate Medical Education (ACGME).** 401 N. Michigan Avenue, Suite 2000, Chicago, IL 60611. Private accreditation body; accredits residency and fellowship training programs at Penn Medicine, Temple, Jefferson, CHOP; ACGME accreditation conditions Medicare GME funding via hospital cost reports (cross-reference D21 SD1 GME architecture). *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **The Joint Commission.** 1 Renaissance Boulevard, Oakbrook Terrace, IL 60181. Private accreditation body; accreditation provides deemed status for Medicare CoP compliance for participating hospitals. *Administrative vulnerability LOW*.

**State Statutory Layer.**

- **PA Health Care Facilities Act (35 P.S. § 448.101 et seq.).** Authorizes PA Department of Health to license hospitals; sets state regulatory floor for hospital operations. *Statutory stability HIGH*.

- **28 Pa. Code Ch. 101 et seq. (PA hospital licensing regulations).** Implements the Health Care Facilities Act through PA DOH regulations; sets physical-plant requirements, medical-staff requirements, infection-control requirements, governance requirements. *Statutory stability HIGH*.

- **PA MCARE Act (40 P.S. § 1303.101 et seq.).** Medical Care Availability and Reduction of Error Act establishes PA Patient Safety Authority and PA medical malpractice architecture; MCARE Fund provides excess medical malpractice coverage; 2024 tort reform legislation reshaped some claim-limitations architecture [Source 66]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at PA legislative tort-reform cycle*.

- **PA Act 77 of 1968 (Hospital Charity Care).** Mandates Pennsylvania hospitals to offer financial assistance / charity care programs [Source 59]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **PA Fair Credit Extension Uniformity Act (73 P.S. § 2270.4).** Prohibits unfair and deceptive practices in debt collection including medical debt collection [Source 60]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW*.

- **PA wage garnishment prohibition (42 Pa. C.S. § 8127).** Prohibits wage garnishment for medical debts in Pennsylvania [Source 63]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW*.

- **PA medical debt credit reporting prohibition.** Pennsylvania prohibits reporting patient medical debt to credit agencies; debt is voided if reported in violation of PA law [Source 64]. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW*.

- **HUP v. Commonwealth (charitable-exemption standard; PA Act 55 of 1997 five-part test).** Cross-reference D9 SD4 for substantive analysis. *Statutory stability HIGH at common-law architecture; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at PA county property-tax assessment*.

- **Act 47 of 2025 / 2024 tort reform legislation.** Reshapes certain medical malpractice claim-limitations architecture; shortened deadline to 18 months for certain claims against government-affiliated healthcare entities or cases involving administrative exhaustion requirements [Source 66]. *Statutory stability MODERATE*.

**State Agency Layer.**

- **Pennsylvania Department of Health (PA DOH).** 625 Forster Street, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Licenses hospitals under PA Health Care Facilities Act; conducts surveys under federal Medicare CoP delegation; investigates patient-safety complaints; oversees state-level public health architecture. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **Pennsylvania Patient Safety Authority.** 333 Market Street, Lobby Level, Harrisburg, PA 17101. Receives patient safety event reports; analyzes patterns; issues guidance; coordinates with PA DOH and federal CMS. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **Pennsylvania Insurance Department (PA Insurance Commissioner Michael Humphreys per Source 49 official capacity).** 1326 Strawberry Square, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Regulates the commercial-insurance side of hospital reimbursement; administers MCARE Fund operations; cross-reference D21 SD3 No Surprises Act state interface. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **PA Office of Attorney General.** Strawberry Square, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Investigates hospital fraud, anti-trust violations, and consumer-protection violations in healthcare context. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

- **Philadelphia Code (Title 6 — Health; Title 17 — Real Estate; Title 19 — Finance).** Authorizes Philadelphia Department of Public Health operational architecture; sets local real-estate-tax architecture (cross-reference D9 SD4 for PILOET architecture as PRINCIPAL ANCHOR). Local statutory layer is operationally light for hospital institutional architecture; principal authority at state and federal levels.

**Local Agency Layer.**

- **Philadelphia Department of Public Health (PDPH).** 1101 Market Street, Philadelphia, PA 19107. Cross-reference D21 SD5 for safety-net delivery architecture and D2 SD6 for federal § 330 program architecture. PDPH operates as a regulatory and partnership entity for hospital institutional architecture at the local level; key partnership architecture includes the SEPA Regional CHNA spearheaded by the Health Care Improvement Foundation and PDPH [Source 52].

- **City of Philadelphia Department of Revenue.** Administers Philadelphia property-tax architecture relevant to anchor-institution charitable-exemption analysis (cross-reference D9 SD4 PILOET PRINCIPAL ANCHOR for substantive analysis). *per Standard 13.C carve-out: Mayor Cherelle Parker administration officials in healthcare-policy capacity identified per Mayor's office communications; named officials in official capacity per source-grounded surfacing*.

**Constituent Layer.** Aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5: PA-3 patient navigating hospital financial assistance policy at Penn Medicine; PA-3 patient experiencing medical debt collection at Temple Health; PA-3 patient navigating EMTALA emergency-stabilization architecture at Jefferson Health; PA-3 family of pediatric patient at CHOP navigating CHNA-informed community programs.
