## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

The hospital institutional pathway for a PA-3 constituent depends on the trigger (scheduled care vs. emergency; outpatient vs. inpatient; insured vs. uninsured) and on the institutional choice (Penn Medicine vs. Temple Health vs. Jefferson Health vs. CHOP vs. community hospital). The institutional pathway is the substantive delivery architecture; the financing-side architecture is owned by SD1-SD3 (Medicare; Medicaid; ACA marketplace + commercial). D21 SD4 traces the constituent-to-institution interaction across the regulatory-architectural touch points.

*Triggering condition.* A PA-3 constituent enters hospital institutional architecture under documented pathways: (a) emergency department presentation for emergency medical condition triggering EMTALA screening obligations [42 U.S.C. § 1395dd]; (b) scheduled inpatient admission for elective procedure with insurance pre-authorization; (c) outpatient department visit for specialty consult, diagnostic imaging, or treatment; (d) labor and delivery; (e) inter-facility transfer from community hospital, FQHC, or other facility; (f) hospital-affiliated outpatient surgery or procedure at an ambulatory surgical center.

*EMTALA emergency stabilization pathway.* PA-3 constituent presenting to an emergency department at any Medicare-participating hospital (every Penn, Temple, Jefferson, CHOP facility plus all community hospitals) receives EMTALA-mandated medical screening examination regardless of insurance status or ability to pay. If an emergency medical condition is identified, the hospital must provide stabilizing treatment within its capabilities or arrange transfer to a higher-acuity facility (EMTALA-protected transfer). Cross-reference D21 SD7 emergency clinical delivery for substantive analysis. The constituent is liable for the cost of care; financial assistance policy applications may be initiated either at admission or post-discharge per the hospital's 501(r) FAP architecture.

*501(r) financial assistance policy pathway.* For uninsured or under-insured PA-3 constituents receiving hospital care, the financial-assistance-policy (FAP) architecture is the principal mechanism for cost-sharing protection. Each 501(c)(3) hospital must adopt and publish a FAP with: (a) eligibility criteria typically based on household income relative to FPL (Penn Medicine FAP at illustrative threshold; Temple FAP at illustrative threshold; Jefferson FAP at illustrative threshold; CHOP FAP at illustrative threshold — specific thresholds are F-flagged for retrieval, F21-SD4-01); (b) application procedures including required documentation (income verification, household composition, residency); (c) basis for amounts charged to FAP-eligible patients (typically AGB / Amounts Generally Billed methodology per 501(r)); (d) billing-and-collection procedures including a documented "extraordinary collection action" architecture that requires reasonable effort to determine FAP eligibility before pursuing actions like lawsuit or wage garnishment [26 U.S.C. § 501(r)(6); Treasury Reg 1.501(r)-6]. Income thresholds for hospital charity care programs are often 200-400% FPL [Source 60]; documented pattern is that uninsured patients frequently do not know they qualify [Source 60].

*Pathway breakdown at FAP architecture for PA-3 constituents.* The 501(r) FAP architecture is structurally protective but operationally complex: (a) the constituent must know of the FAP and apply; (b) the constituent must produce documentation often within billing-cycle deadlines; (c) the FAP determination is hospital-specific; (d) the constituent's effective protection varies by hospital-specific FAP design within the regulatory floor. Documented gap: many uninsured patients do not apply for FAP they would qualify for [Source 60]; only 30% of PA hospitals are compliant with the federal Hospital Price Transparency Rule [Source 64], so the upstream "how much will this cost" question is structurally opaque before service.

*Medical debt collection pathway.* For PA-3 patients who incur hospital bills not covered by FAP or insurance, the debt-collection pathway operates under: (a) federal Fair Debt Collection Practices Act (15 U.S.C. § 1692 et seq.) [Source 60]; (b) Pennsylvania Fair Credit Extension Uniformity Act (73 P.S. § 2270.4) [Source 60]; (c) PA wage-garnishment-prohibition for medical debt (42 Pa. C.S. § 8127) [Source 63]; (d) PA medical-debt-credit-reporting-prohibition (PA-specific protection — debt is voided if reported in violation of PA law) [Source 64]; (e) PA medical-debt statute of limitations of four years from last payment activity (42 Pa. Consol. Stat. § 5525(7)) [Source 65]. The CFPB rule to remove all medical debt from credit reports was vacated by a federal court in July 2025 (*Cornerstone Credit Union League v. CFPB*, E.D. Tex.) [Source 60]; the prior credit-bureau voluntary changes (paid medical debt removed; unpaid debt under $500 not reported) remain in place. PA Rep. Arvind Venkat (PA House District 30, Allegheny County — outside PA-3 but operating PA-state-level legislation) has introduced medical-debt-relief legislation; Governor Shapiro proposed $4M state investment to erase $400M of medical debt in 2024-25 budget proposal [Source 68].

*Inpatient admission and discharge pathway.* Scheduled or admitted PA-3 constituent navigates: pre-admission insurance verification (if insured); informed-consent documentation; HIPAA notice of privacy practices; admission-and-discharge planning (CMS CoP requirement); inpatient clinical care under medical-staff governance architecture; discharge planning including post-acute care arrangement (HHA, SNF, IRF, LTAC, home with home health, or home without). The CMS Conditions of Participation discharge planning requirement (42 C.F.R. § 482.43) is the structural protection at this transition.

*Specialty consultation and outpatient pathway.* For specialty consult, PA-3 constituent navigates: referral architecture (insurance-required pre-authorization for many specialty services; provider-network participation determining access); specialty consult; diagnostic and treatment plan; potentially ongoing specialty care or care plan handoff back to primary care. Each anchor system operates a comprehensive specialty department roster — Penn Medicine (Abramson Cancer Center; Penn Heart and Vascular Center; cross-reference D21 SD7 for cancer architecture); Temple Health (Temple Lung Center; cardiology; cross-reference D21 SD7); Jefferson Health (Vickie and Jack Farber Institute for Neuroscience; cardiology); CHOP (pediatric specialty across all subspecialties; cross-reference D21 SD7). Provider-network adequacy varies by insurance product; cross-reference D21 SD2 / SD3 for the insurance-side architecture.

*ACGME training architecture pathway (workforce-pipeline interface).* Penn Medicine, Temple, Jefferson, and CHOP collectively host the substantial majority of GME training programs in PA-3; Medicare GME funding flows through hospital cost reports per Medicare GME architecture (cross-reference D21 SD1 GME territory). The training architecture determines specialty-distribution patterns: PA-3 residents-and-fellows in training represent a workforce pipeline that — depending on specialty selection and post-training practice location — contributes to or detracts from PA-3 primary-care and specialty-care access. The ACGME accreditation architecture conditions training-program operation on accreditation standards; the federal Medicare GME architecture conditions training-program funding on hospital-cost-report-validated FTE counts. Cross-reference D21 substructure §6 ACGME Both/And designation — substantive training pipeline AND structural specialty-distribution / primary-care-pipeline / geographic-concentration effects.

*Decision points where the pathway breaks for PA-3 constituents.* The hospital institutional pathway breaks down at several documented points: (a) at the price-opacity layer (only 30% of PA hospitals compliant with federal Hospital Price Transparency Rule [Source 64]; the upstream "how much will this cost" question structurally opaque before service); (b) at the FAP application layer (income thresholds often 200-400% FPL but documented under-application [Source 60]); (c) at the medical-debt-collection layer (federal FDCPA + PA Fair Credit Extension protections operative but documented harassment patterns persist [Source 63]); (d) at the medical-malpractice claim-filing layer (2024 tort reform shortened certain claim windows to 18 months [Source 66]); (e) at the inter-facility-transfer layer (EMTALA-protected transfer is operationally permitted; documented patterns at the safety-net interface include inappropriate-transfer-and-screening concerns); (f) at the discharge-planning layer (post-acute care availability varies; documented patterns of hospital discharge into housing-insecure or homeless conditions affect outcomes); (g) at the ACGME-specialty-distribution layer (primary-care workforce gap in PA-3 is documented downstream pattern of specialty-selection economics).

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**PA-3 anchor hospital institutional roster.** Penn Medicine principal Philadelphia-based hospitals: Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania (HUP) at 3400 Spruce Street; Penn Presbyterian Medical Center at 51 N. 39th Street; Pennsylvania Hospital at 800 Spruce Street [Source 56]. Temple Health principal Philadelphia-based hospitals: Temple University Hospital at 3401 N. Broad Street; Episcopal Campus at 100 E. Lehigh Avenue. Jefferson Health post-2021 Einstein merger principal Philadelphia-based hospitals: Thomas Jefferson University Hospitals at 111 S. 11th Street; Einstein Medical Center at 5501 Old York Road; Jefferson Methodist at 2301 S. Broad Street; Jefferson Bucks (suburban; cross-county). Children's Hospital of Philadelphia: principal Philadelphia hospital at 3401 Civic Center Boulevard; CHOP Care Network with more than 50 locations across PA and NJ [Source 58]. Community hospitals operating in PA-3 geographic area include St. Christopher's Hospital for Children (1601 W. Allegheny Avenue, owned by Tower Health), Roxborough Memorial Hospital (Prime Healthcare), and others. Wills Eye Hospital (840 Walnut Street, ophthalmology specialty hospital) operates as standalone specialty hospital in PA-3.

**2025 SEPA Regional CHNA participating systems.** Eleven SEPA health systems participated in the 2025 Regional Community Health Needs Assessment spearheaded by the Health Care Improvement Foundation and Philadelphia Department of Public Health [Sources 50, 52, 57]: Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, ChristianaCare – West Grove, Doylestown Health, Grand View Health, Jefferson Health (including Einstein Health post-merger), Main Line Health, Penn Medicine, St. Christopher's Hospital for Children, Temple University Health, Trinity Health Mid-Atlantic, Wills Eye Hospital. The Regional CHNA cycle is the 501(r) every-three-years architecture; the 2025 cycle informs 2025-2027 implementation strategies at each participating system.

**PA-3 hospital community benefit aggregate magnitude.** Per documented patterns, Pennsylvania nonprofit hospital community benefit aggregate magnitude is in the billions of dollars annually across the four academic medical center systems (specific PA-3 anchor community benefit aggregate magnitude per Form 990 Schedule H is F-flagged for retrieval, F21-SD4-02). Cross-reference D9 SD4 PILOET architecture for substantive fiscal-anchor analysis; D9 SD4-Q2 held-open-magnitude for aggregate magnitude territory. D6-Q2 sixth confirmed commitment-vs-outcome HOM instance is the environmental-compliance dimension for the anchor framework (cross-reference D6 verified file); D21 SD4 7th HOM candidate is at the healthcare-delivery community-benefit dimension.

**Medical debt in Pennsylvania.** Approximately 1 million Pennsylvanians carry some kind of medical debt per Shapiro 2024-25 budget address [Source 68]. 2024 Commonwealth Fund survey: 83% of respondents with medical debt had loads of $500 or more; nearly half were paying off $2,000 or more; hospital care was the most frequently cited source [Source 67]. Pennsylvania-specific medical-debt-collection-lawsuit volume by hospital is F-flagged for retrieval (F21-SD4-03).

**Hospital Price Transparency Rule compliance.** Only 30% of PA hospitals are compliant with the federal Hospital Price Transparency Rule per an independent audit [Source 64]. Specific compliance status by Penn Medicine, Temple, Jefferson, CHOP is F-flagged (F21-SD4-04).

**Medical malpractice activity at PA-3 anchor hospitals.** HUP $182.7M/183M verdict (April 2023; upheld February 2024; subject to ongoing appeal as of November 2025) is the single largest medical malpractice verdict in Pennsylvania history [Source 61, 62]. Temple University Hospital $44.9M verdict (Philadelphia County 2024) [Source 66]. Thomas Jefferson $2.5M verdict (Philadelphia County 2024) [Source 66]. Hahnemann University Hospital $32M settlement (Philadelphia County 2024) — Hahnemann is the closed safety-net hospital (cross-reference D21 SD5 Hahnemann-precedent for substantive analysis) [Source 66]. The 2024 PA tort reform legislation has reshaped certain claim-limitations architecture; shortened deadline to 18 months for certain claims [Source 66].

**ACGME training architecture at PA-3.** Penn Medicine, Temple, Jefferson, and CHOP collectively operate the substantial majority of ACGME-accredited training programs in PA-3 (specific program counts and slot counts are F-flagged for primary-source retrieval; F21-SD4-05). Cross-reference D21 SD1 GME architecture for Medicare GME funding flow.

**PA-3 sub-area variation.** Hospital institutional architecture is geographically distributed across the four sub-areas: HUP, Penn Presbyterian, Pennsylvania Hospital, CHOP, Jefferson (Center City), and Jefferson Methodist (S. Broad) primarily serve the West Philadelphia Core and the Center City interface with South/Southwest; Temple Hospital and Episcopal serve the North/Northwest Core; Einstein Medical Center (now Jefferson Einstein) serves the Northwest. Specialty-care concentration at the academic medical centers means PA-3 sub-area residents must travel across sub-areas for specialty care; geographic-access patterns vary by sub-area resource concentration. Sub-area-disaggregated hospital utilization data F-flagged (F21-SD4-06).

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

Profiles per M0 Standard 13 are aggregate, not named individuals.

**Profile 1: Uninsured PA-3 constituent navigating Penn Medicine financial assistance policy in West Philadelphia (MC53 BOTH/AND PRIMARY substantive contribution).**

*Constituent type:* Working-age adult uninsured (loss of employer-sponsored coverage; income approximately $32,000; single household; West Philadelphia Core sub-area). *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core. *Triggering event:* Emergency department presentation at HUP for acute appendicitis; emergency surgical care delivered under EMTALA stabilization architecture; post-discharge billing exposure.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* HUP delivers EMTALA-mandated medical screening and emergency stabilization regardless of payment status [42 U.S.C. § 1395dd]; constituent receives appendectomy and post-operative care; post-discharge billing process initiates with hospital billing department. Constituent receives notice of Penn Medicine FAP and application procedures per 501(r)(4) architecture; applies for financial assistance based on income relative to FPL; documentation produced (most recent tax return; pay stubs; bank statements). FAP determination process completes; if eligible, amounts billed are at AGB or lower per 501(r)(5); if not eligible at full FAP but eligible at partial discount, hospital applies discount.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* HUP location: 3400 Spruce Street, Philadelphia, PA 19104 [D]. EMTALA medical screening obligation [42 U.S.C. § 1395dd; D]. 501(r) FAP architecture: eligibility criteria, application procedures, AGB methodology [26 U.S.C. § 501(r); D]. Income threshold for hospital charity care often 200-400% FPL [Source 60; D]. Federal-poverty-level threshold for illustrative income $32,000: approximately 242% FPL for single household (illustrative calculation; F21-SD4-07 for current FPL verification). Documented pattern: many uninsured patients do not know they qualify for FAP [Source 60; SI].

*Outcome.* Constituent receives full emergency care; if FAP-eligible at full amount, billing reduced to AGB or less; if partial discount, billing reduced proportionally; if FAP-ineligible, billing at chargemaster or contracted-rate level. The MC53 Both/And operationalizes here: substantive 501(r) FAP architecture provides cost-sharing protection for income-qualifying patients AND structural impact on PA-3 household financial security via the revenue-cycle architecture for FAP-ineligible patients or patients who do not successfully navigate the FAP application — the burden falls heavily on the constituent's navigation capacity within billing-cycle deadlines.

**Profile 2: Medical-debt-collection-affected PA-3 constituent post-Temple Health discharge in North Philadelphia (MC53 BOTH/AND structural impact).**

*Constituent type:* Working-age PA-3 resident with high-deductible employer-sponsored insurance (effective uninsured for first $5,000-$8,000 of medical care annually); income approximately $48,000; small household; North/Northwest Core sub-area. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* North/Northwest Core. *Triggering event:* Inpatient hospitalization at Temple University Hospital for cardiac evaluation; deductible-and-coinsurance exposure of approximately $9,000 unmet at discharge.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Constituent receives full inpatient care under insurance pre-authorization; post-discharge billing begins with payor adjudication; insurance applies plan deductible-and-coinsurance architecture; constituent receives explanation of benefits and provider bill. Hospital billing department initiates payment collection; constituent unable to pay in full; constituent attempts negotiation; if no resolution, hospital may send debt to in-house collection or external collection agency. Constituent encounters FDCPA architecture (federal) + PA Fair Credit Extension Uniformity Act (state) + PA wage-garnishment-prohibition for medical debt (42 Pa. C.S. § 8127) [Source 63]. PA medical-debt-credit-reporting prohibition is protective [Source 64]. Statute of limitations is 4 years from last payment activity (42 Pa. Consol. Stat. § 5525(7)) [Source 65].

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Temple University Hospital location: 3401 N. Broad Street, Philadelphia, PA 19140 [D]. Median high-deductible health plan deductible: $5,000-$8,000 (illustrative based on documented HDHP architecture). PA medical debt statute of limitations: 4 years from last payment activity [42 Pa. Consol. Stat. § 5525(7); Source 65; D]. PA wage garnishment prohibition for medical debt: documented PA-specific protection [Source 63; D]. CFPB rule to remove all medical debt from credit reports vacated July 2025 [Source 60; D].

*Outcome.* Constituent navigates a multi-jurisdictional medical-debt-collection architecture with substantial PA-specific protections (wage-garnishment prohibition; credit-reporting prohibition) but with substantial collection-action-pressure persisting. If constituent pursues hospital FAP (which the constituent likely qualifies for at 248% FPL even with insurance), the post-discharge financial picture can be substantially improved. The MC53 Both/And operationalizes here at the structural-impact dimension: substantive 501(r) FAP architecture exists AND structural impact on PA-3 household financial security via revenue-cycle architecture / debt collection / persistent collection-action pressure documented at meaningful magnitude.

**Profile 3: Pediatric patient family navigating CHOP CHNA-informed community programs in Northwest Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* Family of three (working parents; child age 8 with chronic asthma requiring specialty pulmonology); insurance: family employer-sponsored plan; household income approximately $72,000; Northwest sub-area. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* Northwest. *Triggering event:* Pediatric specialty referral for asthma management; family navigates CHOP pediatric pulmonology pathway and engages with CHOP CHNA-informed community-asthma-management programs documented in the 2025 SEPA Regional CHNA implementation plan [Sources 50, 52].

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Pediatrician refers to CHOP pediatric pulmonology; family schedules consult; CHOP delivers specialty consultation, treatment plan including environmental-asthma-management coordination; family may engage with CHOP-affiliated community programs (cross-reference D11 SD7 SDP-Penn partnership analog; cross-reference D6 environmental health architecture for environmental-asthma-trigger remediation). The CHOP 2025 CHNA implementation plan documents strategies to address community asthma; the family's participation depends on referral pathway and program-capacity.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* CHOP location: 3401 Civic Center Boulevard, Philadelphia, PA 19104 [D]. CHOP CHNA cycle: 501(r) every-three-years per 26 U.S.C. § 501(r)(3); 2025 cycle delivered through 2025 SEPA Regional CHNA [Sources 50, 52, 57; D]. CHOP Care Network 50+ locations across PA and NJ [Source 58; D].

*Outcome.* Family receives specialty pediatric care at CHOP plus access (subject to program capacity and eligibility) to CHOP community-asthma-management programs. The 501(r) CHNA architecture delivers the substantive program design; the implementation reach varies by program-capacity and family-navigation capacity. Cross-reference D6 environmental health architecture for environmental-trigger context (D6 MC-04 environmental justice; D13 SD4 air quality cumulative-burden geography).

**Profile 4: PA-3 patient experiencing EMTALA-protected emergency transfer from Jefferson Health to higher-acuity facility.**

*Constituent type:* Working-age adult uninsured (recent loss of Pennie coverage post-EPTC-expiration per D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01); income approximately $58,000; household of two; South/Southwest sub-area. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* South/Southwest. *Triggering event:* Acute traumatic injury; presentation at Jefferson Methodist Emergency Department; clinical severity exceeds Methodist's trauma-care capability; EMTALA-protected transfer to Thomas Jefferson University Hospital trauma center.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Jefferson Methodist delivers EMTALA medical screening; identifies emergency medical condition exceeding Methodist's stabilization capability; arranges EMTALA-protected transfer to Jefferson Center City trauma center; receiving facility accepts transfer; constituent receives stabilizing trauma care. Post-discharge billing process initiates; constituent uninsured; constituent receives notice of Jefferson FAP and application procedures; cross-reference Profile 1 outcome for FAP-pathway analysis.

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Jefferson Methodist Hospital location: 2301 S. Broad Street, Philadelphia, PA 19148 [D]. Thomas Jefferson University Hospital location: 111 S. 11th Street, Philadelphia, PA 19107 [D]. EMTALA-protected transfer requirements: receiving facility availability; transfer documentation; clinical stabilization to extent of transferring facility's capability [42 U.S.C. § 1395dd; D].

*Outcome.* Constituent receives full emergency stabilization and trauma care across the two-facility EMTALA pathway; post-discharge financial exposure is the load-bearing variable; FAP determinations at Jefferson — and the additional analytical layer that EPTC-expiration-driven uninsurance creates new FAP-eligible patient populations at PA-3 hospitals — drives the SD3-to-SD4 flow-through. Cross-reference D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01 MC61 candidate and D21 SD4 G21-SD4-04 below for the combined-architecture analysis.

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most analytically important feature visible at SD4 is the convergence. Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health (post-Einstein merger), and CHOP operate simultaneously across six accountability dimensions documented in the project: environmental compliance (D6), real estate (D7), procurement (D8), fiscal architecture (D9), employment (D10), and healthcare delivery (D21 SD4). Each dimension has its own regulatory architecture; the federal regulatory floor at healthcare delivery is uniquely dense (CMS Conditions of Participation; EMTALA; 501(r); ACGME; HIPAA; ACA Section 9007; Hospital Price Transparency Rule; No Surprises Act). The six-dimensional architecture is what makes anchor accountability a sustained analytical problem rather than a single-dimension question — the same four institutions appear at multiple regulatory interfaces, and the accountability question at each interface compounds across the others.

The most common misunderstanding about anchor hospitals in Philadelphia is that "community benefit" is a single quantity reported on Form 990 Schedule H. It is not. The 501(r) framework distinguishes between several categories: charity care (patient-care services for which payment is not received from the patient or insurer); unreimbursed Medicaid costs (the gap between Medicaid payment and the hospital's documented cost); subsidized health services (services delivered at a financial loss due to community need); health professions education; research; community benefit operations; and community building activities. Each category has its own measurement convention; aggregate community benefit reports combine these categories without uniform weighting; the aggregate magnitude is not directly comparable across institutions or across years. The 2025 SEPA Regional Community Health Needs Assessment is the every-three-years architecture for community engagement; the implementation strategies vary by institution; the actual community-served population varies by institutional geographic catchment, service line, and engagement-program capacity.

The human consequence visible in 2026 is the convergence of two patterns. First, the substantive community-benefit contribution is real and operates at meaningful magnitude: the four academic medical center systems deliver substantial uncompensated and below-cost care to PA-3 residents through emergency stabilization (EMTALA), financial assistance policy application of AGB-or-better billing, and CHNA-informed community programs that operate at scale. Second, the structural-impact dimension is also real and operates at meaningful magnitude: approximately 1 million Pennsylvanians carry medical debt, hospital care is the most frequently cited source, only 30% of PA hospitals comply with the Hospital Price Transparency Rule that would enable price-shopping before service, the CFPB rule that would have removed all medical debt from credit reports was vacated in July 2025 before taking effect, and PA-specific HUP $182.7M malpractice verdict is the single largest in PA history (under continuing appeal). Both patterns operate at the same institutions on the same population; the analytical posture must hold both rather than collapse to either. This is the operational meaning of the MC53 Both/And designation that crosses SD4 — substantive 501(r) FAP architecture provides real cost-sharing protection AND structural impact on PA-3 household financial security via revenue-cycle architecture is real and at meaningful magnitude. The commitment-vs-outcome HOM 7th-candidate territory holds the additional disciplinary structure: multiple documented commitment mechanisms (501(r) Schedule H reporting; CHNA implementation; FAP architectures; community engagement) and multiple documented outcome dimensions (community benefit dollars; charity care provision; medical debt collection volumes; lawsuits filed; wage garnishments) interact in ways that resist closure-by-analytical-assertion of "biggest cause" of the commitment-vs-outcome gap.

The most analytically important federal-engagement feature visible at SD4 is the intersection. The OBBBA provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown (cross-reference D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02 + D9 SD4) flows through to the same anchor hospitals that operate the 501(r) community benefit architecture at SD4; the EPTC expiration (D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01) flows through to anchor-hospital FAP populations; the medical-debt federal architecture (CFPB rule vacatur; FDCPA; the pending CFPB rulemaking trajectory under Trump CFPB) affects anchor-hospital collection practices. Federal House representation has direct legislative authority on the 501(r) framework itself (potential amendments to require more granular reporting; potential modifications to AGB methodology; potential modifications to extraordinary-collection-action requirements); on Hospital Price Transparency Rule enforcement; on No Surprises Act IDR rulemaking; and on the federal Medicare GME / ACGME training-funding architecture. PA-state-level engagement at PA Act 77 charity care architecture, PA medical-debt-collection protections (PA Fair Credit Extension; wage-garnishment-prohibition; credit-reporting-prohibition), and PA tort-reform legislation is the complementary locus.

## Section 8: Representation Question Closure

**What the legal framework provides or promises.** The federal hospital-institutional regulatory framework establishes substantial structural protections: CMS Conditions of Participation (42 C.F.R. Parts 482-485) condition Medicare/Medicaid participation on patient rights, quality assurance, infection control, medical staff governance, and discharge planning standards; EMTALA (42 U.S.C. § 1395dd) requires emergency screening and stabilization regardless of payment status; IRC § 501(r) conditions nonprofit hospital tax-exempt status on CHNA every three years, financial assistance policy, billing-and-collection requirements, and emergency medical care policy; the Hospital Price Transparency Rule (45 C.F.R. § 180.40 et seq.) requires hospitals to publish payer-specific negotiated rates and gross charges; the No Surprises Act protects consumers from balance billing at in-network facilities. The Pennsylvania regulatory framework adds state-level texture: PA Act 77 mandates hospital charity care programs; PA Fair Credit Extension Uniformity Act + 42 Pa. C.S. § 8127 wage-garnishment-prohibition + PA medical-debt-credit-reporting-prohibition provide consumer-protection floor in debt collection; PA MCARE Act establishes medical-malpractice architecture.

**What PA-3 constituents actually receive.** Penn Medicine, Temple Health, Jefferson Health, and CHOP deliver substantial uncompensated and below-cost care to PA-3 residents through EMTALA emergency stabilization, 501(r) FAP application of AGB-or-better billing, and CHNA-informed community programs operating at scale through the 2025 SEPA Regional CHNA cycle. Approximately 1 million Pennsylvanians carry medical debt [Source 68]; hospital care is the most frequently cited source [Source 67]. Only 30% of PA hospitals comply with the federal Hospital Price Transparency Rule [Source 64]. The CFPB rule that would have removed all medical debt from credit reports was vacated July 2025 before taking effect [Source 60]. The HUP $182.7M malpractice verdict is the single largest in PA history under continuing appeal [Sources 61, 62]; Temple $44.9M, Jefferson $2.5M, Hahnemann $32M Philadelphia County 2024 verdicts indicate active medical-malpractice litigation territory [Source 66]. PA 2024 tort reform shortened certain claim windows to 18 months [Source 66].

**What accounts for the gap.** Six structural features account for the gap between framework and received delivery: (a) the price-opacity layer at the 70% of PA hospitals not compliant with Hospital Price Transparency Rule structurally precludes informed cost-comparison before service; (b) the FAP application complexity creates documented under-application by income-qualifying patients [Source 60]; (c) the medical-debt-collection architecture (federal FDCPA + state PA Fair Credit Extension + wage-garnishment-prohibition) provides substantial protections in PA but documented harassment patterns persist [Source 63]; (d) the EPTC-expiration / OBBBA-Medicaid-disruption upstream flow-through to anchor-hospital FAP populations and uncompensated care exposure compounds across SD2 + SD3 + SD4; (e) the CFPB-rule vacatur (July 2025) removes a substantial federal protection that would have addressed credit-reporting impacts of unpaid medical debt; (f) the ACGME-specialty-distribution architecture has documented downstream effects on PA-3 primary-care workforce capacity. The cumulative effect produces the MC53 substantive-and-structural Both/And operationalized in this SD; the commitment-vs-outcome HOM 7th-instance candidate (substructure OQ1) at the anchor community-benefit dimension preserves multiple documented commitment mechanisms and multiple documented outcome dimensions without closure-by-analytical-assertion of single-mechanism primacy.

**What the gap means for the representation analysis.** Federal House representation operates at: (a) 501(r) framework amendments and IRS Form 990 Schedule H reporting requirements; (b) Hospital Price Transparency Rule enforcement and CMS civil money penalty trajectory; (c) No Surprises Act IDR rulemaking; (d) CFPB medical-debt protection rulemaking trajectory post-*Cornerstone Credit Union* vacatur; (e) Medicare GME architecture and ACGME-specialty-distribution-incentive design; (f) EMTALA enforcement-resourcing and HHS OIG hospital-institutional oversight; (g) upstream EPTC-extension and OBBBA-technical-corrections legislation that flows through to anchor-hospital fiscal architecture (cross-reference D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02, D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01, D9 SD4 PILOET PRINCIPAL ANCHOR). PA-state-level texture engagement at PA Act 77 charity care, medical-debt protections, MCARE Act / 2024 tort reform legislation is the complementary locus. The six-dimensional anchor accountability framework (D6 environmental + D7 real estate + D8 procurement + D9 fiscal + D10 employment + D21 SD4 healthcare delivery) is now complete at the project's analytical inventory; subsequent domains will inherit the framework. The MC53 Both/And and the commitment-vs-outcome HOM 7th-instance candidate at SD4 preserve the disciplinary structure that resists premature analytical closure.

# D21 Healthcare Delivery — Sub-Domain 5
## FQHC and Safety-Net Delivery

**Date:** 2026-05-11
**Phase:** 1 (Per-SD Execution; fresh cycle)
**Domain:** 21 — Healthcare Delivery
**Sub-Domain:** 5 of 7
**Cycle position:** Phase 1 SD2-N batch execution; SD1 + SD2 + SD3 + SD4 complete
**Methodology stack:** M0 v2.2 · M2 v1.2.1 · Skills v3
**Status:** Phase 1 draft — SD5 of SD2-N batch

## Section 1: Header and Regime Declaration

**Sub-domain scope.** SD5 documents the FQHC (Federally Qualified Health Center) and safety-net delivery architecture serving PA-3's underserved populations: the federal Health Center Program under Section 330 of the Public Health Service Act (42 U.S.C. § 254b) administered by HRSA Bureau of Primary Health Care; the 340B Drug Pricing Program (42 U.S.C. § 256b) and HRSA 340B Rebate Model Pilot Program disposition (rebate pilot originally scheduled to launch January 1, 2026; halted by federal court due to lack of administrative record; HRSA RFI published February 17, 2026 seeking stakeholder feedback); the Community Health Center Fund (CHCF) authorization architecture extended through December 31, 2026 via 2026 Consolidated Appropriations Act at $4.6 billion FY 2026 [Source 81]; the Federal Tort Claims Act (FTCA) medical malpractice coverage for FQHCs and their providers; the PCMH (Patient-Centered Medical Home) recognition framework; Ryan White HIV/AIDS Treatment Extension Act and Title X family planning frameworks at FQHC delivery; the PA-3 FQHC roster (Philadelphia FIGHT, Puentes de Salud, MANNA, Resources for Human Development / RHD, Family Practice & Counseling Network / FPCN, plus additional PA-3-serving FQHCs); the Philadelphia Department of Public Health (PDPH) clinic system (Health Center 4 at 4400 Haverford Avenue; Health Center 5 at 1900 N. 20th Street; Health Center 6 at 321 W. Girard Avenue; Health Center 9 at 131 E. Chelten Avenue; Strawberry Mansion Health Center; plus additional PDPH sites); the safety-net hospital institutional architecture (institutions serving disproportionately Medicaid + uninsured + medically-complex populations); the Hahnemann University Hospital 2019 closure precedent for safety-net fragility; and the cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanism architecture (OBBBA Medicaid cuts; Medicaid procedural-loss; 340B rebate pilot disposition; CHCF reauthorization uncertainty; provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown; Hahnemann-precedent). **Boundary**: D2 SD6 owns federal § 330 program architecture; D21 SD5 owns delivery-side comprehensive analysis (institutional set; clinical operations; community-board governance; sliding-fee scale; payer mix). Cross-reference D21 SD2 (Medicaid delivery; OBBBA flow-through), D21 SD3 (ACA marketplace; EPTC-expiration uninsurance flow-through), D21 SD4 (hospital institutional architecture; six-dimensional anchor framework).

**Regime declaration.** Internet-access regime. Current-state HRSA Section 330 architecture status, CHCF FY 2026 funding level, 340B Rebate Model Pilot disposition (post-court halt; HRSA RFI trajectory), OBBBA Medicaid delivery-side flow-through (cross-reference D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02), PA-3 FQHC roster and operational status, PDPH clinic operations, Hahnemann-precedent context, and Rural Health Transformation Fund allocation are expected to be retrieved; specific figures carry sources per Standard 12. The Section 330 PHSA framework, 340B Drug Pricing Program statutory architecture, FTCA medical malpractice coverage, Ryan White, and Title X are framework-level statutory references; specific section citations are sourced where claims are specific rather than framework-level. Mixed-regime drafting prohibited per Standard 1.

**Analytical posture.** Both/And-primary at MC55 (FQHC + safety-net architecture). Per D21 substructure §6, MC55 is the within-domain analytical-tension flagged at SD5: substantive § 330 community-board governance + sliding-fee + 340B + Medicaid managed care serves underserved populations AND structural cumulative fiscal vulnerability under multiple federal-policy-cycle mechanisms. **Hold-open-magnitude territory operative** at SD5 per D21 substructure §6 OQ2 — emergent-from-interaction HOM shape; 2nd confirmed-pending instance project-wide after D11 SD7 school-to-prison (within-shape sub-pattern variation: cumulative-impact-on-institutions for D21 SD5 vs. cumulative-impact-on-individuals for D11 SD7). Multiple separate federal-policy-cycle mechanisms (OBBBA Medicaid cuts; Medicaid procedural-loss; 340B rebate pilot disruption; CHCF reauthorization uncertainty; provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown; Hahnemann-precedent), each with own intended effect, none intending "safety-net fiscal collapse risk" cumulative outcome. Diagnostic test fits: "no law intends" cumulative fiscal vulnerability outcome; multiple separate-purpose elements producing cumulative outcome that no single architecture explicitly produces. Phase 1 SD5 confirms empirical magnitude; closure-by-analytical-assertion of "biggest cause" remains unsupported per HOM discipline (per substructure lead-substantive note: financial-architecture covering doesn't reach core issues).

**Thread declarations.** Four analytical threads operative at SD5:

- **D21-SD5-Thread A (federal Health Center Program § 330 + 340B + CHCF safety-net architecture).** HRSA Section 330 grants provide approximately 70% of federal grant funding to FQHCs through the Community Health Center Fund (CHCF) [Source 78]; CHCF authorization was extended through December 31, 2026 via the 2026 Consolidated Appropriations Act at $4.6 billion FY 2026 [Source 81]; without further reauthorization, CHCF funding expires December 31, 2026. The 340B Drug Pricing Program provides discounted prescription drug pricing to FQHCs and other covered entities under 42 U.S.C. § 256b. FTCA medical malpractice coverage applies to HRSA-deemed FQHCs and their providers. Approximately 52 million patients nationally rely on CHCs [Source 76]; FQHCs operate with median operating margins below negative 2% per recent NACHC data [Source 81].

- **D21-SD5-Thread B (340B Rebate Model Pilot Program disposition; court-halted; HRSA RFI February 2026).** HRSA's 340B Rebate Model Pilot Program was originally scheduled to launch January 1, 2026 and would have required covered entities including FQHCs to pay full price upfront for selected drugs dispensed to 340B-eligible patients with rebates processed through the Beacon platform [Sources 77, 79]. The pilot was halted by federal court in late 2025 due to lack of administrative record [Source 85]. HRSA published an RFI in the Federal Register on February 17, 2026 seeking stakeholder feedback on potential 340B rebate model implementation [Sources 80, 85]; the RFI comment period closed April 20, 2026 (extended from original March 19, 2026 deadline) [Source 76]. The bipartisan Community Health Center Drug Pricing Protection Act (H.R. 7391) introduced by Reps. Jack Bergman (R-MI) and Jake Auchincloss (D-MA) would protect 340B upfront discounts for community health centers [Source 76]. The 340B rebate pilot architecture status is the principal federal-engagement variable for FQHC pharmacy-revenue territory in 2026-2027.

- **D21-SD5-Thread C (OBBBA Medicaid delivery-side flow-through to FQHC revenue architecture; cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanisms).** OBBBA Medicaid provisions affect FQHC delivery-side architecture through: (a) Section 71115 provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown (cross-reference D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02; D9 SD4); (b) Section 71107 6-month redeterminations beginning December 2026 producing Medicaid procedural-loss disruption that flows through to FQHC patient continuity [Source 73]; (c) Section 71119 work-requirements creating coverage instability for expansion adults; (d) Section 71109 noncitizen Medicaid restrictions effective October 1, 2026 — FQHCs serve all consumers regardless of ability to pay; the noncitizen restrictions affect FQHC revenue [Source 74]. The $50B Rural Health Transformation Fund (RHTF) Section 71401 authorized $10B annually 2026-2030 for rural states [Source 74]; RHTF is rural-only and does not directly serve urban FQHCs including those in PA-3 [Source 72]. Medicaid is approximately 40% of FQHC revenue per documented patterns [Source 72]; OBBBA-driven Medicaid disruption operates against this revenue base.

- **D21-SD5-Thread D (Hahnemann-precedent and emergent-from-interaction HOM 2nd-instance shape at SD5).** Per D21 substructure §6 OQ2, emergent-from-interaction HOM shape diagnostic operative at SD5 — cumulative fiscal vulnerability emerging from interaction of OBBBA Medicaid cuts + Medicaid procedural-loss + 340B rebate disposition + CHCF reauthorization uncertainty + provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown + Hahnemann-precedent. The 2019 Hahnemann University Hospital closure precedent (cross-reference D21 SD4 medical-malpractice litigation at the former Hahnemann facility [Source 66]) is the structural-precedent for safety-net fragility at the institutional level. The HOM diagnostic preserves the multiple-mechanism architecture without closure-by-analytical-assertion of "biggest cause"; per substructure lead-substantive note, financial-architecture covering doesn't reach core issues. 2nd confirmed-pending HOM instance project-wide (after D11 SD7 school-to-prison); within-shape sub-pattern variation: cumulative-impact-on-institutions (D21 SD5) vs. cumulative-impact-on-individuals (D11 SD7).

## Section 2: Authority Chain

The FQHC and safety-net delivery authority chain in PA-3 follows the federal-state-local layered architecture with substantial federal-architectural density at HRSA / CHCF / 340B layers.

**Constitutional Foundation.** U.S. Constitution Article I, Section 8 (Spending Clause; General Welfare Clause) authorizes federal grants to FQHCs under Section 330; Tenth Amendment reserves state public health authority.

**Federal Statutory Layer.**

- **Section 330 of the Public Health Service Act (42 U.S.C. § 254b).** Authorizes HRSA Health Center Program; sets community-board governance requirements (51% of board members must be patients); sliding-fee scale for income-qualifying patients; required service-area medically-underserved-area / population designation; FTCA medical malpractice coverage for HRSA-deemed entities. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at HRSA rulemaking and at CHCF reauthorization cycles*.

- **Community Health Center Fund (CHCF; authorized through annual Consolidated Appropriations Acts).** Provides approximately 70% of federal grant funding to FQHCs [Source 78]. Currently authorized through December 31, 2026 at $4.6 billion FY 2026 via the 2026 Consolidated Appropriations Act [Source 81]; prior authorization expired September 30, 2025 and was extended to January 30, 2026 via 43-day-federal-shutdown-extension-budget-act, then extended through December 31, 2026 via 2026 CAA [Source 81]. *Statutory stability LOW (chronic short-term extension pattern; not multi-year reauthorized since 2019 per NACHC [Source 84])*.

- **340B Drug Pricing Program (42 U.S.C. § 256b).** Authorizes HRSA to administer the 340B Drug Pricing Program providing discounted prescription drug pricing to covered entities including FQHCs, disproportionate-share hospitals, and certain other safety-net entities. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability HIGH at HRSA rulemaking and at potential rebate-model implementation* [Sources 76, 79, 85].

- **Federal Tort Claims Act (FTCA; 28 U.S.C. § 2671 et seq.).** Provides medical malpractice coverage for HRSA-deemed FQHCs and their providers; eliminates need for FQHCs to purchase commercial medical malpractice coverage. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE at HRSA FTCA deeming and at DOJ Federal Tort Claims Act adjudication*.

- **One Big Beautiful Bill Act of 2025 (P.L. 119-21).** OBBBA Sections 71107, 71109, 71115, 71117, 71119, 71401 directly affect FQHC delivery-side architecture (cross-reference D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02 for substantive analysis). Section 71401 establishes $50B Rural Health Transformation Fund / $10B annually 2026-2030 (rural-only; does not directly serve urban FQHCs) [Source 74]. *Statutory stability HIGH at enactment; administrative vulnerability HIGH at CMS / HRSA implementation*.

- **Ryan White HIV/AIDS Treatment Extension Act (42 U.S.C. § 300ff et seq.).** Authorizes Ryan White program at FQHC delivery; Philadelphia FIGHT is principal Ryan White provider in PA-3. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **Title X family planning (42 U.S.C. § 300 et seq.).** Authorizes federal family-planning grants; FQHCs and other entities deliver Title X services. *Statutory stability MODERATE-HIGH; administrative vulnerability HIGH at federal rulemaking on family-planning architecture under Trump HHS posture*.

- **CMS Medicaid FQHC PPS architecture (42 C.F.R. Part 405 Subpart X).** Establishes FQHC Prospective Payment System for Medicaid reimbursement; PPS rate covers facility costs across cost categories. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE at state Medicaid agency PPS rate-setting*.

**Federal Agency Layer.**

- **Health Resources and Services Administration (HRSA) Bureau of Primary Health Care.** 5600 Fishers Lane, Rockville, MD 20857. Administers Section 330 Health Center Program; oversees community-board governance compliance; administers Uniform Data System (UDS) annual reporting; FTCA deeming authority; PCMH-recognition coordination. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH at HRSA rulemaking under Trump HHS posture* [Source 72].

- **HRSA Office of Pharmacy Affairs.** Administers 340B Drug Pricing Program including the 340B Rebate Model Pilot Program disposition [Sources 79, 80, 85]. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH*.

- **Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS).** Administers Medicaid FQHC PPS architecture; cross-reference D21 SD2 for managed care architecture. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH*.

- **Federal Tort Claims Act program administered through HHS Office of General Counsel + DOJ.** Administers FTCA coverage for HRSA-deemed entities. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

**State Statutory Layer.**

- **Pennsylvania Public Welfare Code (62 P.S. § 101 et seq.).** Authorizes PA Medical Assistance; Medicaid PPS rates for PA FQHCs determined through PA DHS architecture. *Statutory stability HIGH; administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

- **Pennsylvania Department of Health regulations (28 Pa. Code).** Sets PA-state-level public-health and clinic licensing requirements applicable to FQHCs and PDPH clinics operating in Pennsylvania. *Statutory stability HIGH*.

**State Agency Layer.**

- **Pennsylvania Department of Human Services (DHS) Office of Medical Assistance Programs (OMAP).** 625 Forster Street, Harrisburg, PA 17120. Administers Medicaid PPS rates for PA FQHCs; sets state Medicaid FQHC PPS architecture; cross-reference D21 SD2 for Medicaid delivery architecture. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE-HIGH at PA DHS rulemaking*.

- **Pennsylvania Department of Health (DOH).** Public-health-related functions; coordinates with FQHCs on disease surveillance, immunization, and public-health infrastructure. *Administrative vulnerability MODERATE*.

**Local Statutory Layer.**

- **Philadelphia Code Title 6 (Health).** Authorizes Philadelphia Department of Public Health (PDPH) operations including PDPH health center system. *Statutory stability HIGH*.

**Local Agency Layer.**

- **Philadelphia Department of Public Health (PDPH).** 1101 Market Street, Philadelphia, PA 19107. Operates Philadelphia health center system including Health Center 4 (4400 Haverford Avenue), Health Center 5 (1900 N. 20th Street), Health Center 6 (321 W. Girard Avenue), Health Center 9 (131 E. Chelten Avenue), Strawberry Mansion Health Center, and additional sites. PDPH clinics deliver safety-net primary and preventive care to PA-3 underserved populations parallel to the FQHC system. *Administrative vulnerability LOW-MODERATE*.

- **PA-3 FQHCs.** Principal PA-3-serving FQHCs include: Philadelphia FIGHT (1233 Locust Street, Philadelphia, PA 19107; Ryan White principal provider; HIV/AIDS specialty); Puentes de Salud (1700 South Street, Philadelphia, PA 19146; Latino community health); MANNA (420 N. 20th Street, Philadelphia, PA 19130; medically tailored meals + clinical services); Resources for Human Development / RHD (4700 Wissahickon Avenue, Philadelphia, PA 19144); Family Practice & Counseling Network / FPCN (1900 N. 9th Street, Philadelphia, PA 19122; FQHC look-alike). Additional PA-3-serving FQHCs and FQHC look-alikes operate through coordinated networks. *Administrative vulnerability HIGH given cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanism exposure*.

**Constituent Layer.** Aggregate constituent profiles introduced in Section 5: PA-3 uninsured constituent navigating sliding-fee at Philadelphia FIGHT; PA-3 Medicaid-enrolled household navigating FQHC PCMH at FPCN; PA-3 Ryan White-eligible HIV patient navigating Philadelphia FIGHT specialty care; PA-3 Latino community member navigating Puentes de Salud bilingual primary care.

## Section 3: Pathway Tracing

The FQHC and safety-net delivery pathway for a PA-3 constituent operates through three channels: HRSA-deemed FQHCs (Philadelphia FIGHT, Puentes de Salud, MANNA, RHD, FPCN, and others); FQHC look-alikes; and the PDPH clinic system. D2 SD6 owns federal § 330 program architecture; D21 SD5 owns delivery-side comprehensive analysis.

*Triggering condition.* A PA-3 constituent enters FQHC / safety-net architecture under documented pathways: (a) uninsured status requiring affordable primary or specialty care under sliding-fee scale; (b) Medicaid enrollment with FQHC as PCMH primary care; (c) Ryan White-eligible HIV/AIDS care need (Philadelphia FIGHT principal PA-3 provider); (d) Title X family-planning need; (e) referral from ED, hospital, or other safety-net entity for post-acute continuity.

*FQHC sliding-fee-scale pathway.* HRSA-deemed FQHCs operate sliding-fee scale per Section 330 requirements; intake includes household income, household size, and insurance status; sliding-fee determination places patient at nominal-fee level (typically $20-$40 for primary care) or sliding-scale percentage. FQHCs cannot deny services for inability to pay.

*FQHC PPS pathway for Medicaid-enrolled patients.* Medicaid-enrolled patients trigger PPS reimbursement under 42 C.F.R. Part 405 Subpart X; PA Medicaid pays FQHC a per-visit PPS rate. Medicaid is approximately 40% of FQHC revenue per documented patterns [Source 72]; OBBBA-driven Medicaid disruption (cross-reference D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02) flows through to FQHC revenue.

*340B pharmacy delivery pathway.* PA-3 FQHCs operate 340B-covered-entity pharmacy architectures providing discounted prescription drug pricing. The 340B Rebate Model Pilot Program was originally scheduled to launch January 1, 2026 [Sources 77, 79]; halted by federal court in late 2025 due to lack of administrative record [Source 85]. HRSA published RFI February 17, 2026 (5,576 comments received per Federal Register [Source 80]); comment period extended to April 20, 2026 [Source 76]. Bipartisan H.R. 7391 (Bergman R-MI / Auchincloss D-MA) would protect 340B upfront discounts for CHCs [Source 76]. 340B rebate-pilot disposition is principal federal-engagement variable for FQHC pharmacy-revenue territory 2026-2027.

*Ryan White HIV/AIDS specialty pathway.* HIV/AIDS-diagnosed PA-3 constituent receives Ryan White-funded specialty care at Philadelphia FIGHT (1233 Locust Street). Ryan White covers HIV/AIDS treatment, care coordination, social services, and ADAP medication coverage. Cross-reference D2 SD7 and D21 SD7.

*PDPH health center pathway.* PA-3 constituent at PDPH health center (e.g., Health Center 6 at 321 W. Girard Avenue); intake similar to FQHC sliding-fee architecture; PDPH-clinic-specific operational architecture differs from HRSA-deemed FQHC (no community-board governance required; FTCA does not apply; city-operated).

*PCMH integration pathway.* Many PA-3 FQHCs operate as recognized PCMHs under NCQA or other frameworks; PCMH-recognition disposition F-flagged (F21-SD5-01).

*FTCA medical malpractice coverage pathway.* HRSA-deemed FQHCs receive FTCA medical malpractice coverage under 28 U.S.C. § 2671 et seq.; eliminates commercial coverage need; substantive claims proceed through FTCA adjudication.

*Cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanism flow-through.* PA-3 FQHC and safety-net architecture is exposed to six cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanisms operating concurrently in 2026-2027: (a) OBBBA Medicaid cuts (D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02; D9 SD4); (b) OBBBA Section 71107 Medicaid procedural-loss (D12 SD2/SD8); (c) 340B Rebate Model Pilot disposition; (d) CHCF reauthorization uncertainty (extended only through Dec 31, 2026); (e) provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown (D9 SD4); (f) Hahnemann-precedent + 2024 PA tort reform institutional fragility context. Each mechanism has own intended effect; none intends "safety-net fiscal collapse risk" cumulative outcome. MC55 PRIMARY HOM emergent-from-interaction shape diagnostic operationalizes here.

*Decision points where the pathway breaks for PA-3 constituents.* The FQHC / safety-net pathway breaks at: (a) upstream Medicaid procedural-loss (6-month redetermination beginning Dec 2026 transitions patients to sliding-fee or uninsured); (b) EPTC-expiration uninsurance flow-through (D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01); (c) 340B pharmacy revenue layer (rebate-model disposition); (d) CHCF reauthorization layer (chronic short-term extension architecture); (e) OBBBA Section 71109 noncitizen Medicaid restrictions effective Oct 1, 2026 [Source 74]; (f) provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown delivery-side flow-through; (g) FQHC capacity layer (median operating margins below negative 2% with less than 90 days cash per NACHC [Source 81] = acute fiscal vulnerability).

## Section 4: Statistical Profile and Sub-Area Variation

**PA-3 FQHC roster.** Principal PA-3-serving FQHCs: Philadelphia FIGHT (1233 Locust Street); Puentes de Salud (1700 South Street); MANNA (420 N. 20th Street); RHD (4700 Wissahickon Avenue); FPCN (1900 N. 9th Street). Additional PA-3-serving FQHCs and look-alikes operate in Philadelphia (specific roster magnitude F-flagged, F21-SD5-02).

**PDPH health center system.** PDPH operates Health Centers 4, 5, 6, 9, Strawberry Mansion plus additional sites; PDPH-clinic patient-visit volume F-flagged (F21-SD5-04).

**FQHC patient volume.** PA-3 FQHC aggregate patient-volume F-flagged (F21-SD5-05); national FQHC patient volume approximately 52 million [Source 76]. Structural inference: PA-3 FQHC patient volume in the hundreds of thousands annually.

**FQHC revenue architecture.** Medicaid ~40% of FQHC revenue [Source 72]; balance from Section 330 grants (CHCF + discretionary), Medicare, private insurance, 340B savings, Ryan White, Title X, local grants. Median CHC operating margins below negative 2% with less than 90 days cash on hand per NACHC 2026 data [Source 81]. PA-3-specific FQHC revenue-mix F-flagged (F21-SD5-06).

**CHCF funding architecture.** CHCF FY 2026: $4.6 billion (largest increase to CHCF in a decade per NACHC) [Source 81]. Authorization expires December 31, 2026 absent further reauthorization. NHSC FY 2026: $350 million base. THCGME FY 2026: $225 million [Source 81].

**340B Rebate Model Pilot Program scope.** Eight manufacturers selected; rebate plans covered Eliquis, Enbrel, Farxiga, Imbruvica, Januvia, Jardiance, Stelara, Xarelto, NovoLog/Fiasp products [Source 79]; Entresto scheduled April 1, 2026 [Source 79]. Halted by federal court late 2025 [Source 85]. HRSA RFI Feb 17, 2026 with 5,576 comments [Source 80].

**Rural Health Transformation Fund (Section 71401).** $50B total / $10B annually 2026-2030; rural-only [Sources 22, 74]; does not directly serve urban PA-3 FQHCs [Source 72]. PA state allocation F-flagged (F21-SD5-07).

**Hahnemann-precedent context.** Hahnemann University Hospital closed 2019; locus of 2024 $32M Philadelphia County malpractice settlement [Source 66]; structural-precedent for safety-net institutional fragility.

**PA-3 sub-area variation.** PDPH Health Center 5 (1900 N. 20th Street) and Health Center 6 (321 W. Girard Avenue) serve North/Northwest Core; Health Center 4 (4400 Haverford Avenue) serves West Philadelphia Core; Health Center 9 (131 E. Chelten Avenue) serves Northwest; Strawberry Mansion serves North Philadelphia; PA-3 FQHCs distributed across all four sub-areas with concentration in lower-income tracts. Sub-area FQHC-utilization F-flagged (F21-SD5-08).

## Section 5: Aggregate Constituent Profiles

Profiles per M0 Standard 13 are aggregate, not named individuals.

**Profile 1: Uninsured PA-3 constituent navigating Philadelphia FIGHT sliding-fee + Ryan White HIV care in Center City (MC55 PRIMARY substantive contribution).**

*Constituent type:* HIV-positive working-age PA-3 resident; uninsured (recent loss of Pennie coverage post-EPTC-expiration per D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01); income approximately $24,000; Center City interface with West Philadelphia. *Specific PA-3 sub-area:* West Philadelphia Core interface with Center City. *Triggering event:* HIV diagnosis requiring sustained specialty care plus primary care continuity.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Constituent presents at Philadelphia FIGHT (1233 Locust Street); sliding-fee places constituent at approximately 188% FPL for single-person household (illustrative; F21-SD5-09); receives Ryan White-funded HIV specialty care plus FQHC primary care; ADAP coverage for HIV medications; FTCA-deemed provider coverage; 340B pharmacy for discounted medications. Cross-reference D21 SD3 G21-SD3-01 (EPTC uninsurance) and D2 SD7 (cancer/serious-illness).

*Illustrative figures with data provenance.* Philadelphia FIGHT location: 1233 Locust Street, Philadelphia, PA 19107 [D]. Ryan White architecture [Title XXVI PHSA; D]. ADAP coverage [D]. FTCA-deemed coverage [Source 71; D]. 340B [42 U.S.C. § 256b; D]. FPL threshold 188% for single-person: F21-SD5-09.

*Outcome.* Constituent receives sustained HIV specialty + primary care plus ADAP medication coverage. MC55 substantive-contribution operates fully — Philadelphia FIGHT provides comprehensive HIV-specialty + primary-care continuity unavailable through any other delivery channel for this constituent. MC55 PRIMARY HOM emergent-from-interaction shape preserves cumulative fiscal-vulnerability magnitude at the FQHC institutional level without closure-by-analytical-assertion.

**Profile 2: Medicaid-enrolled PA-3 family navigating FQHC PCMH at FPCN in North Philadelphia.**

*Constituent type:* PA-3 family of four (two working-age parents, two school-age children); HealthChoices Medicaid (Group VIII expansion); selected FPCN as PCMH; North/Northwest Core sub-area.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Family selects FPCN as PCMH primary care; FPCN delivers comprehensive primary care including pediatric and adult services; PPS reimbursement under PA Medicaid FQHC PPS; 340B pharmacy savings support FPCN operations. OBBBA 6-month redetermination architecture beginning December 2026 (D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02) creates procedural-loss exposure; cross-reference D12 SD2/SD8.

*Illustrative figures.* FPCN location: 1900 N. 9th Street, Philadelphia, PA 19122 [D]. FQHC PPS architecture [42 C.F.R. Part 405 Subpart X; D]. Medicaid ~40% of FQHC revenue [Source 72; D]. PA Medicaid procedural-loss disruption beginning Dec 2026 [Sources 22, 25; D].

*Outcome.* Family receives comprehensive PCMH primary care at FPCN. MC55 PRIMARY HOM emergent-from-interaction shape operates at cumulative-vulnerability dimension: substantive FQHC delivery serves the family AND structural fiscal vulnerability at FPCN institutional level emerges from cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanisms operating concurrently. Family's care continuity is dependent on FPCN's institutional fiscal sustainability across the cumulative-mechanism architecture.

**Profile 3: Latino PA-3 community member navigating Puentes de Salud bilingual primary care in South Philadelphia (OBBBA Section 71109 noncitizen-restriction exposure).**

*Constituent type:* Working-age PA-3 resident; lawfully-present immigrant status; limited English proficiency; income approximately $32,000; single-person household; South/Southwest sub-area.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Constituent presents at Puentes de Salud (1700 South Street); receives bilingual Spanish-English primary care under sliding-fee; if previously Medicaid-enrolled, faces OBBBA Section 71109 noncitizen Medicaid restrictions effective Oct 1, 2026 [Source 74]; if previously Pennie-enrolled, faces OBBBA-modified lawfully-present-immigrant Pennie eligibility (cross-reference D21 SD3 G21-SD3-02 + Source 45). Puentes de Salud as FQHC look-alike delivers services regardless of ability to pay.

*Illustrative figures.* Puentes de Salud location: 1700 South Street, Philadelphia, PA 19146 [D]. OBBBA Section 71109 noncitizen Medicaid restrictions Oct 1, 2026 [Source 74; D]. OBBBA-modified lawfully-present-immigrant Pennie eligibility [Source 45; D]. FQHC sliding-fee scale [42 U.S.C. § 254b; D].

*Outcome.* Constituent receives bilingual primary care at Puentes de Salud regardless of Medicaid / Pennie eligibility disposition. OBBBA noncitizen-restriction architecture creates additional fiscal-vulnerability exposure at the FQHC institutional level.

**Profile 4: PA-3 PDPH health center patient navigating safety-net continuity at Health Center 6 (MC55 PRIMARY HOM structural impact).**

*Constituent type:* PA-3 senior (age 68; dual-eligible Medicare + Medicaid CHC); chronic conditions; income approximately $14,000 (SSI); single-person household; North/Northwest Core sub-area.

*Pathway through the institutional system:* Constituent receives primary care at PDPH Health Center 6 (321 W. Girard Avenue); Medicare administers primary care payment with CHC Medicaid as secondary; PDPH operates clinic without FTCA coverage and without FQHC PPS Medicaid architecture (PDPH is city-operated rather than HRSA-deemed); cross-reference D21 SD1 G21-SD1-04 dual-eligible architecture.

*Illustrative figures.* PDPH Health Center 6: 321 W. Girard Avenue, Philadelphia, PA 19123 [D]. PDPH-operated clinic architecture (not FTCA; not HRSA-deemed FQHC; city operations) [D]. Dual-eligible Medicare + CHC Medicaid (D21 SD1 G21-SD1-04).

*Outcome.* Constituent receives sustained primary care at Health Center 6. MC55 PRIMARY HOM emergent-from-interaction shape operates at the structural-impact dimension at the PDPH institutional layer: PDPH safety-net operations exposed to cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanisms; Hahnemann-precedent territory at safety-net institutional level applies to PDPH safety-net infrastructure. PDPH-side fiscal sustainability across cumulative-mechanism architecture is the load-bearing variable.

## Section 6: Conversational Narrative Note

The most analytically important feature visible at SD5 is the cumulative architecture. Six federal-policy-cycle mechanisms operate concurrently in 2026-2027 against PA-3's FQHC and safety-net delivery infrastructure: OBBBA Medicaid cuts; OBBBA Section 71107 Medicaid procedural-loss; 340B Rebate Model Pilot Program disposition; CHCF reauthorization uncertainty; provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown; and the Hahnemann-precedent context for institutional fragility. Each mechanism has its own legislative or rulemaking pathway; each has its own intended effect; none intends "safety-net fiscal collapse risk" as its cumulative outcome. The MC55 PRIMARY HOM emergent-from-interaction shape diagnostic preserves this multiple-mechanism architecture without closure-by-analytical-assertion of single-mechanism primacy. The substructure lead-substantive observation that "financial-architecture covering doesn't reach core issues" applies directly: safety-net institutional fragility risk does not emerge from any single mechanism but from cumulative operation of mechanisms each independently legitimate at the federal-policy-cycle level.

The most common misunderstanding about FQHCs and safety-net delivery in Philadelphia is that "FQHC" is a single category of institution operating under a unified federal framework. It is not. HRSA-deemed FQHCs (Philadelphia FIGHT, Puentes de Salud, FPCN, RHD, MANNA, and others) operate under Section 330 with community-board governance (51% patient board members), sliding-fee scale, Medicaid PPS rates, FTCA medical malpractice coverage, and 340B pharmacy access. FQHC look-alikes operate under the substantive Section 330 framework without federal grant funding. PDPH health centers operate as city-operated entities outside Section 330 — without community-board-governance, without FTCA coverage, without FQHC PPS architecture — but deliver safety-net primary care to the same underserved PA-3 populations. The cumulative federal-policy-cycle mechanism architecture affects each category differently but with structurally similar cumulative fiscal-vulnerability exposure.

The human consequence visible in 2026 is documented at the institutional fragility layer. Median FQHC operating margins below negative 2% with less than 90 days cash on hand per NACHC indicates acute fiscal vulnerability to any single mechanism, with cumulative vulnerability across mechanisms operating at meaningful institutional magnitude. Approximately 52 million patients nationally rely on community health centers; PA-3 share is structurally substantial. The 2019 Hahnemann University Hospital closure precedent operates as structural-precedent for safety-net fragility at the institutional level — Hahnemann closed despite serving disproportionately Medicaid + uninsured + medically-complex populations; the closure precedent is the operational reminder that safety-net institutions can close under sufficient financial pressure regardless of substantive community contribution. MC55 PRIMARY HOM operationalizes here: substantive § 330 + sliding-fee + 340B + Medicaid managed care architecture serves PA-3 underserved populations AND structural cumulative fiscal vulnerability under multiple federal-policy-cycle mechanisms operates at meaningful magnitude through 2026-2027.

The most analytically important federal-engagement feature visible at SD5 is the convergence of six legislative-and-rulemaking trajectories. CHCF reauthorization (legislative; expires December 31, 2026); 340B Rebate Model Pilot disposition (HRSA rulemaking + H.R. 7391 legislation); OBBBA technical corrections affecting Sections 71107 / 71109 / 71115 / 71117 / 71119 / 71401 (legislative; CMS / HRSA rulemaking); RHTF allocation and potential urban-FQHC expansion (administrative); provider-tax stepdown trajectory (legislative for FY 2028+ amendments); and the PA-state-level texture engagement at PA Act 77, PA hospital licensing, and PA tort-reform implementation. Federal House representation engagement is directly consequential at each trajectory; the cumulative nature of the architecture means single-mechanism federal engagement does not address the cumulative outcome, even if any single mechanism's amelioration would reduce overall vulnerability.

---

## Section 7: Gap Analysis

**G21-SD5-01: PA-3 FQHC and safety-net cumulative fiscal vulnerability under multiple federal-policy-cycle mechanisms (MC55 PRIMARY BOTH/AND; 2nd confirmed-pending emergent-from-interaction HOM instance per substructure OQ2; within-shape sub-pattern variation cumulative-impact-on-institutions).**
*Finding — substantive contribution:* PA-3 FQHC architecture (Philadelphia FIGHT, Puentes de Salud, MANNA, RHD, FPCN, plus additional FQHCs) plus PDPH clinic system (Health Centers 4/5/6/9/Strawberry Mansion) plus safety-net hospital architecture (cross-reference D21 SD4) delivers comprehensive primary care, specialty care, pharmacy access, and care coordination to PA-3 underserved populations. Section 330 community-board governance (51% patient board members) + sliding-fee + FTCA + 340B + Medicaid PPS architecture provides structural infrastructure.
*Finding — structural cumulative vulnerability:* Six federal-policy-cycle mechanisms operate concurrently against PA-3 safety-net infrastructure: (a) OBBBA Medicaid cuts (D21 SD2 G21-SD2-02; D9 SD4); (b) OBBBA Section 71107 6-month redetermination procedural-loss beginning Dec 2026 [Source 25]; (c) 340B Rebate Model Pilot disposition (court-halted; HRSA RFI Feb 17, 2026 with 5,576 comments; H.R. 7391 legislative protection bipartisan) [Sources 76, 80, 85]; (d) CHCF reauthorization uncertainty (expires Dec 31, 2026; FY 2026 $4.6B largest increase in a decade per NACHC) [Source 81]; (e) provider-tax safe-harbor stepdown (D9 SD4); (f) Hahnemann-precedent + 2024 PA tort reform institutional fragility context [Source 66]. Each mechanism has own intended effect; none intends cumulative safety-net fiscal-collapse-risk outcome. Median FQHC operating margins below negative 2% with less than 90 days cash on hand per NACHC [Source 81].
*HOM diagnostic application:* Per D21 substructure §6 OQ2, emergent-from-interaction HOM shape; 2nd confirmed-pending instance project-wide after D11 SD7 school-to-prison. Within-shape sub-pattern variation: D11 SD7 cumulative-impact-on-individuals (kids end up in prison) vs. D21 SD5 cumulative-impact-on-institutions (safety-net hospitals/FQHCs fiscally vulnerable). Multiple separate-purpose elements producing cumulative outcome that no single architecture explicitly produces. *Hold-open-magnitude designation operative; no single-mechanism-primacy assertion advanced.*
*Evidence type:* [D] for each federal-policy-cycle mechanism architecture; [D] for documented FQHC operating margin data; [SI] for PA-3-specific cumulative vulnerability operationalization.
*Confidence:* HIGH for structural feature; HOM PRESERVATION at specific cumulative magnitude.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation engagement at each of the six mechanism trajectories simultaneously is required to address cumulative-vulnerability outcome; single-mechanism engagement (e.g., supporting H.R. 7391 alone) reduces individual mechanism magnitude but does not address cumulative architecture. PA-state-level texture engagement at PA Medicaid PPS, PA Act 77 charity care interface, and PA tort-reform context is the complementary locus. *T21-SD5-01: CHCF reauthorization legislative trajectory through Dec 31, 2026 cliff; multi-year reauthorization (NACHC requested $5.8B / 3 years) versus continued short-term extension pattern.*

*Verification update (2026-05-15): Phase 3 confirms Phase 1 framing on mechanism (d) CHCF reauthorization architecture. 2026 Consolidated Appropriations Act extended CHCF authorization through December 31, 2026 at $4.6 billion FY 2026 (largest annual increase in a decade per NACHC); multi-year reauthorization not enacted since 2019; NACHC-requested $5.8B / 3-year multi-year reauthorization not granted; chronic short-term extension pattern confirmed. The 43-day federal government shutdown (longest in US history; November 2025) extended prior authorization from September 30, 2025 to January 30, 2026, creating additional pressure on the FY 2026 funding cycle. Companion provisions in 2026 CAA: NHSC $350M FY 2026; THCGME $225M scaling to $300M by FY 2029 over five years; Medicare telehealth flexibilities extended through December 31, 2027. MC55 PRIMARY HOM emergent-from-interaction shape preserved at-magnitude per HOM discipline; no closure-by-analytical-assertion of single-mechanism primacy advanced. T21-SD5-01 carries to embedded Temporal-Changes Sidecar as pre-window-date-locked vector point per Standard 18.A with next observation point at December 31, 2026 cliff and multi-year reauthorization legislative trajectory status (no introduced multi-year reauthorization bill identified in available verification searches as of 2026-05-15).*

**G21-SD5-02: 340B Rebate Model Pilot Program disposition trajectory delivery-side impact.**
*Finding:* 340B Rebate Model Pilot originally scheduled to launch Jan 1, 2026 [Sources 77, 79]; halted by federal court late 2025 due to lack of administrative record [Source 85]. HRSA RFI Feb 17, 2026 (5,576 comments) [Source 80]; comment period closed April 20, 2026 [Source 76]. H.R. 7391 (Bergman R-MI / Auchincloss D-MA) Community Health Center Drug Pricing Protection Act would protect 340B upfront discounts for CHCs [Source 76]. Eight manufacturers originally selected; ten high-cost drugs in scope [Source 79]; Entresto April 1, 2026 expansion [Source 79]. PA-3 FQHC 340B-pharmacy-revenue magnitude F-flagged (F21-SD5-10).
*Evidence type:* [D] for pilot program architecture, court halt, HRSA RFI, H.R. 7391; [F] for PA-3 magnitudes.
*Confidence:* HIGH for federal architecture; MEDIUM for PA-3 magnitudes.
*Representation implication:* Federal House representation has direct legislative authority via H.R. 7391 cosponsorship/voting; HRSA oversight at 340B rulemaking trajectory; CMS interaction with Medicaid Drug Price Negotiation Program (cross-reference D21 SD1 IRA architecture). *T21-SD5-02: HRSA 340B Rebate Model Pilot post-RFI trajectory 2026-2027; H.R. 7391 Senate progression.*

Me.), February 10, 2026 court vacatur and remand to HHS confirmed (vacating 90 Fed. Reg. 36,163 August 1, 2025 application notice; 90 Fed. Reg. 38,165 August 7, 2025 corrected application notice; and manufacturer-application approvals announced October 30 to November 14, 2025). HRSA Request for Information published in Federal Register February 17, 2026 (91 Fed. Reg. 7,287; HHS Docket No. HRSA-2026-03042); 30-day comment period extended to April 20, 2026 confirmed; Information Collection Request comment period April 27, 2026. HRSA Federal Register notice contemplates potential expansion of rebate model to 13 manufacturers (those subject to 2026 and 2027 Medicare negotiated discounts). H.R. 7391 Community Health Center Drug Pricing Protection Act (Bergman R-MI / Auchincloss D-MA) introduced February 5, 2026; 35 cosponsors confirmed by late February 2026; PA cosponsors include Rep. Rob Bresnahan (PA-8), Rep. Madeline Dean (PA-4), and Rep. Lloyd Smucker (PA-11) per PACHC; no Senate companion bill identified as of verification date. Senate "Bipartisan Gang of Six" working group on broader 340B reform (SUSTAIN 340B discussion draft) circulating but not introduced as legislation. H.R. 8574 340B ACCESS Act broader reform pending with concerns from health-center advocacy groups. HHS reconsidering whether to implement rebate model "consistent with its statutory authority"; no NPRM issued as of verification date. T21-SD5-02 carries to embedded Temporal-Changes Sidecar as pre-window-date-locked vector point per Standard 18.A with next observation points at HRSA post-RFI rulemaking decision and H.R. 7391 floor action.*
